Calò’s agent slams Mancini after Italy snub: ‘I hope meritocracy isn’t dead’

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Giacomo Calò’s agent has criticised Roberto Mancini for leaving the Frosinone midfielder out of the Italy squad for the Nations League games against France and Belgium. He warned that the decision risks being “the death of meritocracy”.

According to Vincenzo Pisacane, via TuttoMercatoWeb, who represents the 29-year-old, Calò’s numbers make him the best midfielder in Italy right now, and he can’t understand why the Frosinone playmaker was overlooked.

Mancini named a 31-man squad on Thursday for the games against France at San Siro on 12 November and Belgium in Brussels three days later. He picked ten midfielders, including first call-ups for teenagers Riccardo Cassano of Como and Simone Lontani of Parma, but Calò was not among them.

Calò has been one of the surprises of the season. He spent most of his career in Serie B and Serie C, and in his first top-flight campaign the regista has five league goals, including goals in four games in a row. No player in the division has created more chances. Frosinone, promoted last season thanks largely to his 10 goals and 15 assists, sit sixth in the table, ahead of Juventus and Napoli.

“It was strange not to see him in Roberto Mancini’s squad. I know Mancini, and I know he takes these things into account,” Pisacane told TuttoMercatoWeb. “If he were 34 or 35, I could have understood it, but he’s 29. On numbers, he’s currently the best midfielder in Italy.

“The players Mancini called up are all very strong, but I don’t think any of them have Calò’s numbers at the moment. That’s why it would have been right to call him up. I hope yesterday’s omission isn’t the death of meritocracy and that Giacomo can be called up later if he keeps this up. Otherwise, it means there’s no logic to the national team call-ups.”

Calò’s form could have taken him away from Frosinone in the summer, but Pisacane revealed that both the player and the club rejected an approach from Sampdoria.

“There was very strong interest from Sampdoria at one point, but Calò always wanted to stay, and the club itself stood in the way and tied him down,” he said.

“I can say that a lot of Serie A clubs I had called before have got back in touch to tell me, ‘What a mistake I made not listening to you.’ That’s already important. There will be time to talk about the market and the future, but right now Giacomo is 100% focused on Frosinone.”

Pisacane also credited coach Massimiliano Alvini, who previously worked with Calò at Cosenza and pushed for his signing.

“His relationship with Alvini is wonderful. Both he and director Castagnini really wanted him last year and did everything to sign him, and he’s fully repaying that trust,” he said. “For me, Alvini has achieved far too little compared to what he could have.”

“Giacomo is calm, though, and even more focused and motivated for the game against Cagliari.”

Charlton at risk of being chewed up with WSL step up ‘an absolute ocean’

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pointless after eight games, Karen Hills’ team are struggling to compete against clubs with chunkier wallets

Played eight, lost eight. Welcome to the Women’s Super League, Charlton Athletic, a club in danger of being chewed up, toyed with and spat straight back out by the top tier.

Bruised by their 6-0 defeat against Chelsea on Sunday, last season’s playoff winners are finding WSL life tough, conceding more than three goals per game on average and struggling to compete against clubs with chunkier wallets. But are they underperforming or are they merely the victims of their own success?

Promoted against the odds last season, defying some of the bigger-spending sides in the second tier such as Newcastle and Bristol City, Karen Hills’ team overachieved by eventually beating Leicester on penalties to secure a top-flight spot. And that was the last time they had anything to cheer about. Sunday’s trip to second-bottom Crystal Palace, another of the promoted sides, theoretically offers Charlton one of their better opportunities to match the 2026 UK Eurovision entry Look Mum No Computer and register one point.

Charlton have been unfortunate. The club-record signing, Linda Líf Boama, suffered a serious knee injury in September, while another of their most important additions, the France defender Hawa Cissoko, signed for her top-tier experience, is with an ankle injury. It would, therefore, be wrong to say there was no serious attempt to strengthen the squad before this season, but Charlton’s fellow promoted teams’ summer business was undeniably more ambitious, whether it be Birmingham’s capture of two Manchester United players or Palace signing the former Tottenham captain Bethany England and the WSL2 Golden Boot winner Lexi Lloyd-Smith.

Charlton lost significant cash in WSL2. The club’s financial accounts for the year ending 30 June 2025 – which were filed in October – stated that the women’s team made a loss of £1.32m, that they had net liabilities of £2.18m and that additional funding would be required. Precise details on wage bills, revenue and expenditure were not published after the company was deemed to be entitled to exemption from audit under section 479A of the Companies Act 2006.

Promotion to the WSL also comes with additional expenditure needed to meet enhanced minimum requirements and, not insignificantly, higher minimum salaries. In WSL2 last season, clubs had to pay players aged 23 and over a minimum salary of £26,900; aged 21 and 22 a minimum of £22,200; and those aged 18 to 20 at least £17,500. In the top tier, those minimum salaries rise to £42,500, £34,700 and £26,900 respectively.

Meanwhile, central income is modest; WSL Football’s financial accounts for 2024-25 stated that £9.41m was paid to their 24 member clubs, approximately £400,000 each. Unlike promotion to the men’s top tier, which can transform a club’s financial fortunes, the main benefit of reaching the WSL for a women’s team is the potential for greater exposure on Sky Sports and the BBC’s broadcast coverage. Charlton, it should be noted, also do not have the comfort blanket of a wealthy men’s team owner to invest in their women’s side.

There is a feeling within the women’s game that summer recruitment was somewhat rushed. Their first signing was not announced until 18 July. In contrast, Birmingham’s head coach, Amy Merricks, spoke repeatedly of trying to become “WSL ready” as early as the 2024-25 campaign.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Birmingham and Palace have both drawn at Arsenal this season and caused other sides problems, whereas Charlton – only the fifth team to lose their opening eight WSL games – have struggled to keep the ball, completing only six sequences of 10 or more passes, averaging only a 65% pass accuracy. All three teams, however, are finding the step up to the top tier a challenge and it is not surprising that the WSL’s bottom three are the promoted trio. Merricks, whose team travel to London City Lionesses on Sunday, said on Friday that “the way the table looks now is probably a way off how it will finish” but the gap between the divisions was “an absolute ocean”.

Asked how wide she feels the gulf is, Hills, who says her Charlton players have “prepared really well this week”, replied: “We’re trying to make the jump and trying to stay with this pack. The gap is continuing to get bigger and you see with some of the top teams the players that they’ve brought in and the finances and the resources that they have. It’s going to be a big challenge and we’re finding that out on a weekly basis.”

EVgo opens its first Tesla Superchargers, with hundreds more planned

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EVgo has opened its first Supercharger station, and it’s in a Chicago grocery store parking lot. The site has 12 fast-charging stalls with both NACS and CCS plugs, and it’s the first of hundreds of Tesla Supercharger stalls EVgo plans to add across the US.

The 325 kW “EVgo Superchargers” are at Riverside Square & River’s Edge, at 3145 S Ashland Avenue. EVgo’s August announcement cited up to 500 kW for the Supercharger rollout.

The Chicago location features Magic Dock technology, which means both NACS and CCS vehicles can charge without an adapter. Drivers of compatible non-Tesla EVs can start a session through the Tesla app. If you drive a Tesla, the charging station shows up in the car’s navigation and Trip Planner. Other EVgo fast chargers with NACS plugs have been showing up in Tesla navigation since early September if you turn on third-party charging.

“By serving both Tesla and non-Tesla NACS drivers, we’re doubling EVgo’s addressable market and continuing to meet EV drivers where they are and where they want to charge,” said Badar Khan, EVgo CEO.

EVgo owns the Superchargers and self-brands them, and Tesla runs and maintains them. The first sites will be at Regency Centers shopping plazas with grocery stores.

EVgo is also adding NACS plugs to its regular fast-charging network, a rollout it started in 2025. The company aims to have 500 of those stalls across the US by the end of 2026, with Supercharger stalls counted separately. Over the next two years, it plans to put at least one NACS plug at every EVgo site built in 2023 or later.

Please Do Not Buy Costco’s $520 Canon R100 Kit

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please Do Not Buy Costco’s $520 Canon R100 Kit

The holiday season is ramping up and that once again means Canon is trotting out the same cash cow it has milked for the last three straight years. Costco (soon to be followed by other stores, surely) is offering a Canon EOS R100 kit for an enticing $520. Do not buy it.

Consider this a public service announcement, and a reiteration of the same plea I published two years ago (except, somehow, the R100 is more expensive now!). The only reason to buy the Canon EOS R100 is that it is cheap. Like, cheap cheap. It is easily the most affordable interchangeable lens mirrorless camera that you can buy brand-new right now, but that is quite literally the only thing it has going for it.

As an investment in someone’s future in photography, it is a terrible purchase.

“Using the R100 felt like going a few steps backward in camera technology. Yes, it’s simple in its intention and design, but maybe a little too simple,” Chris Niccolls wrote in his review. “Even significantly older cameras than the R100 outperform it. I don’t think I would recommend this camera to many people unless the budget had to remain absolutely fixed at sub-$500. Even then, I would probably just stick to my smartphone.”

We've been particularly harsh on what we call "toy cameras" lately, and those are basically any compact camera that repurposes what are glorified dashcam sensors and crams them into plastic camera-shaped bodies. Those are e-waste waiting to happen. The R100 is not e-waste in the sense that it is a camera that does take decent photos and features a removable battery. It is better than a piece of garbage, but the benchmark for a $500+ purchase should be significantly higher than "not garbage." There are better options out there that are within the same price tier.

While the R100 wins as the cheapest, there are three cameras that are better that can be purchased brand-new today for around the same price.

82 PixelMeter Nikon Z30 Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Nikon Z30 is a compact, user-friendly mirrorless camera designed primarily for vloggers and content creators who prioritize video production. While it excels at capturing sharp 4K video with reliable autofocus and a convenient vari-angle screen, it lacks an electronic viewfinder and in-body stabilization, making it less suitable for dedicated stills photography. Nikon Z30Buy new on B&HNikon Z30Buy used on KEH.com

First, let's consider the Nikon Z30. While it isn't perfect, it is a substantially better camera. While it does not have an electronic viewfinder, unlike the R100, it does feature a fully articulating rear touchscreen, is smaller and lighter than the R100, takes great video, and has excellent autofocus performance. It has a faster burst photo mode than the R100, has a good degree of ruggedness to it, and is overall a better feeling and operating camera. The Z30 is also substantially discounted versus its launch price: it costs under $600 now.

82 PixelMeter Canon EOS R50 Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Canon EOS R50 is a compact, entry-level mirrorless camera that offers impressive subject-tracking autofocus and sharp 4K video capabilities for beginners. While it serves as an excellent, user-friendly tool for content creators, its small form factor results in limited physical controls and a lack of in-body image stabilization. Canon EOS R50Buy new on B&HCanon EOS R50Buy used on KEH.com

Admittedly, the Z30 isn't the best photo-specific camera, so if that is more important to you when trying to find something better than the R100, then Canon's own R50 is just overall a better camera in every way. Everything that's wrong with the R100 is better on the R50: you get back an EVF, you get a fully articulating rear LCD, improved video features, and -- really importantly -- the R50 supports USB-C charging directly through the camera. One of the biggest drawbacks of the R100 is its lack of support for this, which makes traveling with the camera a hassle since you always have to carry the wall charger with you. The Canon R50 is available today for $679.

84 PixelMeter Canon EOS R50 V Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Canon EOS R50 V is a compact, video-centric mirrorless camera that provides creators with high-quality 4K output, 10-bit color, and convenient vertical shooting tools. Although it lacks in-body image stabilization and an electronic viewfinder, its reliable autofocus and accessible controls make it a strong entry point for those upgrading from smartphones. Canon EOS R50 VBuy new on B&HCanon EOS R50 VBuy used on KEH.com

The R50V, which is a video-focused version of the R50, is also a great choice if you want to stick with Canon but won't miss a viewfinder and want to get even closer to the R100's price point. The R50V is basically the R50 with its EVF chopped off, and for that, it's available for $569.

I will admit, it is impossible to buy any other mirrorless camera brand-new that comes with a lens and memory card for $520. The lens Canon bundles with the R100 isn't good, but to get it separately costs $350. That means the R100 actually is worth just $170 as part of this kit. Again, this camera's only selling point is that it is cheap as dirt. But I beg you to find something that's affordable and also good.

Right now at KEH, you can get a Canon 5D Mark II in excellent condition for $296. You can then pick up a 24-105mm f/4L for $475 or the more entry-level option of 24-105mm f/3.5-5.6 for $256. Both options are slightly more expensive than Costco's kit, but are substantially better combos that will actually provide a burgeoning photographer with the ability to learn and grow. There are so many other possibilities on the used market, too. This is just one example.

Canon 5D Mark IIBuy used on KEH.comCanon EF 24-105mm f/4 L IS USMBuy used on KEH.comCanon EF 24-105mm f/3.5-5.6 IS STMBuy used on KEH.com

This all takes me back to my real main issue with the R100. It's barely fine as far as technology goes, but it is so limited that it serves as a poor camera to invest anything more than a first glance into the world of photography with. It cannot grow with someone beyond the very first stage of photography and after that, they're going to want one of these other cameras I've mentioned that costs just a little bit more. Why spend that amount twice? Skip the R100 stage and go for a camera that can support several years of interest, not just a few weeks.

I love photography. It would break my heart if the reason someone didn't realize they loved it too was because the experience of the R100 was so bland and uninteresting that they gave up on the hobby before it had time to take root.

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

Supreme Court agrees to weigh in on Goldman Sachs arbitration dispute

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Friday afternoon added a new case to its oral argument docket for the 2026-27 term, granting a petition for review filed by Goldman Sachs Bank in a case over whether and when a bankruptcy court can override the Federal Arbitration Act and refuse to honor an agreement to arbitrate disputes.

The dispute in which the justices agreed to weigh in, Goldman Sachs Bank USA v. Brown, began three years ago, when Rhea Brown and Gregory Maze filed for bankruptcy. Both listed credit card debts with Goldman Sachs Bank as one of their debts.

The Bankruptcy Code imposes an automatic stay, which (among other things) bars creditors from attempting to collect debts from someone who has filed for bankruptcy. Because Goldman Sachs, according to Maze and Brown, repeatedly continued to try to seek payment of their credit card debts, they filed an adversary proceeding (that is, a separate lawsuit within the broader bankruptcy case) against the bank, in which they contended that the bank had willfully violated the automatic stay provision.

Pointing to an arbitration clause in their credit card agreements, Goldman Sachs asked the bankruptcy court to put the adversary proceeding on hold and require Brown and Maze to arbitrate their dispute. The bankruptcy court denied that request, and both the federal district court and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 4th Circuit upheld that ruling. A divided three-judge panel of the 4th Circuit concluded that requiring Brown and Maze to arbitrate their claim “would interfere and conflict with the strong and established policies and purposes of the Bankruptcy Code.”

Goldman Sachs came to the Supreme Court in June, asking the justices to take up its case. It told the court that the 4th Circuit’s ruling squarely conflicted with a decision by the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 2nd Circuit. Moreover, it added, “[t]he practical stakes of these issues are enormous” because of the ubiquity of arbitration clauses in consumer credit agreements. The 4th Circuit’s decision, it said, “imperils the enforceability of arbitration agreements in many of them.”

After considering Goldman Sachs’ petition for review at four consecutive conferences, the justices granted it on Friday afternoon. The case likely will be argued sometime next year, with a decision to follow by late June or early July.

More orders from the justices’ private conference on Friday are expected at 9:30 a.m. EST on Monday.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

New Federal Reserve Research Finds Cutting Merchant Swipe Fees Even Hurts Consumers Who Don’t Earn Rewards

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

You can earn no miles, no points and no cash back from your credit card and still lose if the government cuts credit card merchant swipe fees. Research from two Philadelphia Federal Reserve economists explains why: the money banks collect when you spend helps pay for better borrowing terms. That’s a problem for the retailers’ argument that consumers who don’t collect rewards are simply paying for everyone else’s vacations. I wrote this week about why the legislation threatening card rewards isn’t dead, even with Dick Durbin retiring and Roger Marshall losing his Senate seat. The political sales pitch is that reducing merchants’ payment costs saves consumers money. That isn’t true and has never been true anywhere in the world it’s been tried. It’s cover for retailers who want the benefits of accepting credit cards, but think the government should require the service be offered to them for less money. But credit cards aren’t just a convenient way to make purchases. They also provide financing for those purchases, and the merchant fee helps support that. Even A Card With No Rewards Can Give Something Back Satyajit Chatterjee and Burcu Eyigungor’s Credit Effects of Credit Card Interchange Fees uses bank supervisory data to examine an overlooked part of the debate: what interchange does to credit itself. The June 26 paper was highlighted in a new Bank of Japan summary. In the data, rewards cards have higher credit limits. Cards without rewards have lower interest rates despite higher default rates. They argue that rewards make repayment more attractive because default means losing access to future rewards. And when a bank keeps interchange instead of paying rewards, competition encourages it to offer better rates and limits to attract the customer’s business. They find that both rewards and non-rewards cardholders gain, even after accounting for higher retail prices. A bank earning money when that person buys groceries has another reason to want the account. Eliminate that revenue and the account becomes less attractive at the same borrowing terms. The adjustment could show up in the interest rate, credit limit or card approvals. Limiting interchange hurts access to credit and credit costs for the most vulnerable> Most Cards Earn Rewards—And Most Interchange Already Goes Back Out Rewards aren’t confined to a small group of people flying first class. The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau’s latest market report says that in 2024: 80% of general purpose credit card accounts offered rewards. 92% of spending on those cards was on rewards accounts. And a separate New York Fed study finds that banks receive interchange averaging 1.82% of purchase volume and incur rewards costs averaging 1.57%. That’s about 86% of issuer interchange revenue going back out as rewards expense. Limits on credit card swipe fees actually redistribute from consumers to merchants, even as proponents seek to villify the banks. Points Aren’t The Whole Reason To Use Credit Instead Of Debit If an airline takes your money and doesn’t provide the flight, the card you used matters. So does whether a fraudulent charge has already emptied your checking account while everyone sorts out what happened. Credit cards come with federal billing dispute rights, including for purchases that weren’t delivered as agreed. With a billing error dispute, you can withhold the disputed amount during the investigation. Debit cards have fraud protections, too. But the money can already be gone from your account. Having a claim for reimbursement isn’t the same as keeping your cash available for rent while the claim gets resolved. Then there are the benefits particular cards add: purchase protection against theft or damage, extended warranties, return protection, rental-car coverage and insurance for covered travel disruptions. Interchange helps fund the card’s economics: rewards, fraud losses, dispute handling and benefits. Cutting interchange doesn’t repeal federal consumer protections athough those were imposed on an economic model that included them. And it an change what banks charge for the product, which optional benefits they offer and which customers they want. Topics on this pageCredit cardFederal Reserve Bank of PhiladelphiaInterchange feeConsumer Financial Protection BureauDick DurbinRoger MarshallUnited States Senate+4 more More From View from the Wing

That’s a problem for the retailers’ argument that consumers who don’t collect rewards are simply paying for everyone else’s vacations.

I wrote this week about why the legislation threatening card rewards isn’t dead, even with Dick Durbin retiring and Roger Marshall losing his Senate seat. The political sales pitch is that reducing merchants’ payment costs saves consumers money.

That isn’t true and has never been true anywhere in the world it’s been tried. It’s cover for retailers who want the benefits of accepting credit cards, but think the government should require the service be offered to them for less money.

But credit cards aren’t just a convenient way to make purchases. They also provide financing for those purchases, and the merchant fee helps support that.

Satyajit Chatterjee and Burcu Eyigungor’s Credit Effects of Credit Card Interchange Fees uses bank supervisory data to examine an overlooked part of the debate: what interchange does to credit itself. The June 26 paper was highlighted in a new Bank of Japan summary. In the data, rewards cards have higher credit limits. Cards without rewards have lower interest rates despite higher default rates. They argue that rewards make repayment more attractive because default means losing access to future rewards. And when a bank keeps interchange instead of paying rewards, competition encourages it to offer better rates and limits to attract the customer’s business. They find that both rewards and non-rewards cardholders gain, even after accounting for higher retail prices. A bank earning money when that person buys groceries has another reason to want the account. Eliminate that revenue and the account becomes less attractive at the same borrowing terms. The adjustment could show up in the interest rate, credit limit or card approvals. Delta Air Lines Cancels 15 Routes From 3 Busiest Hubs: See Full List Now

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Network consolidation is inevitable at the best of times, and major carriers like Delta Air Lines are not exempt. External pressures such as high fuel prices have forced airlines to be more clinical than usual with route cuts this year, with the Atlanta-based SkyTeam founding member axing a combined 15 routes from its top three hubs by flights and passengers. These are Atlanta, Detroit, and Minneapolis.

Scheduling data from Cirium, an aviation analytics company, shows that Atlanta was the top casualty, losing six Delta Air Lines routes. It was followed by Detroit and Minneapolis respectively, with five and four corridors having been shelved between January and October 2026. This does not include routes that operated only briefly to serve special events.

Starting with Detroit Metropolitan Wayne County Airport (DTW), Delta's Michigan hub saw its first cut in May, when flights to Sacramento International Airport (SMF) in California came to an end. This corridor was followed by Reykjavík Keflavík International Airport (KEF) in August and Pensacola and Bozeman in September. None of these routes currently have any Delta flights scheduled in 2027.

Most recently, the airline ended its flights from Detroit to Bangor International Airport in Maine (BGR) in October. Nonetheless, it hasn't been all doom and gloom for Delta in Detroit this year, with the carrier announcing last month that it would be launching a new route from DTW to Athens in June 2027.

Wayne County Airport Authority CEO Chad Newton welcomed the move.

"We appreciate Delta Air Lines’ continued investment in Detroit and are excited about the new opportunities this service will create for tourism, business, and cultural connections."

Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) had the biggest share of Delta's route cuts this year, with six corridors falling to the sword. Most of these ended relatively recently, except the route to Santa Barbara Municipal Airport (SBA) in California, which Delta last served in January 2026. Otherwise, the rest were axed in September or October 2026.

Arguably the most interesting of the September cuts was Delta's route from Atlanta to Argyle International Airport (SVD) in St. Vincent and the Grenadines, as it lasted only one season. Elsewhere, September also marked the end of Delta's routes from Atlanta to Rapid City Regional Airport (RAP), Traverse City Cherry Capital Airport (TVC), and Vancouver International Airport (YVR).

Delta's Top Hubs In November 2026 (Cirium)

Meanwhile, Detroit was not the only airport from which Delta ceased flying to Bangor in October 2026. Its route from Atlanta to the Maine city also ended last month. As a result, the carrier now serves only two destinations from Bangor: John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) and LaGuardia Airport (LGA). In both cases, flights are operated by Endeavor Air as Delta Connection.

Delta unveils its biggest Delta One overhaul in years, featuring luxury dining, enhanced comfort and global expansion in 2027.

Moving on to Minneapolis-St. Paul International Airport (MSP), the Minnesota hub has lost four of its Delta routes this year. These cuts were split evenly between April and September 2026, with two cuts apiece. In April, the carrier cut its short-haul international leisure network, ceasing service from Minneapolis to St. Maarten in the Caribbean and Mazatlan in Mexico.

Meanwhile, in September, Delta made two more domestic cuts to its Minneapolis network. The routes in question served Williston Basin International Airport (XWA) in North Dakota and Wilmington International Airport (ILM) in North Carolina. As a result, Delta now has no flights at all to Williston, while it continues to serve Wilmington from Atlanta with mainline flights and LaGuardia with regional services.

He Was Spurs’ Record Transfer - Now He’s 29, Without A Club, and Worth Just £1.6m

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Just seven years after becoming Tottenham Hotspur's record signing, Tanguy Ndombele finds himself without a club.

Born in Longjumeau near Paris, Ndombele rose through the ranks at Guingamp and Amiens, before signing for Lyon in 2017. After enjoying two exceptional years with Les Gones, Tottenham forked out a fee worth upwards of £64 million to bring the midfielder to North London.

He went on to make just 91 appearances for the Lilywhites, before a series of uninspiring loan spells resulted in his contract with Spurs being mutually terminated.

Signing a two-year contract at Nice following his departure from the Premier League in 2024, he failed to impress in the South of France and has subsequently been unemployed since being released in the summer.

Having made his debut in Ligue 2 for Amiens at the age of 18, Ndombele was revered as one of the up-and-coming French talents to watch out for. It wasn't long before Lyon came calling, and he was shining on the biggest stage in his home country.

After making 96 appearances for the Ligue 1 giants and helping them reach the Champions League knockout stages in 2018/19, Ndombele attracted interest from several of Europe's biggest clubs in the 2019 summer transfer window. Tottenham reportedly fended off competition from the likes of Real Madrid and Juventus to secure the signature of the then 22-year-old, who was also beginning to establish himself in the French national team under Didier Deschamps.

The North Londoners, who had just reached a Champions League final, paid a premium for the number eight. The £64 million fee surpassed their previous club record signing, Davinson Sanchez, who had joined from Ajax two years earlier for around £38 million.

This substantial outlay came with hefty expectations, and the pressure this created clearly got to the player. Towards the end of his debut campaign in English football, Spurs boss Jose Mourinho publicly criticised Ndombele for what the manager believed to be a lack of professionalism.

"He's a player with great talent," Mourinho told BBC Sport in 2020.

"He has to know he has to do much better and know I cannot keep giving him opportunities to play because the team is much more important."

This unprofessional tag stuck to Ndombele, with the next few Spurs managers not taking a liking to him either. Ryan Mason, Nuno Espirito Santo and Antonio Conte all granted the player few opportunities to impress (barring a relatively promising 2020/21 season), while persistent fitness issues derailed his time in N17.

He was eventually loaned back to Lyon in an attempt to revitalise his career. After this stint failed, he spent a season each at Napoli and Galatasaray before Tottenham and his representatives agreed to terminate his agreement with the North London club in 2024.

Walking into the job market as a damaged asset, desperately in need of a permanent home, Nice offered him a lifeline back in France in the summer of 2024. Ndombele took this opportunity, signing a two-year deal with the Ligue 1 club.

Despite impressing in his first season under Franck Haise at the Allianz Riviera, the box-to-box midfielder struggled in his second campaign, making just 13 appearances across all competitions. This disappointing second year cost him a new contract, meaning he once again entered the market as a free agent.

He's yet to be offered a lifeline this time around, and TransferMarkt subsequently values the ex-Spurs man at just €2 million (£1.67 million).

The wages of every player at Tottenham for the 2026/27 season, including that of James Maddison and Dominic Solanke.

According to the Sun, Ndombele went on trial at Russian club Rubin Kazan back in October. The Russian Premier League outfit seemingly gave the Frenchman an opportunity to prove that he's still at the level, but there have been no reports that he's been offered a contract since.

The 29-year-old remains available, but the longer he goes without finding a new club, the more difficult it becomes to envisage him returning to the pitch professionally.

Liverpool have 'concerns' about Milos Kerkez, Andoni Iraola's updated stance revealed

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Transfer Correspondent AUTHORITY Former Sky Sports and ESPN FC correspondent who specialises in global recruitment, contract negotiations, and high-velocity transfer breaking news. FOCUS Exclusive reporting, domestic and international transfer markets, and elite-level insights. THE INSIGHT Pete utilises a Global Network of Industry Sources to deliver verified, first-to-market reporting. His “Deep-Lens” analysis connects the dots between boardroom strategy and pitch-level recruitment, providing a level of transfer clarity that goes beyond surface-level rumours.

Liverpool are beginning to develop concerns about Milos Kerkez's displays so far this season, sources have told Football Insider.

The 22-year-old has retained his position as the starting left-back this season, and has started ten games across all competitions so far, including eight in the Premier League.

However, his inconsistent displays have led to question marks about his long-term role in the side, and whether Liverpool will need to strengthen in his position.

As I revealed on the latest edition of Football Insider's Transfer Insider podcast, Kerkez's performances have been a concern at Anfield but are unlikely to see him axed.

Andoni Iraola is a big fan of the Hungary international, who he also worked with at Bournemouth, and has been backed to get him back to his best in the near future.

While there are worries about his displays and fans have begun to grow frustrated, Liverpool have no major issues with Kerkez that would lead to any potential exit.

Football Insider reported last month that Kerkez could find himself axed from Iraola's starting XI after a string of disappointing performances for club and country.

Liverpool's 3-2 defeat against Fenerbahce in the Champions League last time out once again highlighted clear weaknesses in his game, which are becoming a problem.

However, Kerkez has the support of Iraola and the Liverpool coaching staff, who have been backed to help him turn around his form and get back to his best levels.

Speaking on the Transfer Insider podcast, I explained: "I think there's a few concerns, there's no doubt about it, in that left back position with Milos Kerkez.

"Kerkes was obviously bought in the previous summer for big money, but maybe he has failed to convince a lot of Liverpool fans with his performances.

"You've got to remember, he's still only 22, so still a young player who has come to a club with big aspirations, so he will be given a bit of time.

"He does try and give his all, but maybe he's lacking in quality with his final ball and everything else, so that has been a bit of a frusration.

"But he's now working under a manager who got the best out of him at Bournemouth, who helped him win that big money move to Liverpool in the first place.

"So, I'm sure Andoni Iraola still has big hopes that Kerkez can come good at Anfield, and they will be working with him to get back to his best."

Kerkez's recent performances have led to calls for Liverpool to strengthen at left-back, adding to the already-present concerns about their current crop of defensive options.

Work is already believed to be underway behind the scenes to strengthen Iraola's side both at right-back and centre-back, where the lack of depth has become a concern.

Football Insider revealed on 26 October that Liverpool have a four-man shortlist of centre-back targets who they feel could improve their squad in next summer's window.

Borussia Dortmund star Nico Schlotterbeck and Inter Milan's Italy international Alessandro Bastoni have both been long-term targets for the Reds in recent years.

Meanwhile, versatile Barcelona defender Jules Kounde ranks highly on their list, and Nottingham Forest's Murillo is also an option the recruitment team will consider.

Democrats just won control of Georgia Power’s regulator for the first time in 30 years — and its OpenAI data center contract was entirely redacted

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

“This story was originally published by Grist. Sign up for Grist’s weekly newsletter here.”

Beginning in January, three of the five officials on the Georgia Public Service Commission will be Democrats. It will be the first time the party has controlled the powerful energy regulation board in 30 years. The commission, which regulates utilities including the state’s largest electric provider, Georgia Power, has come under more scrutiny in recent years due to rising power bills and the high energy demands of data centers.

Now, Democrats plan to focus on affordability, clean energy, and closer scrutiny of Georgia Power.

“There’s a long laundry list of things that need to be looked into,” said Democratic commissioner Peter Hubbard, who won his reelection bid this week. “And so we’ll have to triage, in fact, and decide how best to move forward.”

One of his first priorities will be launching an affordability docket, he said — a series of hearings that would delve into the reasons for high power bills and ways to save customers money. Hubbard said he and his fellow Democrats also plan to bring close scrutiny to upcoming Georgia Power requests around making more energy and adjusting customer rates. Shelia Edwards and Alicia Johnson are the other two Democrats on the PSC.

But any changes, especially to power bills, will likely take time.

“We may not be able to expect immediate effects on our wallets,” said Emory University associate professor Kristin Phillips, who studies energy burden and the PSC. “But I think we can expect immediate change in the types of decisions that are made.”

Affordability and high energy bills were driving issues in the campaigns both this year and last year, the two elections that together resulted in the new Democratic majority on the commission.

“We knew that we had to connect the dots between energy and affordability,” said Brionté McCorkle of Georgia Conservation Voters, a group that campaigned for the Democratic candidates and worked to draw attention to the PSC elections. “It’s not just this obscure regulatory body like it used to be treated. Its decisions directly affect household budgets, communities, health, and our energy future.”

Chris Womack, the CEO of Southern Company, Georgia Power’s parent company, also acknowledged the central importance of affordability during the company’s third-quarter earnings call on Thursday. But Womack said he’s confident the utility can address those concerns with the new commissioners.

“If you look at what we have been doing with rate freezes, with benefits, with how we are providing benefits from growth to customers — existing customers — as well as to the communities, we think that’s a strong platform to stand on,” he said.

Since the data center boom in Georgia began, Georgia Power has maintained that the increased revenue would more than pay for the new infrastructure to serve them, which the utility argues would keep rates lower for residential and small business customers. The utility calls this “downward pressure.” It doesn’t mean rates will go down, but rather that any increases would be offset by the influx of revenue from data centers.

The utility recently doubled down on that promise as part of its deal to serve a massive new OpenAI data center. The deal, Georgia Power said, would bring $15 of not savings but downward pressure per month for what Georgia Power calls the “typical” residential customer.

Separately, Georgia Power also credited the increased revenue from data centers for the temporary rate freeze approved last year. But the rate freeze received criticism from some advocacy groups. Instead of first holding a PSC hearing to give advocates a chance to verify the utility’s financial claims, Georgia Power announced the temporary rate freeze. Advocates feel that the backward process left them unable to confirm whether the company was delivering on its promises and fully shielding ordinary customers from data center costs.

It’s part of a broader criticism often leveled at the PSC and Georgia Power: lack of transparency. The OpenAI contract was entirely redacted, and only a summary provided to the public after it was approved. Specific details involved in service contracts, deals to buy energy, and construction costs are often redacted from the utility’s public filings under the auspices of trade secrecy.

“I absolutely think transparency is potentially one of the biggest impacts here,” said Phillips of the election results. “It has been often on advocates to push on the way that the PSC communicates about the decisions that it’s making, and I’m hoping that there will be more clarity and transparency.”

The Democrats on the commission have promised to bring increased scrutiny to the next rate-setting process, due in 2028, along with Georgia Power’s long-term plans to make and deliver energy, its forecasts about rising demand, and other issues. Hubbard has also proposed conducting a rate case for the state’s natural gas utilities, which he said hasn’t been done in over a decade. And he said they’ll push for other changes going forward.

“We’ll continue with an agenda next year and the year after that to take advantage of this four-year period when we have the majority and can push for long-lasting reforms and shifting the direction of the state in terms of energy,” Hubbard said.

This article originally appeared in Grist at https://grist.org/georgia-psc/democrats-take-control-of-georgias-powerful-utility-regulator/.

Grist is a nonprofit, independent media organization dedicated to reporting on climate change.

Stuck on a 'treadmill of high inflation': San Francisco Fed's Daly says more rate hikes likely needed

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The San Francisco Fed president said the labor market gives the central bank room to focus on inflation after October payrolls rose 165,000

San Francisco Fed President Mary Daly said Friday that further rate hikes will probably be necessary to rein in inflation, though when the Fed acts and by how much remains undecided, she told CNBC.

"My expectation is we're going to have to tighten policy further," Daly said, according to Bloomberg. "Exactly when and how much is still to be decided because we just have to take all the variables that are coming in."

Her remarks came as the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported that nonfarm payrolls rose by 165,000 in October and the unemployment rate was 4.1%. Average hourly earnings are up 3.1% from a year earlier.

Daly described the jobs report as "good news for American workers" while warning that rising prices are putting a strain on family finances, according to CNBC. "The job market remains stable and solid, but not booming, and you see real wages falling because those wages are not really keeping up with inflation," she said. "I think that's the crux of this."

The consumer price index rose 3.7% in September from a year earlier. "This has been high for too long, almost six years, and we're really starting to feel the burden of that spread everywhere," Daly said.

Daly said officials had expected cooler prices outside of housing in the services category, but that has not happened, according to Bloomberg. She also said the solid labor market gives the Fed room to treat inflation as its top priority.

Daly said she continues to prefer a measured pace of tightening, and she gets a vote on the rate-setting committee next year, according to CNBC. She said she backed both the September hike and the decision to hold last week.

Richmond Fed President Thomas Barkin said Thursday that he too expects rates to climb but does not know when that will happen, according to CNBC.

The Fed held its benchmark rate at 3.75% to 4% last week, with two officials dissenting in favor of a quarter-point increase. Fed Governor Lisa Cook and Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack cast the dissenting votes. The committee had raised the rate by a quarter point in September, its first increase since July 2023.

The committee said it would rather hold off until "the accumulation of more information" could guide what it does next, according to CNBC.

Here's How the Blue Tsunami Will Turn the Healthcare Tide

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

— The Democrats have an opportunity to advance public health, but getting things done won't be easy

Hubris played such a central role in the 2026 midterm election that the ancient Greeks would surely have loved it.

The midterms featured a mortal who promised to avoid war, lower prices, expel immigrants who had committed crimes, and bring prosperity to his nation. He proudly insisted that he -- and only he -- knew how to achieve these Herculean goals. He bragged about the cleverness of his methods and his many (supposed) successes.

All the while, he sowed chaos and distrust, ignored human needs, blamed others for his failures, used excessive force, alienated friends, embraced dictators, and hurt the economy. A survey showed that more than six in 10 of his countrymen said he prioritized his own interests above those of his country.

On November 3, 2026, tens of millions of people rose up to punish his extreme pride, dangerous overconfidence, and arrogance that have, in one way or another, affected them negatively. Healthcare issues were certainly among the grievances.

Now that the blue tsunami has changed the playing field, Democrats must deliver. They need to achieve something concrete over the next 2 years, and they must prioritize the issues, such as healthcare, that were salient to voters in this election cycle. Otherwise, they risk being punished for their own ineptitude and hubris.

Democrats will quickly learn that getting something done will be harder than they imagine. Here are some of the health policy challenges Democrats, Republicans, and President Trump will face in 2027 and 2028.

Between the election and the swearing-in of a new Congress on January 3, 2027, the old Congress will be in a lame duck session. These periods tend to promise a lot and then deliver extraordinarily little. Beyond appropriations, defense reauthorization, and perhaps some renewed efforts to end the war in Iran, I expect little to nothing else, including on healthcare.

During a lame-duck session, nominations and legislation go nowhere unless they have broad bipartisan support and members of Congress feel some urgency. Even then, agreed-upon compromises may not survive procedural objections, time constraints, or general indifference from departing Congress members who want to go home. Simply put, those who have retired or lost their races are deeply conscious of their lame-duck status.

In prior MedPage Today editorials, I pointed out that cabinet secretaries often leave after 2 years, but that Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. would likely stay. After all, Trump has continued to support Kennedy, and Kennedy has continued to have the opportunity of a lifetime to advance his Make America Healthy Again agenda and anti-vaccine stance.

Post-election, the job will quickly become a nightmare for Kennedy, as he will have to respond to hostile investigative hearings led by Democrats on the health committees. And Kennedy is unlikely to get White House help: Trump will have bigger fights to address and less political capital to spend.

Also, if impeachment hearings were to start, Kennedy would be behind only Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth as the object of Congressional attention. This situation will evolve, but I suspect Kennedy will leave (resign) rather than face congressional challenges to his own hubris.

HHS Leadership and Senate Confirmation Hearings

Controversial nominations rarely advance during a lame-duck session. Once the new Congress starts, all past nominations expire, and the White House must start anew. They are not obligated to submit the same names.

Given the White House affiliation with Heidi Overton, MD, PhD, and her weaker credentials compared to her predecessors, I see no way senators would confirm her as FDA commissioner in the lame-duck session. Obviously, her odds would be even worse next year with a Democratic-led Senate. Kennedy, or perhaps his successor, will need to look elsewhere.

Hypothetically, Overton could be renominated solely to trigger provisions in law that would allow Kyle Diamantas, JD, to continue as acting commissioner in 2027. But more likely, another nominee will emerge.

Investigations, Vetoes, Riders, and Compromises

Investigations are the classic response to leading one or both houses of Congress when the President is of the other party. There will be multiple hearings on the administration's opposition to vaccines; the first one will probably focus on measles.

Of course, the new Democratic majority has other options to advance its policy agenda:

As an example of advancing policy via riders, let's look at one of those must-pass bills: FDA user fee legislation. The bill renewal comes with a tradition of serving as a vehicle for non-user fee issues. If it isn't enacted sooner, I would expect efforts like the Clinical Trial Modernization Act to be added to the reauthorization bill. The FDA user fee legislation must become law by September 30, but ideally should be completed by June or July. Perfect timing.

Of course, it is too soon for anyone to know the Democratic strategy because it hasn't been decided. My guess: it will turn out to be a combination of approaches.

Despite the threat of Trump vetoes, Democrats will have the opportunity to advance two of their healthcare priorities in 2027: undoing Medicaid program cuts and restoring Obamacare insurance premium subsidies.

The easy part is creating a reconciliation bill, so that only 51 votes will be needed for Senate passage.

The harder part is finding $1.2 trillion over 10 years to pay for the program restorations. In the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA), these cuts already offset the cost of the OBBBA tax cuts.

Democrats will have to find that money through program savings, tax increases, or by admitting they are adding to the deficit. Despite Democratic enthusiasm for action early next year, I don't see how they can do that quickly or easily. They will need to build a consensus on the offsets first.

Many of the newly elected House Democrats from red, pink, and purple states will not be reelected in 2028 if Democrats focus on a dramatic, expensive new federal program instead of solving the issues at the heart of the midterm elections. Those Democrats outnumber the enlarged and noisy new influx of progressives pushing Medicare for All.

Polls suggest two-thirds of voters support "Medicare for All." However, support is cut in half when the issue of tax increases is raised. In my opinion, advocates would best serve their cause by focusing first on a smaller Medicare expansion, such as a buy-in option for individuals between 55 and 65.

My larger concern is that Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt.), likely the next chairman of the Senate Committee on Health, Education, Labor & Pensions and a passionate supporter of Medicare for All, will put a spotlight on this issue, and give lesser attention and support to other health programs that have a more realistic chance of moving forward over the next 2 years.

Not long ago, a broad swath of health issues was bipartisan and rarely controversial or challenged: vaccines, an effective CDC, NIH funding, the value of research universities, and a strong FDA. While some resistance may linger, I believe these will be bipartisan again by the next election cycle, if not sooner. That is a huge win for public health and for all Americans.

Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Civil Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

Ralph Lauren Pre-Fall 2027

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ralph Lauren picked up the Menswear Designer of the Year Award at the CFDAs earlier this week. He also took home the organization’s first men’s prize in 1981. That means—if I have my dates right—he’s been at the top of American fashion since before this year’s other winners were born. In an industry built on change, renewing and maintaining a legacy can be as difficult as building one, but even as he approaches the brand’s 60th anniversary, Lauren still has fashion’s attention. PoloFest, the vintage marketplace the company organized in Tokyo last month, with its 5,000 specially selected archival Polo pieces and 10,000 visitors, is bound to spawn imitators across the business.

This pre-fall collection wasn’t designed to make that kind of noise, but it’s as rooted in the brand’s iconography as anything else it does. Take the glen plaid pantsuit in look 3—it’s based on a suit that Lauren himself frequently wears and made by tailors in Italy with the specialized techniques that give men’s suiting its sharp lines and structure. The track pants in look 1 are another Ralph tic; he’s always liked mixing casual and formal, low and high. Purple is the accent color here this season, as it is across so much of fashion, alongside minty green, though we can’t blame the prevailing currents. Lauren pays no heed to trends. Case in point: The silvery plunge-front halter top is a lift from his own 1990s archives.

Art Collector Barbara Jakobson’s Famous Upper East Side Townhouse On the Market for $9 Million

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Barbara Jakobson was a monumental force in the New York art world for over 60 years, serving as an influential collector, a fierce patron, and a longtime trustee of New York’s Museum of Modern Art (MoMA). She also had a sharp, curatorial eye and a truly singular style. That is perhaps best evident in her home on Manhattan’s Upper East Side, which doubled as her own ever-evolving gallery from the mid-1960s until her death in 2025.“I see the house as a vessel for an ongoing autobiographical exercise,” Jakobson told Curbed in a 2021 interview. “I keep the transformation as proof of life.” Related Articles R.M. Schindler's Modernist Beach House, Built for a California Health Nut, Hits the Market for a Breezy $18.8 Million Art Collector and Publisher Benedikt Taschen Lists Mid-Century Modern Beverly Hills Home for $25 M.

The five-story townhouse along East 74th Street in the Lenox Hill neighborhood was built in 1910 and later expanded by architect John Kingsland in 1956. Prior to Jakobson, filmmaker Elia Kazan lived in the 20-foot-wide abode while directing East of Eden (1955) and Splendor in the Grass (1961). Hollywood stars including James Dean, Natalie Wood, and Marlon Brando reportedly visited then.

Jakobson, a native New Yorker, bought the pad in 1965 and lived there for nearly six decades. During that time, she welcomed New York’s creative set, such as artists Jasper Johns, Andy Warhol, Cy Twombly, and Robert Rauschenberg. After her death last summer, the property was brought to market in April 2026 for $9.8 million. It received a price cut of over $800,000 before reaching its current asking price of $8.99 million. Leslie Hirsch, Edward Joseph, Howard Morrel, and Benjamin Anderson of Christie’s International Real Estate New York hold the listing.Nicknamed “the Squirrel House” on account of the bronze squirrel sculptures at the entrance, the home is whimsical yet refined. Red lacquered doors open to 6,000 square feet of stylish interiors, with eight bedrooms and six full bathrooms spread throughout.A grand entry hall with triple arches and custom wallpaper by artist Peter Halley leads to a parlor floor featuring a formal living room, a gallery, and a salon with a wood-burning fireplace. A staircase—on which Jakobson was photographed for Vogue—spirals down to a spectacular double-height great room, where an outline of Frank Stella’s 1971 Felsztyn III remains. It once hung there but was sold in 2005 at a Christie’s auction featuring works from Jakobson’s collection. Glass doors lead out to a private 350-square-foot garden.The guest bedrooms, including two junior suites with dressing rooms and en suites, sprawl across the next two levels. The primary suite, meanwhile, covers the entire top floor, offering a light-filled private lounge for reading, relaxing, or even pondering art.

Carson Daly, Laura Jarrett Ready to Launch ‘New Era’ at NBC’s ‘Saturday Today’

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Carson Daly was looking for a new challenge in a long career in the media business. On Saturday morning, he’ll get it.Daly will team up with Laura Jarrett, Joe Fryer, and meteorologist Angie Lassman on NBC’s “Saturday Today” telecast, the latest attempt by one of the big broadcast networks to create bespoke versions of their morning franchises that appeal to weekend audiences with a little more time on their hands in the A.M.

“If people are waking up with us, we want to respect that and we want to give them something that’s worth tuning into,” says Jarrett, who joined “Today” in 2023, a year after moving to NBC News from CNN. Related Stories Judge Orders Donald Trump to Testify Under Oath in $10 Billion BBC Defamation Case James Austin Johnson's Trump Stops by 'SNL U.K.' for Meeting With Burnham and Zelenskyy: 'You Don't Have One Guy Who Can Play Trump?'

NBC, ABC and CBS have worked to create weekend-news programs with a little more distinction from their weekday flagships. The Saturday telecast of CBS’ “Saturday Morning” puts a spotlight on topics such as food and music. ABC recently recalibrated the anchor teams on its weekend broadcasts of “Good Morning America,” adding Rachel Scott and Rhiannon Ally.

NBC has been encouraged in this vision by the Sunday telecast of “Today,” led by Willie Geist. The anchor offers a slower-paced hour of “Today” that tackles headlines as warranted, but also offers up a deeper interview with a person of interest as well as other features. Geist typically writes much of the show by himself. NBC News has experimented with turning Geist’s longer-form interviews into ticketed events, a move that generates live-media revenue.

Adding Daly, however, brings a person with a different set of skills to the fore of the Saturday show. Jarrett’s previous co-host, Peter Alexander, had a long tenure as a Washington and White House correspondent. Daly has cultivated a broader set of experiences, famously hosting MTV’s “TRL” earlier in his career, then going to host the wee-hours NBC program “Last Call” and host and executive produce “The Voice.”

Over that time, he’s learned to lead in-depth conversations and hold forth with musicians, newsmakers and entertainers. “I’ve always enjoyed longer-form interviews,” Daly says, and he studied Bob Costas and Greg Kinnear, who hosted late-night shows at NBC before his tenure started. “I have a natural curiosity,’” he says, and often comes into a segment with “one thousand questions” he might want to ask a guest — if only “Today” had more time for extended interviews

Daly has been a staple on “Today” since 2013, when he joined as a correspondent of sorts, curating interesting developments taking place on social media from the show’s “Orange Room.” These days, he’s more of a regular presence in the weekday telecast’s second hour, and is often found talking alongside Savannah Guthrie, Craig Melvin, Al Roker and other “Today” anchors.

Several months ago, he considered how he might push himself, and asked “Today” producers if they would consider letting him co-anchor on days when others were off — even on holidays. “I’ve never been afraid to take on a challenge,” Daly says. “I’m glad I stuck my neck out.”

Jarrett has been enjoying her time on “Today” as well as her position as NBC News’ senior legal correspondent. Saturday viewers can still expect to get the headlines, she says. “We will still do the news. We will still do special reports” if warranted, she says.

Jarrett has “a style on TV that I happen to not only respect but also enjoy watching, and so I think I’m going to learn a lot,” says Daly.

Daly takes up his new duties just as the holiday season approaches, a cycle that often brings a broader audience to “Today.” On Saturday, says Jarrett, viewers might get a peek at the Rockefeller Center Christmas Tree arriving in New York — in addition to the official launch of “a new era” for the Saturday program.

Taylor Kitsch, Alexandra Daddario & Christopher Walken Set For True Crime-Thriller ‘The Long Island Bank Robber’ — AFM

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Taylor Kitsch (Lone Survivor), Alexandra Daddario (The White Lotus) and screen legend Christopher Walken (The Deer Hunter) are set to star true crime thriller The Long Island Bank Robber.

Based on a true story, Kitsch will star as Stephen Trantel, “a successful Wall Street commodities trader. After being fired from his job, Stephen’s desperate attempts to keep his family afloat lead him down the unimaginable path of becoming one of Long Island’s most prolific bank robbers.”

Daddario will star as Jeanne Trantel, Stephen’s “discerning wife who grows increasingly suspicious of her husband and yearns to pave her own path outside of the home.” Oscar winner Walken will play Stephen’s father, a retired police officer ‘with a strong ethical code.” Related Stories News International Buyers From 12 Territories Launch Film Distribution Network Lokia On Eve Of AFM News Jack Whitehall, Nina Dobrev, Greg Kinnear & Andie MacDowell Set For Rom-Com 'Honeymooners'; HanWay & CAA Launch For AFM

Matthew Gentile (American Murderer) will direct from a screenplay he co-wrote with Bianca Vera, based on the non-fiction book ‘Til Heist Do Us Part by Jeanne Callahan and Michael Harvey. Production is to begin this month in New Jersey. Watch on Deadline

Vaneast Pictures has acquired worldwide sales rights and will launch the project for buyers ahead of next week’s AFM.

The film is being produced by Vince Jolivette and Richie Hill of Filmopoly (The Threesome), Chris Bellant of Vision Entertainment (Neighborhood Watch) and Danielle Gasher of Vaneast Pictures (Hellhound). Taylor Kitsch, Andres Budnik, Liam Scholey, Raymond Kimsey, Matt Allen, Dan Reardon, Kyle Fox, Alan B. Bursteen and Dawn Bursteen serve as executive producers. Patricia DiCerto (Blue Jasmine) has joined as casting director.

Gentile’s feature directorial debut, American Murderer, was a true-crime thriller starring Tom Pelphrey, Ryan Phillippe, Idina Menzel, and Jacki Weaver. The movie won awards at multiple American festivals after launching at Taormina in Italy.

“The Long Island Bank Robber is a true story I learned about in my youth, growing up in New York City as a true-crime obsessive. A propulsive heist film on the surface, the story also tackles complex themes of grief, survival, and marriage. With Taylor, Alexandra, and Christopher as our three leads — I could not have dreamed of a better cast to bring this film to life,” said director and co-writer Matthew Gentile.

“This package is a rare find, with that perfect combination of a gripping true-crime script, a talented director, and a star-studded ensemble. We can’t wait to get this one in front of buyers,” said Danielle Gasher, Vaneast Pictures.

“We’re thrilled to bring Matthew Gentile’s vision for this film to life with this incredible cast and crew, and to be partnering with Vaneast for the global sales. This is such a compelling and layered story that we can’t wait to share with the world,” stated producer Richie Hill.

Kitsch is represented by Untitled Entertainment and CAA. Daddario is represented by CAA, Untitled Entertainment, and Jackoway Austen. Walken is represented by CAA. Matthew Gentile and Bianca Vera are both with Vision Entertainment and Goodman, Genow.

Chen Zhao is the head of economics research, where she produces research on the housing market for public and internal audiences. Why Wallpaper Is the Secret to Making Even the Smallest Bathroom Feel Special

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A little pattern can turn into a true design moment.

When you’re working with limited space, it’s almost instinctive to tone down your design details to avoid feeling overwhelmed—but even small spaces need character. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in excellent design, no matter the room size or purpose. This year’s top recommendation for a chic bath is wallcoverings.

When installed properly, wallcoverings are one of the most reliable ways to add an extra touch of flair to interiors. Here’s how the designers of our 2026 Whole Home used them to bring big personality to small spaces.

For many designers, powder rooms are the perfect opportunity to play. Designer Mark Kennamer, founder and principal designer of his eponymous Birmingham, Alabama–based firm, used wallcoverings to add bespoke character to a nearly monochromatic powder-pink scheme. In the guest bath, the Zoffany mauve moiré wallcovering lends a subtle shimmer and dimension, while its wood-like grain adds an unexpected, welcome layer of texture.

Anissa Zajac of House Seven Design outfitted the primary bathroom with a botanical-printed wallcovering by Sanderson that incorporated nearly every hue in her palette while adding a dose of whimsy. The pattern’s vertical lines play off the texture of the fluted vanity cabinets, making a bold statement without straying too far from the design scheme.

In the gallery bath, the team at R&R Interior Design 365 took inspiration from Charleston’s famed cobblestone roads. They opted for a blue-brick wallpaper by Perennials to channel the perfect level of city flair while keeping the feel warm, intimate, and inviting.

5 Historic ‘Minimansions’ Under $400K Where You Get 3,000+ Square Feet for the Price of a Starter Home

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you’re in the market for a large home but have a limited budget, consider a house that needs some TLC.

For the ambitious investor who wants to roll up their sleeves and dive into a home improvement project, a historic home might just fit the bill.

From New York to Tennessee, we found five “minimansions,” each with more than 3,000 square feet of living space for the price of a starter home.

Magnolia Manor: Here’s a chance for an investor to transform this 1.28-acre legacy compound into a "hospitality destination" or investment property of their dreams, subject to applicable permitting.

Built in 1849 for Judge Austin Miller, the 4,228-square-foot estate, formerly a bed-and-breakfast, has a four-bedroom Greek Revival brick home with a covered front porch, a one-bedroom guest cottage, a potting house, a gazebo, and a detached two-car garage. Historic highlights include double parlors, original architecture, hardwood floors, chandeliers, oversized windows, and brick details.

Located in the North Main Street Historic District, the home needs some work, as reflected in the price, and is being offered as is.

Larry Childers of Childers Real Estate is the listing agent.

Italianate brick mansion: This "treasured" six-bedroom brick estate near the Mohawk River was built in 1860 and has had only two owners.

Two entrances, including one with original horse ties and carriage step, lead to the 4,192-square-foot home full of charm and character. Period details include a grand staircase, preserved hardwood floors, custom moldings, expansive windows, French doors, a butler's pantry, and an original marble fireplace. An original basement kitchen features a fireplace. A cupola provides panoramic waterfront views of the Mohawk Valley.

Listed on the National Register of Historic Places, the home offers the next buyer the opportunity to enjoy a "living piece of American history."

Rebecca Zarski of Howard Hanna Delmar is the listing agent.

The A.M. Legg House: Built in 1913, this historic five-bedroom “architectural treasure” was once the home of former Pontiac Mayor Achalis Morris Legg.

The 3,715-square-foot estate has a covered front porch with original mosaic tile flooring, custom millwork, multiple French doors, preserved hardwood floors, elegant built-ins, four fireplaces, and a pocket door. Other amenities include a sunroom, a dining room with built-in cabinetry, a full walk-up attic with room for expansion, and a partly finished lower-level entertainment room with a bar and a canning room.

A detached two-car garage with a dual-access driveway is also on the property.

David R. Wayman of Eppel Realty is the listing agent.

Louis Bierbauer Brewery country estate: Sitting on 3.59 acres at the base of the Canajoharie Gorge, this picturesque four-bedroom compound is surrounded by original stone walls and sits along a creek.

Built in 1860, the thoughtfully updated 3,528-square-foot interior still has wood-paneled walls, stone details, and built-ins. Modern updates include radiant-heated floors, a kitchen with wood cabinetry and stainless-steel appliances, and a circular staircase. The primary suite has an exposed wood-beam ceiling and a decorative fireplace. An underground grotto “still exists where the beer vats were aged,” according to the listing, which also says the property is not in a flood zone.

A one-bedroom apartment could be used for multigenerational living or as an income-producing rental. A salon space with a dedicated entry could also be used for other business purposes.

Rochelle Monk of Krutz Properties is the listing agent.

Updated Victorian: Built in 1903, this five-bedroom home, originally owned by Delia and Henry Logan of Logan Automobile Co., has 3,180 square feet of carefully updated living space.

Time-honored details include original hardwood floors, period millwork, pocket doors, stained glass, formal living and dining rooms, and seven fireplaces. Updates include new windows, a remodeled kitchen with stainless-steel appliances and a breakfast bar, and a finished third-floor bonus level. A wraparound covered porch would be a nice spot to enjoy morning coffee.

A detached one-car garage is also on the fenced lot.

Blaine Ritchie of Coldwell Banker Select Properties is the listing agent.

First came her 46th birthday looks (that’s right, looks plural): Rousteing hand-selected two glittering minidresses—one sage green, the other burgundy—with matching bejeweled headpieces from the fall 1995 haute couture collection for Kardashian’s celebratory dinner at the Waldorf Astoria Beverly Hills. (What could be a better birthday gift, than the keys to the Rabanne archive?)

Kim Kardashian in archival Rabanne at her 46th birthday party last month.
