Ferrari Is Unstoppable Right Now

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Good morning! It's Friday, November 6, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift, your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition, Ferrari is making even more money than it expected as the rich continue to spend big, Audi could boost the size of its planned U.S. auto plant, battery electric vehicle market share just hit a new record in Europe and Mitsubishi looks to the Pajero to turn things around.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

It's true that you and I don't have any money, but do you know who does? Ferrari buyers. Lots of it, too. That's why the Italian supercar maker raised its forecast for this year's results after beating estimates on core profits and revenue in the third quarter. If only the F1 team could be as successful.

Ferrari pointed to sales of higher-margin vehicles and lucrative optional extras added to its vehicles as reasons for the company's strong financial performance. Order books now reach into 2028, and as we reported earlier this week, the Luce EV is already sold out for all of 2027. From Reuters:

Ferrari guided for its ‌adjusted ⁠earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortisation (EBITDA) to rise to at least €3 billion ($3.36 billion) in 2026, versus a previous forecast for a result of at least €2.97 billion.

The company also nudged up its forecast for this year's net revenue, while guiding for an industrial free cash flow generation of up to €1.65 billion in 2026.

Ferrari's ​forecast upgrade was based on ​stronger-than-expected personalisations – those finishing ⁠touches customers request at extra cost to make their cars better suited to their individual tastes, led by the 296 Speciale model family – as ​well as softer currency headwinds and its current second place in the Formula ​1 Championship.

In the third quarter, Ferrari's adjusted EBITDA rose 12% to €753 million, topping a €739 million analyst consensus in a Reuters poll.

Sales of the €3.6 million limited-edition F80 supercar improved Ferrari's product mix in the ⁠third ​quarter and its result in the period, the company said, ​despite another drop in overall car shipments, as it presses ahead with a planned model changeover.

Between July and September, Ferrari says it shipped 3,368 vehicles, which is actually 33 fewer than the same period last year, but it doesn't seem that the slight discrepancy mattered much.

Ferrari CEO Benedetto Vigna added that Ferrari still has two more model launches coming this year, and he's "sure" clients will be "super happy" about them. Of course, while clients seem to be happy about the Luce, folks who cannot afford one seemed to be very upset. It remains to be seen if that history will repeat itself.

Audi is gearing up to build a massive $4 billion assembly plant in Macon, Georgia, and even though it won't open until the early 2030s, the German automaker is already considering a second phase that'll add $3 billion in costs and an extra 3,000 workers when it's completed in 3033, according to documents released by the Mid-Georgia Gateway Development Authority.

Georgia is giving Audi the 1,500-acre site, providing $458 million in tax incentives and over $120 million in grants for infrastructure, rail improvements, environmental sites, remediation and construction. That's not all, there's another $430 million coming Audi's way for water, road improvements, utility issues and workforce training and recruitment. It's a pretty sweet deal for Audi, I'll tell you what. Of course, that's all on top of the tariff relief President Trump promised Audi to help secure the plant's future. From Reuters:

Two ​sources briefed on ​the matter said ⁠Audi has pushed the US to reduce the 15% tariffs it pays on imports from the EU ​to 7.5%, but that the US Commerce Department was ​expected to ⁠agree to around 10%.

The tariff relief program aims to prod automakers to add US production or other investments, the sources added.

Trump's tariffs on ⁠autos, steel, ​aluminum and parts cost Audi around €1.2 billion ($1.4 ​billion) last year as it imports all its vehicles for the US market from Europe ​or Mexico.

Currently, Audi's plan is for the plant to pump out 200,000 SUVs per year and employ 4,500 workers, so adding another 3,000 workers is no small chunk. There's no word on how much capacity would be increased, but I'd have to assume it's going to be sizable.

Battery-electric vehicles took 34% of new car registrations in 12 European markets in October, which, if you're keeping track at home, is a record share. While that may sound like good news, it's causing a bit of a headache for automakers as it strains supply and squeezes margins, as the surge came faster than some OEMs' battery supply and cost structures could comfortably handle. In Germany, the country where the largest automaker is Europe's are located, BEVs hit a record 37%. That's sort of wild to think about. From Automotive News:

BEV sales in the 12 markets rose 57 percent during the month to reach a 34 percent market share, up from 22.4 percent in October 2025, according to an Automotive News Europe analysis. BEV penetration averaged 21 percent in the first half of 2026 before steadily increasing through the third quarter and reaching 33 percent in September.

In the first 10 months of 2026, BEV sales increased 44 percent to 2.63 million vehicles, while the overall market grew 6 percent. As a result, BEV market share rose to 27 percent from 20 percent a year earlier.

Germany was among the fastest-growing EV markets in October. BEV registrations rose 79 percent to 94,086 vehicles, accounting for a record 37 percent of monthly sales. Through October, battery-electric vehicles represented 28.2 percent of the German market, up nearly 10 percentage points from 2025.

In the U.K., BEVs captured 32.7 percent of registrations in October, up 6.5 percentage points from the same month in 2025. Through October, BEVs held a 27 percent share of the market, compared with 22 percent a year earlier.

France also remained a strong BEV market. Battery-electric registrations totaled 61,958 in October, giving BEVs a 41.5 percent market share, only slightly below September's record 41.7 percent. Through October, BEVs accounted for 32 percent of French registrations, compared with 20 percent in 2025.

Several smaller markets continued to post some of Europe's highest EV penetration rates. BEVs accounted for 55 percent of registrations in the Netherlands, 51 percent in Sweden and 49 percent in Belgium.

There are bottlenecks to deal with for some automakers, though.

Mercedes-Benz said its electric GLC midsize SUV is sold out in Europe for the remainder of 2026 because of exceptionally strong demand. For some configurations, customers placing orders now may have to wait until the third or fourth quarter of 2027 for delivery.

Volkswagen Group has also reported stronger-than-expected demand for its new entry-level EVs. Orders for the ID Polo exceeded 30,000 by early September, while the Cupra Raval accumulated more than 51,000 reservations. The automaker said demand could lead to extended waiting times for some versions.

Automakers are also grappling with the financial consequences of the accelerating shift to BEVs.

During VW Group's third-quarter earnings call on Oct. 29, CFO Arno Antlitz said the company's BEV transition is occurring faster than anticipated. The group's order book now consists of about 40 percent BEVs, a level it had originally expected to reach only in 2029.

Till Conrad, head of marketing at Toyota Europe, said battery-electric vehicles still generate lower margins than hybrids and conventional vehicles, although falling battery costs should help close the gap.

This seems like a real "damned if you do, damned if you don't" situation. I'm not really sure what the solution is, because I'm a goofus, but I can certainly understand why automakers have fought so vehemently against the transition to EVs.

Mitsubishi cut its full-year profit forecast, saying the war in the Middle East is hurting sales and driving up costs. Now, the Japanese automaker says it expects its operating profit to decline 7.3% — a reversal from a previously predicted expansion. From Automotive News:

Mitsubishi's revision has operating profit declining to ¥70 billion ($444.8 million) in the current fiscal year ending March 31, from ¥75.5 billion ($479.8 million) the year before.

In August, the company predicted profit would grow 19 percent to ¥90 billion ($571.9 million).

Mitsubishi also downgraded its net income outlook to be flat with the previous year. It had earlier expected net income to more than double in the current fiscal year.

In announcing financial results, COO Keisuke Kishiura said Middle East disruption will dent full-year operating profit by ¥45 billion ($285.9 million), including lost sales and higher transportation and raw material costs. The reversal shows how the lingering conflict continues to affect auto industry costs and demand, while Mitsubishi lags rivals in hybrids and electric vehicles.

Mitsubishi dialed back its outlook despite announcing a 75 percent surge in operating profit and narrower net loss for the fiscal second quarter ended Sept. 30.

The automaker lowered its global sales forecast to 764,000 vehicles for its current fiscal year due to declining volume in Southeast Asia, Australia, North America and Europe.

The new guidance represents a 4.1 percent decline from the previous year.

Mitsubishi had earlier forecast global sales to increase 7.5 percent to 857,000 vehicles.

Mitsubishi expects North American deliveries to fall 7 percent to 154,000 vehicles, European sales to decline 5 percent to 40,000 and Southeast Asia to lose 12 percent to 216,000.

Mitsubishi sees Japan sales increasing 11 percent to 136,000 vehicles.

Mitsubishi is hoping the newly reintroduced Pajero will boost sales. The automaker has already taken 9,000 orders worldwide for its new off-road SUV, meant to take on the Toyota Land Cruiser. Its goal is to sell 10,000 of them by the end of the current fiscal year, which ends on March 31, 2027. It's already on sale in Thailand — where it's built — and will hit dealerships in Japan and Australia in December. There's still no word on whether or not it'll come Stateside, but we can keep our fingers crossed.

I do thank Rutgers for being the birthplace of college football, especially because it really hasn't worked out for the Scarlet Knights since then. Over 112 seasons of play, the team is 686–710. It also has 42 ties for good measure. Not exactly stellar, and this season, it's off to a 2-6 start. Ouch. It's not like my Penn State Nittany Lions have fared much better of late. Anyway, if you want to learn more about the first college football game, head over to History.com.

Friends, I've got some great news to cap off a pretty solid week, all things considered. Fuel prices are continuing their downward trajectory, and they're even picking up a little bit of steam on the way. Helping matters is the fact that WTI Crude Oil futures and Brent Crude prices are both down, sitting at $91 and $98, respectively, at the time of publication.

Here's where national average prices stand right now, according to AAA:

This all means the average price of a gallon of gas dropped another 3 cents overnight to $4.07, according to AAA. Diesel saw a similar 3-cent drop to $6.23. I'll give President Trump credit. He said fuel prices would be coming down after the midterms, and would ya look at what they're doing. I'm sure he doesn't much care at this point, though.

Everybody has to stop what they're doing, because Olivia Rodrigo just dropped a Christmas song. It's your job to listen right now, and no, I'm not interested in hearing you say "it's too early to listen to Christmas music." Keep that to yourself, Grinch.

A longtime Elon Musk backer is done with Tesla stock and scaling back on SpaceX

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Scottish Mortgage's SpaceX holding fell to 11.1% of assets from 15% at Sept. 30, as its net asset value returned 31.5% in six months

Scottish Mortgage Investment Trust exited Tesla $TSLA entirely in April and has trimmed its SpaceX holding to 11.1% of total assets, according to half-year results the London-listed, Baillie Gifford-managed fund published Friday.

SpaceX's weighting in the trust's total assets was 11.1% on Wednesday, versus 15% on Sept. 30. The trust's net asset value returned 31.5% over the half-year ended Sept. 30, while the FTSE All-World Index returned 16%. Its share price rose 33.8% on a total-return basis. SpaceX added 8 percentage points to the trust's return.

Proceeds from SpaceX sales went partly toward paying down the trust's debt. Net gearing, which gauges how much the trust borrows against its assets, dropped from 11.0% to 4.3%.

Scottish Mortgage first invested in Tesla in 2013. In a July portfolio update, the trust said its roughly $470 million stake in the company had produced about $6.2 billion of realized profit across 13 years.

“Since we first invested in 2013, it has returned 13 times the capital invested, but we felt the share price had come to reflect much of its promise in autonomy and robotics,” the trust said, according to CNBC. It also cited fiercer rivalry facing Tesla’s main car operations, above all in China.

Scottish Mortgage first bought into SpaceX in December 2018, at a reported valuation of roughly $31 billion, and pledged about $200 million over the period to 2021, according to CNBC. SpaceX made up about a quarter of the portfolio’s value at the time of its June public debut.

The trust has sold SpaceX stock as the share lockups imposed after the IPO have expired in phases. It added that its belief in the company is still strong. "The decision to reduce the position following its flotation, while retaining a substantial investment, demonstrates the importance of balancing conviction with disciplined portfolio management," the trust said, according to CNBC.

Shares of SpaceX slid roughly 9% on Thursday while Tesla's dropped about 11%, extending a selloff that followed Democratic gains in the midterm elections. The two companies' combined market capitalization was down by more than $420 billion from Tuesday's close.

Investors worry that congressional investigators may turn their attention to Musk once the midterms are over, which could leave his companies subject to stricter regulation, according to CNBC.

Stock market hours and futures trading hours: 10 timing answers

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

What time does the stock market open, and when do futures trade? 10 answers on U.S. stock hours, extended sessions, futures schedules, holidays and overseas hours

The opening bell at 9:30 a.m. Eastern is the best-known time on Wall Street, but it is not when trading begins. By the time the bell rings at the New York Stock Exchange and Nasdaq $NDAQ, prices have often been moving for hours. Some of that activity happens in extended sessions for stocks. Much of it happens in futures contracts that trade nearly 24 hours a day, five days a week.

That is why a simple question — what time does the stock market open — has more than one useful answer. The regular session for U.S. stocks runs from 9:30 a.m. to 4 p.m. Eastern, Monday through Friday. Pre-market and after-hours sessions stretch that window on either side. Each broker decides how much of that extra time its customers can reach. Stock index futures follow a separate clock. CME $CME Group's Globex platform opens the futures trading week on Sunday evening and closes it on Friday afternoon, with a short break each day.

The timing matters for practical reasons. Companies often release quarterly earnings after the closing bell. Economic data, central bank decisions and overseas events can also arrive while U.S. exchanges are closed. Trading conditions change by session, too. Fewer buyers and sellers are active outside regular hours. The gap between bid and ask prices is typically wider as a result.

The calendar adds another layer. U.S. exchanges close for a set list of holidays each year, and some sessions end early. Futures markets keep their own holiday schedules, which do not always match the stock exchanges. Time zones matter as well. A trader in California sees the opening bell at 6:30 a.m. local time. The gap between New York and London shifts for a few weeks each year because the U.S. and Europe change their clocks on different dates.

This guide covers 10 timing topics. It explains regular stock market hours, pre-market and after-hours trading, and the schedules for stock index, commodity and Treasury futures. It also explains why futures move before the open and how index futures expire. The final slides cover the days the market closes, how the open translates across U.S. time zones and when trading begins in London and other overseas markets. It is a selection, not a full directory of global exchanges. Each answer stands on its own. Readers can go straight to the schedule they need.

The regular trading session on the New York Stock Exchange and Nasdaq $NDAQ runs from 9:30 a.m. to 4 p.m. Eastern time, Monday through Friday. That window lasts six and a half hours. It is the schedule most people mean when they ask what time the stock market opens.

Both exchanges keep the same core hours, even though they operate in different ways. The NYSE combines a physical trading floor at 11 Wall Street in Manhattan with electronic trading. Nasdaq has no trading floor and runs entirely on electronic systems. A share of Apple $AAPL, listed on Nasdaq, and a share of Coca-Cola $KO, listed on the NYSE, both trade in the regular session on the same clock.

The trading day starts with an opening auction rather than an ordinary trade. The auction gathers buy and sell orders submitted ahead of time and matches them at a single price. Nasdaq calls this process the Opening Cross. On the NYSE, designated market makers oversee the opening auction for the stocks assigned to them. The result is an official opening price for each listed stock.

The session ends the same way. At 4 p.m., a closing auction pools the orders marked for the close and sets one closing price per stock. Nasdaq calls its version the Closing Cross. Traders who want to take part send market-on-close or limit-on-close orders before the auction runs.

Funds and index providers rely on those closing prices. U.S. mutual funds generally calculate their daily net asset value as of the 4 p.m. Eastern close. Index providers use the official closes to set end-of-day index levels. Many index funds trade in the closing auction to match the levels their benchmarks record.

The bells that mark the open and close are also public events. The NYSE rings an opening bell and a closing bell each trading day, often with executives or guests from listed companies on the podium. Nasdaq holds its own opening and closing ceremonies at its MarketSite studio in Times Square $SQ.

Trades that take place before 9:30 a.m. or after 4 p.m. fall outside the regular session. Prices from those hours do not count as the official open or close.

Pre-market trading lets investors buy and sell U.S. stocks before the regular 9:30 a.m. Eastern open. On major exchange venues, including Nasdaq $NDAQ and NYSE Arca, the session can begin as early as 4 a.m. Eastern.

That 4 a.m. start is the earliest the exchanges allow, not the time every investor can trade. Brokerage firms set their own pre-market windows, and many open later than the exchanges do. Some platforms do not offer pre-market access at all. A broker's website or trading platform lists the hours that apply to its accounts.

Pre-market orders are matched electronically rather than through the opening auction that starts the regular day. Prices can differ from one venue to another before the open. A single large order can move a stock further than it would after 9:30 a.m. A trade can also fill at a price some distance from the last quote.

Brokers limit order types for that reason. Most accept only limit orders before the open. With a limit order, the investor sets the highest price to pay or the lowest price to accept. Market orders, which fill at whatever price is available, are generally not permitted in the session. Some brokers also require an order to be marked specifically for extended hours. Their rules differ on whether an unfilled pre-market order carries into the regular session.

Scheduled economic data is one reason the early session gets attention. The Bureau of Labor Statistics releases its monthly jobs report and the consumer price index at 8:30 a.m. Eastern, an hour before the opening bell. Many companies also publish quarterly results before the market opens. Pre-market trading gives investors a way to act on that information before the regular session starts.

A stock's pre-market move is usually shown as a change from the previous day's 4 p.m. close. That figure rests on a smaller pool of trades than the regular session produces. The official opening price is set separately when the regular session begins at 9:30 a.m. Eastern. A stock can open at a different level from where it traded minutes earlier.

After-hours trading lets investors buy and sell U.S. stocks once the regular session ends at 4 p.m. Eastern. On major exchange venues, including Nasdaq $NDAQ and NYSE Arca, the extended session runs until 8 p.m. Eastern, though each broker sets its own access hours.

The session draws attention mainly because of when companies release news. Many publicly traded companies publish quarterly earnings shortly after the 4 p.m. close rather than during the trading day. That timing lets investors read the results before the next regular session begins. It also means the first market reaction to those results often plays out after hours.

An earnings release is commonly followed by a conference call with analysts, often held in the late afternoon or early evening. Remarks from executives on that call, including guidance about coming quarters, can push a stock's after-hours price further after the initial release.

Other corporate announcements also tend to arrive after the bell. These can include mergers, leadership changes and plans to sell new shares.

Price quotes need careful reading during this window. A stock's official closing price is set at the 4 p.m. close and does not change because of later trades. Financial sites and broker apps typically show after-hours moves as a separate figure next to that closing price. The next day's percentage change is measured from the official close, not from the last evening trade.

Benchmark indexes follow the regular session as well. The S&P 500 is calculated from trades during regular hours. An evening jump in one of its largest companies does not alter the index's closing level.

Access is the other practical question. Brokers that offer extended hours often require an order to be marked specifically for the extended session. The hours a broker supports may also end well before 8 p.m. Eastern. The broker's stated window and order rules determine what a customer can trade after the close.

Trading on Nasdaq and NYSE Arca then pauses until the pre-market session opens the following morning.

E-mini S&P 500 and E-mini Nasdaq $NDAQ-100 futures trade on CME $CME Group's Globex electronic platform nearly 24 hours a day, five days a week. The trading week opens Sunday at 6 p.m. Eastern and closes Friday at 5 p.m. Eastern.

From Monday through Thursday, trading pauses from 5 p.m. to 6 p.m. Eastern for a daily maintenance break. It then resumes for the next session. That leaves 23 hours of trading on a typical weekday.

The break also marks the boundary between trading days. A trade placed at 7 p.m. Eastern on a Tuesday counts toward Wednesday's session on CME's calendar. In the same way, the Sunday evening open is the start of Monday's trading day, not a separate weekend session.

CME Group is based in Chicago, and its contract pages list hours in Central time. In that format the week runs from Sunday at 5 p.m. to Friday at 4 p.m. Central. The daily pause runs from 4 p.m. to 5 p.m. Central. Both versions describe the same window.

The two contracts track different benchmarks. The E-mini S&P 500, which trades under the ticker ES, follows the S&P 500 index of large U.S. companies. The E-mini Nasdaq-100, ticker NQ, follows the 100 largest nonfinancial companies listed on Nasdaq. CME launched the E-mini S&P 500 in 1997 and the E-mini Nasdaq-100 in 1999.

Contract size is set by a multiplier on the index level. One E-mini S&P 500 contract is worth $50 times the index. One E-mini Nasdaq-100 contract is worth $20 times the index. In 2019, CME added Micro E-mini versions of both at one-tenth of those sizes. The micro contracts run on the same Globex schedule.

These hours stretch well beyond the 9:30 a.m. to 4 p.m. Eastern stock session. Index futures keep trading for one more hour after the stock market's 4 p.m. close, until the daily break begins. They reopen at 6 p.m. Eastern, well before pre-market stock trading begins on major exchange venues.

Holiday weeks follow a separate calendar published by CME Group. The hours above describe a standard trading week only.

Stock index futures keep trading after U.S. stock exchanges close, so their prices change overnight as traders respond to news. By the time the 9:30 a.m. Eastern bell rings, futures have often already absorbed hours of information that the regular stock session has not yet priced.

That gap is why morning market coverage leads with futures. Contracts such as the E-mini S&P 500 and E-mini Nasdaq $NDAQ-100 trade through the evening and night. The regular sessions of the New York Stock Exchange and Nasdaq are closed during those hours. A futures move before the open reflects trades by participants willing to act on events while the cash market waits.

Scheduled U.S. economic data offers a clear example. The Labor Department's monthly jobs report and the consumer price index are released at 8:30 a.m. Eastern. That is an hour before the opening bell. Futures trade straight through those releases, and their prices adjust to the numbers before the regular stock session begins.

A futures contract is priced against an index, but it is not the index itself. The S&P 500 is calculated from the prices of its component stocks. The futures contract is an agreement whose value at expiration is tied to that index level. Before expiration, the two prices can differ.

The expected difference between them is known as fair value. It accounts for two forces. Holding the stocks in the index ties up money that could otherwise earn interest. That cost pushes the futures price above the cash index. Owning the stocks also pays dividends that a futures holder does not receive. That pulls the futures price lower. When interest rates are higher than the index's dividend yield, futures typically trade above the cash index even when no news has moved prices.

A positive futures reading does not by itself signal a higher open. Pre-market commentary often compares futures with fair value rather than with the previous close. A futures price above fair value points to a higher opening for the index. A price below fair value points to a lower one.

The gap between futures and the cash index narrows as expiration approaches. With less time remaining, the interest and dividend effects shrink.

The indication is not a guarantee. The index's actual opening level comes from the first trades in its component stocks, and news that breaks just before 9:30 a.m. can change the picture. Futures show where traders are positioned overnight, not where the market must go once the bell rings.

E-mini S&P 500 and E-mini Nasdaq $NDAQ-100 futures expire four times a year, in March, June, September and December. They are cash settled, which means no shares change hands when a contract ends.

Each quarterly contract has a fixed expiration date: the third Friday of its contract month. A trader holding a June contract, for example, has a position tied to an index level set on the third Friday of June.

Final settlement uses the Special Opening Quotation of the index on the third Friday of the contract month. That figure is calculated from the opening prices of the stocks in the index on that Friday. It is not the index value displayed at 9:30 a.m. It is built stock by stock as each component opens. A stock that starts trading late still enters the calculation at its own opening price.

Trading in the expiring E-mini contract stops at 9:30 a.m. Eastern on the third Friday. After that, the contract's final value depends only on how the component stocks open.

Cash settlement avoids a practical problem. Physical delivery would require handing over every stock in the index in its exact weight. Each open position is instead marked to the final settlement price. The difference is then paid or collected in cash.

Traders who want to keep exposure past expiration do not wait for settlement. They close the expiring contract and open a position in the next quarterly contract. This step is known as rolling. It shifts trading activity from one contract month to the next in the days before expiration.

Futures tickers identify contract months with single letters. H stands for March, M for June, U for September and Z for December. A ticker such as ESZ, followed by a year digit, refers to a December E-mini S&P 500 contract.

The third Friday of each quarterly month also has a name in market slang. It is traditionally called quadruple witching. The label referred to four kinds of contracts expiring on the same day: stock index futures, stock index options, stock options and single-stock futures. Single-stock futures are no longer listed on the major U.S. exchange that offered them. The term triple witching is also widely used for the same quarterly date.

Many CME $CME Group futures, including WTI crude oil, gold and U.S. Treasury futures, trade on the Globex platform from Sunday at 6 p.m. to Friday at 5 p.m. Eastern. From Monday through Thursday, trading pauses for one hour each day, from 5 p.m. to 6 p.m. Eastern.

That schedule gives these markets 23 hours of trading in each session. The daily break marks the line between one trading session and the next. A trade placed on a Monday evening at 7 p.m. Eastern counts toward Tuesday's session, not Monday's.

The three markets sit on different CME Group exchanges. WTI crude oil futures are listed on the New York Mercantile Exchange, known as NYMEX. Gold futures are listed on COMEX. Treasury futures are listed on the Chicago Board of Trade. All three trade through the Globex electronic platform and share the same weekly window.

CME Group, which is based in Chicago, lists its trading hours in Central time. In that format, the week runs from 5 p.m. Sunday to 4 p.m. Friday Central. The daily break runs from 4 p.m. to 5 p.m. Central. Anyone comparing an exchange schedule with a broker's platform should confirm which time zone each one displays.

The long hours matter because the news that moves these contracts does not follow the New York stock clock. Oil prices respond to supply decisions by OPEC and its allies, which can be announced while U.S. exchanges are closed. Gold trades heavily in London and across Asia during the U.S. overnight hours. Treasury futures often react to U.S. economic data released at 8:30 a.m. Eastern, such as the monthly jobs report. That release lands an hour before stocks begin regular trading.

The general window does not cover every contract. Grain futures such as corn and soybeans trade shorter sessions, with a pause between the overnight and daytime segments. Individual contracts can also carry their own rules around expiring months and holidays. The contract specification page on CME Group's website lists the official hours for each product. It is the place to check before trading a specific contract.

The New York Stock Exchange and Nasdaq $NDAQ close for 10 full holidays a year: New Year's Day, Martin Luther King Jr. Day, Presidents Day, Good Friday, Memorial Day, Juneteenth, Independence Day, Labor Day, Thanksgiving Day and Christmas Day. Neither exchange holds a regular session on those days.

Most of the stock market calendar follows federal holidays, but the two lists are not identical. Good Friday is not a federal holiday, yet both exchanges close for it. Columbus Day and Veterans Day work the opposite way. Federal offices and the Federal Reserve close on those dates, while stock trading runs on its normal schedule.

Juneteenth is the newest entry. It became a federal holiday in 2021, and the stock exchanges first closed for it in 2022.

Weekends change the closure dates. When a holiday falls on a Saturday, the exchanges generally close on the Friday before. When it falls on a Sunday, they close on the Monday after. New Year's Day is the usual exception to the Saturday rule. If January 1 lands on a Saturday, trading typically goes ahead on Friday, December 31, as a normal session.

A few sessions end early instead of being canceled. On those days, the regular session closes at 1 p.m. Eastern rather than 4 p.m. The day after Thanksgiving is the most consistent early close. In some years, July 3 and Christmas Eve also end at 1 p.m. Those two dates close early only in certain years, depending on where they fall in the week.

The bond market keeps a separate calendar. SIFMA, the trade association for U.S. securities firms, publishes recommended full and early closes for bond trading. That schedule does not always match the stock exchanges. SIFMA typically recommends a full bond market close on Columbus Day and Veterans Day, for example, while stocks trade normally.

Futures follow yet another schedule. CME $CME Group publishes its own holiday hours for products on its Globex platform. Those hours differ from the stock exchange calendar. On some days when NYSE and Nasdaq are shut, certain futures contracts still trade a shortened session. The exact hours vary by product and by holiday.

The specific dates shift every year. The official holiday calendars posted by NYSE, Nasdaq and CME Group are the reference to check before planning trades around a long weekend.

The U.S. stock market's 9:30 a.m. Eastern open is 8:30 a.m. Central, 7:30 a.m. Mountain and 6:30 a.m. Pacific. Those conversions hold year-round in most of the country. The Eastern, Central, Mountain and Pacific zones all change their clocks on the same dates.

The New York Stock Exchange and Nasdaq $NDAQ set their schedules in New York local time. The opening bell rings at 9:30 a.m. on a Manhattan clock in January, when New York is on standard time. It also rings at 9:30 a.m. in July, when the city is on daylight time. A trader in Chicago, Denver or Los Angeles sees the same one-, two- or three-hour offset in every season.

The closing bell converts the same way. The 4 p.m. Eastern close is 3 p.m. Central, 2 p.m. Mountain and 1 p.m. Pacific. On the West Coast, the regular session runs from early morning to early afternoon.

Alaska sits one hour behind Pacific time and observes daylight saving time on the U.S. schedule. The open arrives at 5:30 a.m. in Anchorage throughout the year.

Two states break the pattern. Most of Arizona stays on Mountain Standard Time all year and does not observe daylight saving time. From March to November, Phoenix shares a clock with Los Angeles. The open arrives there at 6:30 a.m. In winter it returns to 7:30 a.m.

Hawaii also skips daylight saving time. The open lands at 3:30 a.m. in Honolulu while the mainland is on daylight time. It lands at 4:30 a.m. while the mainland is on standard time.

Extended sessions shift by the same offsets as the regular session. On major exchange venues, the post-close session runs until 8 p.m. Eastern, which is 5 p.m. Pacific. Brokers set their own extended-hours windows. Anyone trading outside regular hours from another time zone should check the broker's schedule in local time.

The U.S. moves its clocks forward one hour on the second Sunday in March. It moves them back one hour on the first Sunday in November. Many other countries change their clocks on different dates, and some never change them at all.

Britain and the European Union move their clocks on the last Sunday in March and the last Sunday in October. Those dates do not line up with the U.S. calendar. For a few weeks each spring and autumn, the gap between New York and London differs from its usual size.

During those weeks, the habitual offset is not a reliable guide. A reader tracking the London close or European economic releases should check a current time conversion before acting.

The London Stock Exchange's main trading session runs from 8 a.m. to 4:30 p.m. London time on weekdays. The Tokyo Stock Exchange trades from 9 a.m. to 3:30 p.m. Japan time, with a midday break.

London's session lasts eight and a half hours, two hours longer than the regular session on Wall Street. London uses Greenwich Mean Time in winter and British Summer Time in summer. For most of the year, that puts it five hours ahead of U.S. Eastern time. On that clock, the London open falls at 3 a.m. Eastern and the close lands at 11:30 a.m. Eastern.

That timing leaves a two-hour stretch, from 9:30 a.m. to 11:30 a.m. Eastern, when stocks trade in London and New York at the same time.

Tokyo splits its trading day into two sessions. The morning session runs from 9 a.m. to 11:30 a.m., and the afternoon session runs from 12:30 p.m. to 3:30 p.m. The exchange moved its close from 3 p.m. to 3:30 p.m. in November 2024. That change lengthened the afternoon session by 30 minutes.

Japan does not observe daylight saving time. Japan Standard Time sits nine hours ahead of Coordinated Universal Time all year. During U.S. standard time, Tokyo's open arrives at 7 p.m. Eastern the previous evening. During U.S. daylight saving time, it arrives at 8 p.m. Eastern. The Tokyo close falls at 1:30 a.m. Eastern under standard time and 2:30 a.m. Eastern under daylight saving time.

Those conversions show how the trading day passes from Asia to Europe to North America. Tokyo's session ends before London opens. A U.S. investor checking prices at breakfast is often seeing the result of a finished session in Asia and one still under way in Europe.

Neither exchange follows the U.S. holiday calendar. London closes for U.K. bank holidays. Tokyo closes for Japanese national holidays and a New Year break. Each exchange publishes its own trading calendar, which is the place to confirm hours for a specific date. Exchanges in other cities set their own sessions and midday breaks. Their official calendars are the reference for those markets.

OPEC crude oil output is nearing pre-war levels as Saudi Arabia rebounds

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

OPEC production rose 930,000 barrels a day in October to 22.8 million, about 88% of February levels, a Bloomberg survey shows

Andrey Rudakov / Bloomberg via Getty Images

OPEC pumped 22.8 million barrels of crude a day in October, a gain of 930,000 barrels a day that leaves output at about 88% of its February level, according to a Bloomberg survey.

Saudi Arabia was responsible for the bulk of the increase, according to Bloomberg. The kingdom boosted output by 970,000 barrels a day to 9.2 million, leaving it roughly 800,000 barrels daily short of the pace it held when the year began. Saudi oil exports ran at an average of 7 million barrels daily in October, according to tanker-tracking data compiled by Bloomberg. Bloomberg attributed the gain to Saudi Arabia routing a larger number of tankers via the Strait of Hormuz and loading cargoes along its Red Sea coast, despite hostilities connected to the U.S.-Iran conflict.

Iraq added 140,000 barrels a day to reach 4.15 million, and Kuwait added 130,000 to reach 2 million, according to Bloomberg. Those gains were canceled out by lower output in Nigeria and Iran.

Iran's output dropped 120,000 barrels daily to 1.9 million, the weakest reading in Bloomberg's monthly records going back to 1989. Bloomberg said satellite imagery has not captured a single tanker at Iranian export terminals, Kharg Island among them, since late August, with the U.S. blockade of Iran's ports still in force.

Brent crude traded near $98 a barrel this week.

Saudi Aramco's CEO, Amin Nasser, said at an Abu Dhabi industry conference earlier in the week that the firm intends to "maximize production going forward in the fourth quarter," according to Bloomberg.

Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, Iraq and Kuwait exported a combined 15.53 million barrels of crude a day in October, a 10% increase that put volumes in line with levels at the start of the year. Kuwait's shipments of 1.44 million barrels a day were the highest since before the war began. Iran's exports stayed at zero for a second month.

JPMorgan $JPM estimates Middle East oil exports now stand near 20.7 million barrels a day, up from 9.8 million in March, although they have yet to return to where they were before the conflict erupted in late February, according to BOE Report.

OPEC and its allies are examining the volumes each member is technically capable of producing, as groundwork for 2027 output targets to be set at a ministerial meeting on Nov. 29, according to Bloomberg.

New Laws Could Add an Estimated 103,000 Homes a Year, Study Finds

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

New state laws to encourage homebuilding could bolster the nation's home production by six figures in coming years—but there’s a small catch.

The American Enterprise Institute tracked 10 state bills passed this year and estimates they could potentially lead to 103,000 new homes a year across the country. It's positive progress to solving the nation's housing supply shortage, which is complicated by red tape.

AEI’s data, though, shows that about half of those housing units are accessory dwelling units, or ADUs. But dozens more bills and legislative efforts put forth this year could bring more progress, AEI said at a housing conference in Washinton, DC, this week.

This has been the “year of the starter home,” said Ed Pinto, co-director of AEI’s Housing Center. Thank the bipartisan 21st Century Road to Housing Act, the largest housing reform package in decades. Americans are broadly concerned about affordability, and many young people have grown pessimistic that owning a home will be attainable.

The two largest contributors are California's AB 956 and Washington state's SB 6026.

California's AB 956 loosens requirements around building ADUs. AEI thinks that could result in 48,000 new units a year. Behind that is Washington state's SB 6026, which allows homes in commercially zoned areas. AEI thinks it could mean 37,000 new homes a year.

Other big contributions come from Idaho's SB 1352, which allows homes on smaller lots and could mean 5,000 new homes a year. Kansas' SB 418 brings similar potential in that state. Maine, Virginia, and North Carolina also passed new pro-housing laws.

Tobias Peter, co-director of AEI’s housing center, noted that doesn't necessarily mean every new home will come to fruition. Other kinds of reforms could also unlock hundreds of thousands more homes a year.

At the same time, AEI released a study that found broad support for changes to housing policy. Two rounds of polls of about 5,000 people this year found about 83% support a state law allowing more affordably priced starter homes on smaller lots in new neighborhoods.

AEI found 61% said they’d rather see a torn-down home replaced with two smaller homes, compared to a larger and more expensive home. But people’s opinions trading housing development versus individual property rights can be complicated.

Two of the most popular pushbacks on housing reform fit into two categories. “Supply skeptics” don’t think more homes will improve affordability. Others oppose change to the character or scale of established neighborhoods. Bur rather than housing reform being a "YIMBY versus NIMBY" debate, AEI pictures a spectrum of reform supporters and skeptics.

Some of the largest efforts have focused on the legal red tape around where homes can be built. For instance, several states have put forth laws to allow homes on smaller lots.

But more legislation is needed to correct the slowing construction of starter homes in the nation. Freddie Mac found the U.S. built an average of 418,000 new starter homes a year in the 1970s, 34% of all new housing starts. By 2020 that fell to 65,000 starts, or less than 10%.

“You really need a local champion, a quarterback who organizes at the state level,” Peter said, noting some successful bills where the governor, lieutenant governor, or a group of legislators shepherd a bill. “And it’s also important you have the evidence in your back pocket to counter some of the narratives."

Affordability also requires local governments to respond—because zoning and building code reform, by and large, happens at the local level.

Local government controls some of the most significant levers of affordability, says Yadira Ramos-Herbert, mayor of New Rochelle, NY. And that city's updates to its zoning code have meant incremental progress over time.

New Rochelle developed a form-based code that allows developers to build with fewer requirements around building use. It also streamlined its approval process for new developments, with some taking as few as 90 days to achieve approvals.

The program has helped it approve 4,500 new homes since 2020. New Rochelle, a winner of the University of Utah's 2026 Ivory Prize for Housing Affordability, has seen its median rent decline as national averages surge.

"Our core objective is to ensure New Rochelle remains affordable, accessible, and economically resilient," Ramos-Herbert tells Realtor.com. "Affordability remains the defining challenge of our time, and cities have a responsibility to respond with urgency."

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

Good Riddance: The Worst Airport Lounge In The United States Closes

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Swissport Lounge at Chicago O’Hare Airport (ORD) Terminal 5 has just quietly closed. I’m about as sad as I’ll be on the day when Delta retires its latest Boeing 767-300ER…

Historically, Chicago O’Hare Terminal 5 has been a pretty lousy facility, though it has started to get better in recent years, thanks to an expansion to the terminal, and also thanks to the opening of new lounges.

Arguably the single most disappointing thing about Terminal 5 was the Swissport Lounge, which I’ve labeled the worst lounge in the United States — clearly many people agreed with me, since my review of the lounge had over 200 comments. It’s just truly one of those facilities that makes you wonder why we even bother using lounges.

The lounge had no windows, the most random collection of furniture, and a very limited selection of drinks and snacks. Unless you wanted to just guzzle alcohol, you’d be better off sitting in the terminal, in my opinion. Unfortunately for all too long, many premium airlines sent their customers here, given the lack of other lounge options. It’s quite underwhelming to be flying business class on a carrier like Etihad Airways or Qatar Airways, and then be sent here.

That brings us to the interesting update — the Swissport Lounge Chicago has closed as of October 31, 2026, so passengers are no longer being subjected to this facility.

It remains to be seen what happens with this lounge space, as that hasn’t been announced one way or another. In April 2025, Swissport’s head of North America lounges said that the company had “submitted a proposal to the Chicago Department of Aviation (CDA) for a multi-million-dollar renovation and expansion of our current Terminal 5 lounge at O’Hare International Airport.” However, I haven’t been able to find any updates on that, so I’m not sure what the latest is there.

It’s worth acknowledging just how much better the lounge situation at Chicago O’Hare Terminal 5 has gotten in recent years:

If we get another lounge at Terminal 5, that would of course be awesome, so I’m curious to see how this evolves. Chicago O’Hare is the biggest airport in the country to not have any sort of a credit card branded lounge, so I hope that eventually changes. The issue is the extent to which the terminals are dominated by their primary tenants (American and United), combined with the lack of airside connections to Terminal 5.

The Swissport Lounge Chicago O’Hare Terminal 5 has suddenly closed. I’m not sad to see the United States’ worst airport lounge close, though I am curious to see what is next for this space. Fortunately the Terminal 5 lounge situation has improved nicely in recent years, and there are now several solid new options. I do wish we’d still see a proper credit card lounge, though.

What do you make of the Swissport Lounge Chicago closing? Are you happy or elated?

Head Of GTA 6 Owner Reveals His Advice To Be Healthy, Fit, And Live A Long Time

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strauss Zelnick, the head of Rockstar Games owner Take-Two, is known for his dedication to health and fitness. In 2018, he published a book titled Becoming Ageless: The Four Secrets To Looking and Feeling Younger Than Ever, and images of the executive looking shredded and lean at 8% body fat have circulated online. Zelnick has now spoken more about his health and fitness lifestyle, and offered some advice for people who want to get in shape and feel better.

Zelnick started off by telling Business Insider that he's good friends with Bryan Johnson, the billionaire who is trying to live forever, but Zelnick doesn't subscribe to the kinds of things Johnson is trying to do in terms of longevity in that regard.

Instead, Zelnick said he got some advice from his former doctor, the celebrity longevity doctor Peter Attia who appeared in the Epstein files, who told him he could help him with his healthspan but not his lifespan, and Zelnick was into that.

In 2016, Zelnick filmed a video for Business Insider where he showed off his workout routine--you can see it below.

Zelnick said his No. 1 piece of advice for anyone trying to improve their health is to avoid smoking altogether. "Smoking is the factor most highly correlated with a short, unhealthy, and frankly unhappy life. Don't smoke." Second, Zelnick said people should not abuse alcohol and drugs. "Those are the two most highly correlated factors with a bad old age, or no old age," he said.

After that, Zelnick said it's a good idea to get "some exercise," and that could be walking for 30 minutes four or five days a week. Zelnick said he goes way beyond this because health and fitness are his hobbies "but you really don't need to do that." Beyond exercise, Zelnick said he recommends that people eat a "responsible" diet, but it doesn't have to be "insanely healthy." The best practice for food, Zelnick said, is to simply not overdo it on unhealthy foods.

Another part of increasing your healthspan is getting enough sleep, and that should be about seven hours each night, Zelnick said. "If you're getting less than six hours of sleep a night, you're making bad decisions; that's what the math says," he said. "You are going to make bad personal decisions and bad professional decisions."

Having some kind of "emotional or spiritual life" is important, too, Zelnick said. "You have to have people you love and love you in your life, and preferably, you have to have a community around you," he said.

Also in the interview, Zelnick pushed back against taking peptides, which are popular in some fitness circles, but are largely unregulated.

"No. We have an FDA for a reason," he said. "Don't take something that is not double-blind tested. Even if it worked, do the stuff I do, forget the peptides, and you'll be in a better place."

Lastly, Zelnick said if people want to lose weight, they should consider taking some kind of GLP-1.

"Don't be grossly overweight. But in the world of GLP-1s, you have no reason to be," he said. "I am a huge advocate [for GLP-1s]. And no, there is absolutely no shame in taking them, whatsoever. If you're carrying extra weight, take a GLP-1. I happen never to have struggled with my weight, ever; I am blessed that way."

"I have this workout group, and I've had people show up who are heavyset, because we welcome everyone. So one guy shows up and he comes over and he says, 'I want you to talk to me about your diet, because I want to lost weight. I want to lose 50 pounds.' So I said, 'See, there are these things called GLP-1s. Go to my endocrinologist and get a prescription.' He said, 'Really? What about diet?' I said, 'That is literally like saying to me, I have a migraine, and I would say to you, forget about Tylenol, just meditate.' You take prescribed medications to help you live a healthier life. Of course."

Dallas Wants More Gates At Love Field—And To Give Southwest Airlines A Bigger Monopoly

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Dallas is opening the door to more gates at Love Field airport. Two things stand in the way. Federal law caps the airport at 20 gates, and Dallas has already negotiated a lease that would offer most of the additional gates to Southwest Airlines if the law were to change. Southwest already controls nearly all of the gates there. More room for adding Southwest flights is different than more competition for Southwest. Dallas needs a larger airport and to open itself to competition. The Dallas City Manager raised the prospect of additional gates at an aviation event last week. The airport is undergoing a $2.5 billion redevelopment that doesn’t include more gates. Former American Airlines Chairman and CEO Doug Parker defended the existing arrangement on this week’s Airlines Confidential podcast. His argument rests on the existing five-party agreement, federal law and investments made in reliance on both: “agreements are agreements.” That’s only why changing the rules will be difficult, not a reason to keep limits on competition forever. Why Dallas Can’t Just Build More Love Field Gates Southwest Airlines had to fight to operate from Dallas Love Field when it first launched. It flew its first flight in 1971 after being tied up in the courts. Existing airlines agreed to leave Love Field for DFW airport. Southwest wasn’t a party to that agreement. In 1979, the Wright Amendment was passed to limit competition from Southwest Airlines. Deregulation meant that Southwest could fy beyond the state of Texas, and other carriers feared that the close-in airport they operated from would be more desirable than DFW. The upstart Southwest that fought for the right to fly was mature and pivoted to using government to its own advantage by the time they succeeded in securing the Wright Amendment Reform Act of 2006. It was a compromise among Dallas, Fort Worth, DFW Airport, American Airlines and Southwest Airlines. Southwest gained the ability to sell connecting service beyond the old geographic limits, followed by nationwide domestic nonstop service in October 2014. Love Field’s gate count had to be reduced from 32 to 20. Southwest kept most of the gates. The airport couldn’t offer nonstop international passenger service. Basically Southwest got more flying from Love Field and a monopoly there, while American Airlines got a monopoly on international service from Dallas and a cap on how much service could compete from Love Field. Initially, Southwest effectively couldn’t even fly out of Dallas – Fort Worth offering direct competition with American. The five party agreement specified that any gate Southwest operates at DFW (or another airport within 80 miles) would require giving up a Love Field gate, up to 8 total gates. That restriction ended in 2025. The provision even covered affiliates and flights carrying Southwest’s code, with exceptions for existing DFW–Midway codeshare service. Southwest couldn’t even codeshare with partners who flew to DFW, and could set up a regional affiliate to operate there. Dallas Already Gate Southwest An Option On More Love Field Gates The city council approved Southwest’s October 1, 2028 – September 30, 2040 Love Field lease covering 18 of the existing 20 gates. One gate is under Delta’s control and the final gate is under city control and can (theoretically) be used for new service. Delta Air Lines Gate At Dallas Love Field Delta Air Lines Gate At Dallas Love Field The lease anticipates what happens with a change in federal law: Southwest and the airport both agree not to oppose an increase in gates at the airport. Dallas can then seek or support the effort. If the law changes, Dallas can pursue four, eight or twelve additional gates without the usual review by major incument airlines (‘majority-in-interest’ airline review, effectively Southwest) as long as each new gate won’t cost more than $50 million in 2025 dollars. Southwest receives an option on three of four, five of eight, or eight of twelve new gates. Even after changing a law that protects incumbents, Dallas would offer most new gates to its dominant airline. Parker Explains Why American Would Fight This Parker attributed two percentage points of Delta’s margin advantage, in an internal analysis from his American tenure, to Atlanta’s lack of a competing airport like Love Field or Midway. American has a lot to lose if some Dallas travelers gain better alternatives to DFW. Parker acknowledges his bias. American has made enormous investments at DFW, and predictable rules matter when financing airports. But I’d note: The agreement is more than 20 years old. Investors who bet on government keeping out competition also take the risk that the government changes its mind. Twenty years of protection is a substantial benefit, not a guarantee of protection in perpetuity. American hsa benefited from other subsidies, too. Parker was a prime mover behind American’s receipt of $12.8 billion in pandemic payroll support, the vast majority of the second and third tranches displacing payroll the airline was going to pay anyway. American chose not to fly from Love Field. American ended service in 2009 and subleased its gates to Delta. Its later merger settlement required divestiture of the gates, but American had already stopped operating. American preferred concentrating its network at DFW but that shouldn’t give them a permanent veto over competitors serving a different airport. The Government Destroyed Gates To Make This Deal Work Parker correctly recalls the demolition of six gates at the former Legend Airlines terminal. The overall reduction was twelve gates from 32 to 20. Six of those were at the separate Lemmon Avenue facility. I covered the litigation over that terminal and its leases. The Court of Federal Claims awarded the owners $133.5 million when the city took it back through eminent domain. The Federal Circuit reversed in 2018 arguing that the fact the gates couldn’t legally be used meant it had no value. It might soon have had value. The terminal was turned into a Lincoln dealership. North Texas Has Outgrown This Bargain DFW is growing. Its new Terminal F is planned for 31 gates, all serving American, with the first 15 expected to open in 2027. Meanwhile, McKinney is preparing to launch scheduled commercial service on November 11. Different parts of the Metroplex support different airport choices. DFW, Love Field and McKinney aren’t interchangeable for a passenger near downtown Dallas. Noise and road access are real though anyone living near the airport knew there was an airport there. Additional gate construction would necessarily involve an operational, environmental and access plan. American Airlines certainly didn’t walk away from the Naples, Florida airport just because residents there don’t like commercial air service! Love Field should be allowed to grow. The additional preferential gates should be open to airlines that bring new competition, rather than committing ex ante to enlarging Southwest’s existing control. These lease commitment is an impediment to that, and actually gives American Airlines its strongest argument against expansion. Southwest built its business by fighting attempts to keep it out. American chose to leave Love Field. Neither of those things justifies keeping other competitors out of the Dallas market, or serving each of the local markets in North Texas separately. North Texas Has Outgrown This Bargain DFW is growing. Its new Terminal F is planned for 31 gates, all serving American, with the first 15 expected to open in 2027. Meanwhile, McKinney is preparing to launch scheduled commercial service on November 11. Different parts of the Metroplex support different airport choices. DFW, Love Field and McKinney aren’t interchangeable for a passenger near downtown Dallas. Noise and road access are real though anyone living near the airport knew there was an airport there. Additional gate construction would necessarily involve an operational, environmental and access plan. American Airlines certainly didn’t walk away from the Naples, Florida airport just because residents there don’t like commercial air service! Love Field should be allowed to grow. The additional preferential gates should be open to airlines that bring new competition, rather than committing ex ante to enlarging Southwest’s existing control. These lease commitment is an impediment to that, and actually gives American Airlines its strongest argument against expansion. Southwest built its business by fighting attempts to keep it out. American chose to leave Love Field. Neither of those things justifies keeping other competitors out of the Dallas market, or serving each of the local markets in North Texas separately. Parker correctly recalls the demolition of six gates at the former Legend Airlines terminal. The overall reduction was twelve gates from 32 to 20. Six of those were at the separate Lemmon Avenue facility.

I covered the litigation over that terminal and its leases. The Court of Federal Claims awarded the owners $133.5 million when the city took it back through eminent domain. The Federal Circuit reversed in 2018 arguing that the fact the gates couldn’t legally be used meant it had no value. It might soon have had value. The terminal was turned into a Lincoln dealership.

We Write About Saving Money; Here’s How We Trick Our Brains Into Doing It

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Even personal finance writers and editors need psychological hacks to save money. Here are the tips we use, and why they work.

While staring at the Container Store cart on my phone at 11 p.m., I did something for the first time that I’d read about a hundred times. Instead of spending $200 on more organizational boxes that I categorically did not need, I transferred that amount to my savings account.

Normally, my willpower is no match for the very real psychological phenomenon called present bias: Our brains light up more when we’re rewarding our current selves than our future selves.

Research has also shown that you imagine your future self almost like a stranger — it’s tougher to care about that stranger than about who you are right now. But that night, I saw near-future-me using that $200 on next summer’s vacation. I wanted the vacation more than the boxes. So I abandoned my cart.

I had managed to use a psychological savings trick: Find a goal that helps you envision your future self. With this epiphany in mind, I asked some of my fellow Nerds to share the savings habits they’ve put to work in their own lives. Perhaps reading these can give late-night you some saving inspiration.

Kimberly Palmer, senior writer and spokesperson: I let nonessential items sit in my online shopping cart for at least 24 hours before buying them. It definitely makes me realize I don't need a lot of them! I also informed my children of this rule, and it has helped cut down on their requests — or at least they know they have to be patient.

Karen Gaudette Brewer, head of content: Fellow let-it-sit-in-the-cart person here. I wait for notifications that something's at least 30% off before I make any moves. Ideally, 40% to 50% off.

Julie Myhre-Nunes, managing editor: When I browse online, I add an item to my shopping cart and wait 24 to 48 hours. After that, I either decide it’s a waste of money/an impulse purchase and delete it from my cart (or forget about the cart altogether), or I decide I want it and make the purchase — it’s the former 99% of the time.

Why it works: “Waiting 1 to 2 days allows me to take the emotion out of purchasing. I no longer succumb to marketing or sales, and instead make spending decisions based on what my family and I actually need or can use.” — Julie again, eloquently making the point for me.

Caitlin Constantine, managing editor: My one real shopping weakness is books, and I’ve found I can get a very close approximation of that feeling without spending an outrageous amount of money by going to the library, wandering around the stacks to see what books might look interesting, and checking out a bunch of them. Even if I don’t end up reading them, I’m still helping the library with circulation numbers, so I see it as a win-win.

Spencer Tierney, senior writer: Plus one to Caitlin Constantine’s “my one real shopping weakness is books.” If I want an older or smaller book, I'll try to find it at one of four libraries I belong to on Libby, the library e-book reader app. (The Libby waitlist for new books can take months, so I have to account for that.)

Why it works: There’s no secret here: It’s nice to get free stuff. You might be surprised what the library has to offer, like movies, magazine subscriptions, games, classes and theater tickets.

Pamela de la Fuente, managing editor: We always tell people to automate savings, and I do in my high-yield savings account, but Dasha Kennedy, the woman behind “The Broke Black Girl”, told Elizabeth Ayoola on the Smart Money podcast that you should invest, even a little bit every week or so, for future you. So I just set up a small recurring deposit to my robo-advisor.

Why it works: You only have to make this decision once for your future self, not over and over again. And if you do it early enough, you could avoid being part of the 41% of Americans who wish they had started investing earlier, according to a 2026 NerdWallet survey.

Athena Cocoves, managing editor: To cut down on food delivery, I started cooking when I feel like it instead of when I’m supposed to. I exercise free will and make soup at 10 p.m. It sounds more obvious than it feels, and it's made a big difference in my budget. And it'd make an even bigger difference if I wasn't also rewarding myself with fancy spices and vinegars, but a girl's gotta splurge somehow.

Ryan Brady, lead writer: My wife takes advantage of a T-Mobile freebie that gives us a discount for DoorDash deliveries. She uses it to buy groceries from Aldi instead of takeout. Aldi is far from us. Publix is right across the street, but more expensive. So this saves us money on the actual items and also helps us not impulse buy (as we often do when we're in-store shopping).

Why it works: You don’t need to call on willpower if you can avoid the situation that tempts you to spend money.

» Read more: Strategies to save on groceries that actually work

Kenley Young, managing editor: I use credit cards for most purchases to get those sweet, sweet rewards, but there's a lot in this article that I've also taken to heart.

I've started using library resources more, and it's saving me money on things like monthly subscriptions.

I've negotiated down several monthly bills, too: refinanced my car loan, got cheaper homeowners insurance, opted for a little less auto coverage in exchange for a lower premium, and haggled online with everyone from Xfinity and SiriusXM to my local newspaper until they all gave me a discount.

Plus, I've opted for some home improvement projects with the expectation that my utility bills would be lower. A plumber finally fixed our toilet, which has led to lower water bills, and I even installed solar panels.

Lastly, my analog skill (playing live music) has become a steady side hustle. I play eight to 10 gigs a month, where (on top of my baseline rate) I also get tips, free food and beer.

Why it works: Because you’re Kenley and you put in a lot of effort.

But you don’t need to be Kenley to find a savings habit that works for your personality, your financial situation and even your musical talents. You just need to experiment. Pick the strategy that speaks to you most, and test it out. Which one are you going to try first?
