7 Of The Longest Nonstop Boeing 787-9 Flights In 2026

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In the past, the Boeing 747 was the world's longest-range aircraft. Initially, it was the 747-200 and the 747SP that operated the world's longest flights, while the 747-400 opened up the world even more with its range of 7,285 nautical miles (13,490 km). The challenge, however, is that these were huge aircraft that could seat upwards of 400 passengers and consumed massive amounts of fuel.

This only works if an airline can earn significant revenue, and that can be challenging on the world's longest flights. The Boeing 787-9 Dreamliner can fly past 7,800 nautical miles (14,400 km) with a reasonable payload, and unlike the double-decker 747, it's not a 400-seater plane.

It's a 220 to 300-seat aircraft, while its twinjet layout and advanced engines mean that it sips fuel in comparison to the ultra-long-range airliners of the past. It's not the longest-range airliner in service today: that would be the Airbus A350. However, the 787-9 can fly nearly as far, and it burns less fuel, making it extremely attractive.

On June 1, 2016, United Airlines began flying its 787-9 nonstop between San Francisco International Airport (SFO) and Singapore Changi Airport (SIN), ending a nearly three-year period during which the US and Singapore were not connected by nonstop flights. The airline later added a second service from Los Angeles International Airport (LAX), but it later canceled this route and added a second frequency from SFO, a schedule which has persisted to this day.

This is not United's longest route overall (as we will discuss below), but it is the airline's longest year-round service, as well as its longest to Asia. With two daily flights, this is one of United's busier Asia routes, and the nighttime departure from SFO carries the prestigious 'UA1' flight number.

United's standard layout for the 787-9 features 257 seats, but the airline introduced a new, premium-heavy layout earlier in 2026 with 64 Polaris Suites, 35 Premium Plus seats, and just 222 seats in total. SFO-SIN was the first international route to receive the new layout, and United now uses this subfleet on both of its daily Singapore services.

Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport (SYD) is the primary hub for Qantas, and as a oneworld member, Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport (DFW) is an immensely lucrative destination because it's the primary hub for American Airlines. What's more, the two carriers also operate a metal-neutral joint venture, meaning that they essentially act as one entity on US-Australia services.

Of course, this route is incredibly long as well, which requires an aircraft with true ultra-long-haul capabilities. Qantas is currently using the Airbus A380 daily on this route, but it also scheduled the 787-9 to DFW earlier in 2026. These two aircraft have very different strengths; the 787-9 burns far less fuel than the A380, but Qantas's double-deckers can earn significantly more revenue.

When serving a partner's hub like DFW, there's strong demand to fill all 485 seats. Currently, Qantas has not scheduled the 787-9 on the SYD-DFW route for the foreseeable future, suggesting that the A380 is working for the airline. Generally, the A380 has been criticized for being too big and too fuel-thirsty, but it still earns immense amounts of revenue when the seats are occupied. Given that this route connects Qantas's largest hub to its largest partner hub, this isn't too surprising.

An aircraft that has proven its capability across all corners of the globe for a powerhouse of aviation. Here is where United operates the Dreamliner.

Launching in 2018, United Airlines' nonstop flight from Houston George Bush Intercontinental Airport (IAH) to SYD was, and remains, the longest nonstop route operated by a US airline. The carrier operates this route daily from late October to March using the 787-9, and United now uses its new 222-seat version of the aircraft.

Compared to the new layout, United's prior configuration for the 787-9 features 48 Polaris seats, or about 16 fewer than on the latest models. In addition, the Premium Plus cabin is only sized at 21 seats versus 35 on the newest examples, while the economy cabin is larger by 65 seats.

This is still the baseline configuration for United's 787-9, as only around 33 aircraft will receive the new layout. These planes are exceptionally premium-heavy, which makes them attractive for ultra-long-haul services like the Sydney route.

In addition to the new premium-heavy layout, the 222-seat planes feature the new United 'Elevated Interior'. This includes new business class suites with doors based on the Elevate Ascent, as well as updated versions of the Collins Aerospace MiQ and Aspire seats in Premium Plus and economy. In addition, the technology has been significantly updated.

In September 2022, Air New Zealand launched nonstop flights between Auckland Airport (AKL) and New York John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK). Carrying the prestigious 'NZ1' flight number, this route operates three times per week using Air New Zealand's 272-seat 787-9. This is the longest nonstop service that Air New Zealand operates, and it serves as the company's flagship operation.

The following year, however, Qantas joined in, launching a new route between AKL and JFK, with the aircraft and flight number originating in SYD. Notably, this route has been successful for Qantas, which has boosted frequencies with its 236-seat 787-9 on this route. Air New Zealand has reportedly been losing money on this route as it's operating fewer flights, has access to less feed, and a less premium configuration.

Air New Zealand is now taking delivery of new 787-9s with a premium-heavy layout featuring 42 business class seats, 52 premium economy seats, and just 219 seats in total. This should improve Air New Zealand's operating economics on this route, but whether Air New Zealand can overcome its other challenges remains to be seen.

In recent years, Qantas has transformed its operations at Perth Airport (PER) into a connecting hub for nonstop flights to Europe. It started with nonstop 787 services to London in 2018, followed by seasonal flights to Rome in 2022. In 2024, the carrier launched nonstop flights between PER and Paris Charles de Gaulle Airport (CDG), its third nonstop flight to Europe.

Despite the close cultural and economic ties between Australia and Europe, the two were simply too far apart to be connected by nonstop flights in the past. The 787-9, however, has the range to fly nonstop to Europe from Perth, Qantas's westernmost hub, while its size and fuel efficiency mean that it can make these relatively thin routes work.

Due to the conflict in the Middle East, Qantas is unable to operate the departure leg nonstop as airspace restrictions make it too long for the 787-9. Instead, the flight has to operate with a stopover in Singapore. However, Qantas is still flying the return leg from CDG to PER nonstop.

Four out of the seven routes use the Boeing 787.

Melbourne Airport (MEL) is a secondary hub for Qantas, and local demand is strong as Melbourne is Australia's second-largest city. However, Melbourne is located further south and west than Sydney, adding significant distance to North America services, and thereby adding cost.

Qantas first launched nonstop services between MEL and DFW in 2022 using the 787-9. This is the longest route operated out of DFW, as well as Qantas's longest route to the US. What makes this route particularly appealing to Qantas is the American Airlines joint venture.

After all, this provides huge feed in DFW to fill this flight, and the two partners now have an extremely comprehensive network out of DFW. American and Qantas operate a metal-neutral joint venture, which means that between the US and Australia, a Qantas flight also doubles as an American flight and vice versa.

Combined, the two offer nonstop services to SYD and MEL via Qantas's flights, while American Airlines also operates a winter seasonal route to Brisbane. Altogether, the two serve Australia's three largest cities nonstop from DFW, while local customers can also fly nonstop with American to Auckland during the winter.

Qantas's announcement of nonstop flights between PER and London Heathrow Airport (LHR) in late 2016 was initially seen by some as risky. With flight times exceeding 17 hours, some wondered whether passengers would truly prefer such a long nonstop route over a stopover in Singapore or Dubai.

In 2018, Qantas officially launched nonstop services between PER and LHR, as part of a flight that originated in SYD, and the route was a major success. Never before had Australia and Europe been connected by nonstop flights, and Qantas saw this route outperform much of the rest of its network, as passengers overwhelmingly preferred to fly nonstop to London.

Qantas originally had the flight number originate in SYD, but it later became only a PER-LHR nonstop. Note that the airline is now only flying the return segment nonstop due to Middle Eastern airspace restrictions, with the leg towards LHR stopping in SIN. However, while the 787-9 is amazingly efficient and has operated this route for years, it's not perfect either.

Despite only being configured with 236 seats, Qantas still frequently has to limit payload. Low costs are good, but the airline can't make money if revenue becomes too restricted. As such, the carrier plans to place its Project Sunrise Airbus A350-1000ULR on this route in the distant future. While the plane only has two additional seats, it can carry much more payload and has significantly more premium capacity.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The best of Simple Flying, directly in your inbox.

<i><em>I Play Rocky</em></i> Tells Sylvester Stallone’s Underdog Story. Here’s What Really Happened.

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The new biopic revisits the actor-turned-screenwriter’s long-shot fight to get Rocky made.

ROCKY IS A work of fiction, but it’s fair to say Sylvester Stallone threw his heart and suffering onto the page. The then-29-year-old actor-turned-screenwriter was a Hollywood nobody when the boxing movie to end all boxing movies was filmed in early 1976 (with a release later that year). The now-80-year-old had done minor work in largely forgotten films (oh, and don’t forget the porno!) when he wrote himself his dream leading role and convinced studio United Artists to both make the film and take a chance on the hardscrabble, sometimes literally hard-to-understand Sly as the star.

This story is recounted in I Play Rocky, the acclaimed biopic about the making and myth behind both Rocky and Stallone, played by Anthony Ippolito. Sly is, alas, “all but indivisible” from his creation, as The Guardian wrote in a review of his new memoir The Steps, “each an extension of the other.” Sly has his own version of his ascension, though it’s laced with frustration toward family and a certain producer on Rocky who he feels fleeced him (more on that below).

But what’s real in I Play Rocky, what’s Hollywood-ified, and what’s Stallone’s own hard-won truth? Here we dig into the facts behind Rocky’s development, production, and eventual gangbusters box office reception. But we also get into Stallone’s life and career, where the two merge with Rocky “The Italian Stallion” Balboa, and how well I Play Rocky streamlines it all into popcorn fun.

Sylvester Stallone was born on July 6, 1946, in Hell’s Kitchen, then a rough neighborhood of Manhattan, according to History. As Sly tells it in a (fascinating) 1976 profile in The New York Times, he was raised by a “bickering Italian couple” who put him in foster care while they worked. (His hairdresser father, Francesco “Frank” Stallone Sr., who had a tiny part in Rocky as the timekeeper in the climax fight, is embodied by a curmudgeonly Matt Dillon in I Play Rocky. Stallone’s mother, Jacqueline “Jackie” Stallone, is not depicted, but had a wild career as an astrologer, trapeze artist, chorus girl, women’s-only gym owner, and boxing promoter.)

Stallone became a juvenile delinquent who “attended 12 schools by the time he was 15, and was kicked out of most of them.” More promisingly, the native New Yorker went to high school in Philadelphia, aka Rocky town, and threw himself into sports, being a star fullback and discus thrower. In an unexpected move, he then attended university at American College in Leysin, Switzerland, where he picked up a part-time job chasing men away from female dorms, except he found it was more lucrative to “look the other way” for two francs an hour (money that scored him flights back home).

Studying drama came next at the University of Miami for two years before he moved back to New York with the loftiest thespian ambitions. Instead, he found work as an usher at a movie theater and cleaning cages at a zoo. I Play Rocky starts with a very broke Stallone as a theater usher, though there’s no zoo action, and the story cuts the very funny fact that he was fired from said theater when he tried scalping a M*A*S*H ticket to the theater’s owner, Walter Reade. (Also absent from I Play Rocky: Among Sly’s ambitions was a fully autobiographical film that he planned to call From Roaches to Riches.)

Sly took what scraps he could in his New York days, appearing in the low-budget greaser comedy The Lords of Flatbush (contrary to I Play Rocky, he was a co-lead actor, and even wrote additional dialogue). He had an uncredited bit part in Robert Altman’s 1970 touchstone M*A*S*H (yes, the same film for which he tried to scalp a ticket) and acted in Capone (1975) as well as the Roger Corman-produced cult B-movie Death Race 2000 (1975), playing Machine Gun Joe Viterbo.

That’s not all. In desperate need of money, the real-life Italian Stallion took a part in the 1970 softcore porn film The Party at Kitty and Stud's, in which he lets it all hang out—without hardcore sex. He thought the movie was garbage, but he was paid $200 for two days of work. “It was either do that movie or rob someone because I was at the end—at the very end—of my rope,” Stallone told Playboy in 1978, according to Far Out.

California came calling. With the tiniest bit of fame to his name, he moved to Los Angeles with his first wife Sasha Czack (AnnaSophia Robb in I Play Rocky), where they lived in a one-and-a-half-bedroom apartment ''in the pancreas of Hollywood,” he told the Times, for $215 a month.

“Things weren’t going so well there,” Stallone said in an archival interview quoted by Forbes. “As a matter of fact, I actually had to go out and try to sell my dog because it was either that or he wasn’t going to be very well fed around the house.” In I Play Rocky, you see Stallone actually sell his bull mastiff Butkus—then locate and buy back Butkus, who would go on to be the dog in Rocky—though it’s hard to verify if things unfolded exactly this way.

Inspiration came when he watched the championship fight in 1975 between Chuck Wepner, a club fighter known as ''The Bayonne Bleeder,'' and world champion Muhammad Ali. “And for one brief moment, this supposed stumblebum turned out to be magnificent,” Stallone later mused, according to Forbes. “I thought if this isn’t a metaphor for life.”

The lore is that based on this brief moment, Stallone went on a tear, writing the script for Rocky in just three and a half days. This becomes a crucial montage in I Play Rocky, with Sly putting pen to paper, then snoozing while his wife types it out. In reality, Stallone told the Times that he had the fight in his mind for a month, “my inspiration stage. Then I let it incubate for 10 months, the incubation stage.” Finally, writing the screenplay in a mad dash was merely “the verification stage.” Stallone loves stages.

Much of the tension in I Play Rocky actually takes place before a frame of Rocky is shot. Stallone gets a meeting with two producers, Irwin Winkler (P.J. Byrne) and Robert Chartoff (Toby Kebbell). When he turns out not to be right for a part, on his way out of the office, he sneaks his Rocky script onto the secretary’s desk. In reality, Stallone had already sold several scripts, mostly to television, and he simply brought up the Rocky idea to the producers, who asked to see the script.

Winkler and Chartoff, playing up much of the comedic antics and sarcasm in I Play Rocky, loved Stallone’s screenplay. They just didn’t want him to play the title role. The producers were contracted with United Artists. The top brass threw out star names like Burt Reynolds, James Caan, and Ryan O’Neal. But Sly refused to sell the script unless he took the lead role. And refused again. Despite the fact that he had no car and $106 in the bank.

In I Play Rocky, the figure offered to buy the script on the condition that Stallone will not play Rocky goes up to an astronomical $360,000 (which in today’s money would be $2.1 million); however, the 1976 Times profile reports that bidding went up to $265,000. Either way, a beautiful heap of money. Finally, the producers and studio greenlit Rocky with a $1 million budget and paid Stallone $35,000 plus his acting fee. Stallone was Rocky Balboa. The entire production lasted 28 days, ''the gestation time for a water bug,'' Sly quipped to the Times.

Here’s the more important part of the contract that’s left out of I Play Rocky: Stallone sold the rights to Rocky and its characters in accepting the deal. Those rights went to Winkler—which has given Winkler a fortune plus power over the Rocky franchise, and fueled an ongoing feud between the 95-year-old producer and Stallone, who’s not shy about badmouthing Winkler. Stallone is adamant that he was swindled. Per Collider, he has called Winkler “nefarious” and “the remarkably untalented and parasitical producer of Rocky and Creed.”

Despite this supposed crooked deal, Stallone came out more than fine. What’s also not in I Play Rocky is that the then-unknown received 10 percent of the net profits (known as net points in the industry) of Rocky off the backend. Given that Rocky earned roughly $200 million at the box office, becoming the top hit of 1976—on top of picking up 10 Oscar nominations and an illustrious run in ancillary business—Stallone easily made millions off the original movie. (He claims $2.5 million, Winkler claims much higher.) He is leaps and leaps richer now.

Many forget that upon release, the critical response to Rocky was mixed, split between pans and raves. But it energized the masses, and its classic status is unquestionable now. In real life and I Play Rocky, Stallone comes out with a smile on his face, aware that his and his character’s rise from forgotten bum to underdog sensation is complete.

Are you a fan of Rocky? (If you’re not, how the hell are you reading this article?) How about Rocky II, Rocky III, the Creed movies? If so, yes, yes, yes, and again yes. On the other hand, if you don’t like Hollywood behind-the-scenes tales, boxing, or Rocky, sit this one out.

Though it fudges some turns of events and details, smoothing out the messiness and politics of a young star making his way in Hollywood, its insights can be revelatory—for example, the incredible contribution of Steadicam inventor Garrett Brown (played by real-life camera operator Michael Fuchs), whose experimental invention not only made the original Rocky possible on its shoestring budget and tight schedule, but brought a distinctive graceful movement to Rocky’s training and fighting scenes. The cast is all-around captivating; Ippolito finds soul in Stallone while managing a difficult and eccentric accent.

Perhaps unsurprisingly, as directed by Peter Farrelly (Green Book), I Play Rocky is also on the schmaltzy side. But when Sly, or Ippolito, sprints up those Philadelphia Art Museum steps determined to change history, your heart will do a little jump for him.

Paul Schrodt is a freelance writer and editor covering pop culture and the entertainment industry. He has contributed to The New York Times, The Wall Street Journal, GQ, Men's Health, The Hollywood Reporter, Los Angeles magazine, and others.

‘an open field’ Is a Deeply Personal Story of Loss, Grief and Compassion (Exclusive Netflix Trailer)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The award-winning short film, directed by Teboho Edkins and produced by his father, dives into the mourning following the death of the filmmaker’s brother in a Boeing airplane crash over Ethiopia.

In March 2019, a Boeing 737 MAX crashed in Ethiopia just six minutes after taking off from the airport in the country’s capital, Addis Ababa. It had 157 people on board, and all of them died.Among them was Max Thabiso Edkins. His older brother Teboho Edkins decided to make a film about the experience of grief and mourning that his family and the local community went through.The result is the very personal 38-minute short film an open field, directed by Edkins and produced by his father, Don Edkins, and Carine Chichkowsky. The film is now going global, hitting Netflix on Dec. 1, about a year after its world premiere at the International Documentary Film Festival Amsterdam (IDFA) 2025, where it won the best short documentary award. Related Stories Business Netflix Taps Mattel's Roberto Stanichi to Lead Consumer Products and Experiences Business Dark Slope to Open New Virtual Production Stage in Vancouver (Exclusive)

The title of the movie becomes clear when you watch it. In an open field, the filmmaker finds out that dozens of nearby villagers immediately converged on the crash site to help. Some even stood vigil for a month to protect and preserve the area. Years later, they continue to mourn alongside family members who visit the site, including Edkins and his father, also making the film a story about collective grief and compassion.

“Boeing’s widely criticized response to the crash stands in sharp contrast to the community’s enduring compassion,” highlights a synopsis. “Both a personal essay on loss and an exploration of the potential healing power of collective mourning, Edkins’s film is a quiet testament to humanity – moments of warmth, resolve, grief, fear, patience, and simple acts of kindness.”Indeed, the film feels like a cinematic attempt to connect with a late brother and son’s spirit. In that sense, an open field is a family film. And it may be set in a far-away place for many viewers, but it transcends borders, given that the plane’s passengers came from more than 30 countries and that anyone who has lost a loved one has faced the burden of that loss.

an open field is a South Africa-France-Germany production by Day Zero Film and Video Productions in South Africa, Day Zero Films UG in Germany and Survivance in France. The cinematographer is Jide Akinleminu, the editor is Anne Fabini. The film, shot in color and black-and-white, features dialogue in both English and Amharic.

“This is a delicate and personal work and about something fundamental to being human,” says director Teboho Edkins. “I hope that the film can find its way to as many people as possible, and that something in it might resonate with them. Having Netflix bring the film to its global audiences is very meaningful to me, and I’m grateful for their support as it makes its way out into the world.”

THR can now exclusively premiere a trailer for an open field ahead of its Netflix debut.

Stove God Cooks Finds Home for Highly-Anticipated Sophomore Album

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Upstate New York MC just dropped the project's second single alongside Swizz Beatz.

Stove God Cooks has found a home for his much anticipated sophomore album.

After a year of false starts, a scrapped Goat Stamp album, a February lead single in “Welcome to My Garden” and a previous March 27 release date, All This For Me? will be released early 2027 via Babygrande Records in partnership with Warner Records Billboard has exclusively learned.

And while the Syracuse, N.Y. rapper has been keeping himself busy with features on songs like “Live or Let Die” with French Montana and Max B and “The Aroma” with Fat Joe and Jadakiss, he’s remained patient and is excited to finally give his fans a new full length album. Related Kenny MacPherson, Veteran Music Publisher and Big Deal Music Co-Founder, Dies at 71 Jessica Lynch

“Everything played out exactly how it was supposed to,” he said in a press release. “Real power is knowing when to walk away! Even from something the world thinks you need. A lot of people are afraid to do that. I’m not one of them. When God has His hand on you, what’s meant for you can’t miss you. This new season isn’t luck! It’s confirmation! I’m excited about this next chapter and what we’re building with Warner Records, but even more excited to give my fans the music and the moment they’ve patiently waited for.”

Chuck Wilson, founder and CEO of Babygrande Records used his industry connects to help find a home for this particular record after he and Stove renegotiated their initial deal.

“I met Julian Petty from Warner a decade ago. I would send him Stove updates from time to time and at some point he took notice,” he recalled. “He reached out to me, Stove and I flew out to LA to meet Warner’s team and the vibe was great. This all paved the way for what I believe is a fantastic partnership that is beneficial to all parties. Relationships are everything in the music business.”

All This For Me? will be fully produced by Swizz Beatz, who will also serve as the project’s executive producer.

“You don’t often get to revisit the past but this was the rare opportunity to partner with one of my former law clients (Babygrande) to help propel one of hip-hop’s most compelling storytellers, Stove God Cooks,” adds Julian Petty, executive VP and head of business & legal affairs at Warner Records.

You can stream the second single “Stovie’s Home (Down Down)” now.

The Midterms Could Unleash a New Era of Clean Energy in These States

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This story was originally published by Grist. Sign up for Grist’s weekly newsletter here.

When Trump was elected both in 2016 and 2024, promising to make the federal government hostile to climate action, many climate advocates insisted that state and local governments would take up the mantle of pushing for clean energy. That’s the thesis of the “America Is All In” coalition, which brings together cities and states that have vowed to uphold America’s “climate commitments … [i]n the absence of federal leadership.” The unspoken caveat in these claims is that the states leading the way on climate policy are concentrated in a few liberal parts of the country, like New England and the West Coast.

The Democratic Party’s historic victory in this week’s midterm elections could change that. Democrats have secured control of both state legislative chambers and the governor’s mansion — also known as a “trifecta” — in Arizona, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin, three states that have spent years or decades under Republican or divided control. The three states have also lagged on support for clean energy. Democrats also restored trifectas in Minnesota, Michigan, and Nevada this week, but those are states where they last had unified control earlier this decade.

“It is impossible to overstate how monumental these results are,” said David Weiskopf, the senior policy director at Climate Cabinet, a nonprofit that works to elect climate-focused candidates and pass state climate policy. He said that the six states where Democrats have secured trifectas would be one of the world’s largest emitters if they made up their own country, rivaling Canada and Saudi Arabia.  “This is just a massive opportunity to make progress.”

Here’s how the election could open up the floodgates for new climate policy in Wisconsin, Pennsylvania, and Arizona.

Compared to its neighbors in the Upper Midwest, Wisconsin has been far less ambitious when it comes to ditching fossil fuels in its power sector. While Michigan and Minnesota have both passed laws that mandate that the states get all their power from clean sources by 2040, Wisconsin set a measly 10 percent renewable electricity standard more than two decades ago. It blew past that goal but never set a new target. Unlike most other states, it doesn’t even require utilities to plan for what kinds of power plants they want to build. The state gets a larger share of its electricity from coal than its immediate neighbors.   Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Renewable energy advocates in the Badger State believe they can make some short-term gains with Democrats’ first trifecta since 2010. Erik Kanter, the policy director for the nonprofit Clean Wisconsin, said he believes that the new legislature can agree on the “low-hanging fruit” of making utilities plan for the future, which he said will nudge more of them to build renewables rather than extending the lifespans of gas and coal plants. The Wisconsin legislature failed this year to pass a law that would require data centers to source their power from clean sources, and Kanter expects such a measure to pass once Democrats take control. The legislature will also likely renew programs like Knowles-Nelson, a longstanding fund for parks and ecosystem preservation, which once enjoyed bipartisan approval but has lost support from Republicans in recent years.

Weiskopf, of Climate Cabinet, believes that even more ambitious moves are in the offing. He expects Wisconsin’s new class of legislators to pursue a 100 percent clean electricity standard, filling what he calls a “Wisconsin-sized hole” in the Upper Midwest. Not only would that help to phase out coal in the state, it would also make it easier for neighboring states to meet their climate goals, since the whole region shares the same interconnected power grid. Related Content Stevie Nicks Knew a Blue Tsunami Was Coming Will Trump Bring Down His Pumped-Up God of War? Dummymander: The GOP’s Bid to Rig the Midterm Map Failed Spectacularly Democrats Stopped Lecturing America — And Won Big

The Pennsylvania Senate has long been known to Democrats as the place where climate policy goes to die. Even though the party managed to flip the lower house of the legislature a few years ago, Republican leaders in the upper chamber often refused to bring climate bills to a vote. The Keystone State’s status as a large producer of both natural gas and coal also made it difficult to secure agreements within the Democratic Party on clean energy proposals.

“You don’t want to let yourself fully imagine what it can be, because you’ve been let down in the past,” said Lindsey Williams, a state senator who represents a swath of the Pittsburgh metro area. “Now we’re in that moment of, wow, there’s so many things that we can accomplish.”

Every year since 2019, Williams has introduced a bill that would establish mandatory energy and water efficiency standards for appliances like ovens and showerheads, a measure she said could save consumers millions of dollars a year. The legislation has never moved forward — but she thinks it will in next year’s session.

She hopes Democrats will use their first trifecta since 1993 to advance a full-scope climate agenda. In addition to passing laws that would allow community solar and warehouse rooftop solar, Democratic lawmakers also want to pass a funding package for struggling mass transit systems like Philadelphia’s. The $1.5 billion infusion would help prevent the transit “death spiral” for those systems and would reduce reliance on gasoline-powered cars.

The state’s reelected centrist governor, Josh Shapiro, appears to be on board with at least some of these proposals. Although he withdrew the state from the Regional Greenhouse Gas Initiative, a Northeast-wide carbon pricing system, he has promised to create a similar cap-and-trade system for Pennsylvania itself if he can get legislative support. He has also named funding for mass transit as a leading item on his agenda for the new legislative session next year. But Democrats will have to be careful: While they will now control both chambers, they’ll have slim majorities in each, and may have just a narrow two-year window to pass climate policy before one flips back.

“We need to be brave and lead,” said Williams. “Yes, what are the low-hanging fruits, but what are the big ambitious things that we can get done this session?”

The last time Democrats had a trifecta in the Grand Canyon State was in 1966, decades before the general public was even aware of global warming. The state has ample sunshine and wind, so its renewable energy industry has grown quickly over recent years, and its grid is cleaner than in regions like the Midwest. Furthermore, the many new-built suburbs of the Phoenix metroplex tend to be heated with electricity rather than natural gas. Yet Republicans in the state legislature have all but banned climate and environmental policy, prohibiting the state government from regulating greenhouse gas emissions or energy usage, and numerous climate and water bills have failed in recent years.

Lauren Kuby, a longtime Democratic legislator who represents the Phoenix suburb of Tempe, says she and her colleagues have a long wish list that spans several issues. Their first order of business is to reform the Arizona Corporation Commission, the state’s utility regulator, which Kuby says has been slow to embrace new technologies like solar and wind even as developers seek to build more of them. Legislators want to pass a law that would direct the commission to make utilities invest in solar, batteries, and “virtual power plants,” or networks of homes and businesses that coordinate their energy usage. As part of the reform, they might even change the commission’s name, taking out the “corporation” and replacing it with “public utilities.” The two Democrats who appear set to win seats on the commission will make these reforms easier to push through. Trending Stories Appeals Court Says Lawsuit Over ‘The Pitt’ Can Proceed, Cites ‘Numerous’ Similarities to ‘ER’ Adam Lambert on His Future With Queen, New Music, and Why the Show Must Go On Pink Floyd Marks ‘Animals’ 50th Anniversary With Reissue Featuring Unreleased Music As Nicki Minaj Rallied for Trump's GOP, Her Lawyers Told Court She Still Owes Them $295,000

The legislature could also break long-standing logjams on the crucial issue of water — a scarce resource in a desert state where climate change is worsening droughts. Kuby and other senators have been trying for years to pass a bill that would regulate groundwater pumping in the state’s rural areas. These areas are home to massive dairies and alfalfa farms like Riverview and the Saudi-backed Fondomonte, which have been blamed for sinking land and dried-up residential wells. The bill has failed in several legislative sessions, but Kuby believes that some form of regulation can pass in the coming year, bringing an end to the water free-for-all in the desert areas outside Phoenix.

“That has been languishing and has not been heard in the legislature, even though we have agreement from a lot of Republicans on water and environmental protection,” said Kuby. Of the trifecta, she said, “It’s a game-changer.”

Draw Names Instead of Buying for Every Adult – Propose It Before Thanksgiving

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

In a big family, buying a gift for every adult adds up fast. Propose a name draw before Thanksgiving instead, where each person draws one name and buys that person a gift under a price cap you all agree on. Everyone gives and gets one gift instead of a stack, and that one can be better.

In a family of 10 adults, buying a $50 gift for each of the other nine costs every person $450, and the family $4,500 in all.

With a name draw and a $75 cap, each person spends $75 instead of $450, or $375 less, and receives one $75 gift rather than nine $50 ones.

In Deloitte’s 2026 holiday retail survey, 47% of respondents said they would rather spend more on one meaningful gift than give several smaller ones. A name draw lets everyone in the family give that kind of gift.

Propose the switch before Thanksgiving, and before anyone has bought a gift, so the family can draw names at dinner that day or online that weekend. Pitch it as less time in stores and more fun at the exchange, not as a way to save money. Then choose the format that suits your family:

A free site such as Elfster or drawnames will run a Secret Santa draw for you. Both can keep spouses from drawing each other and let each person make a wish list, so the one gift is something its recipient asked for.

Settle the rules before anyone draws. Set one cap for every gift and treat it as a limit, not a target. Decide whether the kids join the draw or still get gifts from everyone. Agree, too, that no one buys extra gifts outside the draw, since each one raises the bill again.

Send the whole family one message with the cap, the format, and a draw date already set, so all anyone has to do is say yes.

The International Power Broker Trying to Normalize Hamas

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: The International Power Broker Trying to Normalize Hamas

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

In 2006, the last time that Palestinians voted in an election, national power broker Mohammed Dahlan fiercely opposed the new Hamas-led administration in Gaza. Dahlan has been in exile in the United Arab Emirates since 2011. But he is now setting out again to win political power in Gaza by uniting the fractious Palestinian movement—this time by including Hamas. And he is suspected of doing so with the support of influential Arab leaders and Israelis and Americans across the political spectrum.

Earlier this month, when Palestinian President Mahmoud Abbas postponed elections scheduled for November to next autumn, it wasn’t surprising. Every attempt since the last election 20 years ago has run into trouble either due to Abbas’s fear of losing or Israel’s refusal to allow voting in East Jerusalem. Fatah and Hamas, the two main Palestinian factions, clashed in 2006, which resulted in a de facto division of Palestinian territories into the West Bank and Gaza.

In 2006, the last time that Palestinians voted in an election, national power broker Mohammed Dahlan fiercely opposed the new Hamas-led administration in Gaza. Dahlan has been in exile in the United Arab Emirates since 2011. But he is now setting out again to win political power in Gaza by uniting the fractious Palestinian movement—this time by including Hamas. And he is suspected of doing so with the support of influential Arab leaders and Israelis and Americans across the political spectrum.

Earlier this month, when Palestinian President Mahmoud Abbas postponed elections scheduled for November to next autumn, it wasn’t surprising. Every attempt since the last election 20 years ago has run into trouble either due to Abbas’s fear of losing or Israel’s refusal to allow voting in East Jerusalem. Fatah and Hamas, the two main Palestinian factions, clashed in 2006, which resulted in a de facto division of Palestinian territories into the West Bank and Gaza.

Israel has exploited Palestinian divisions, citing the absence of a unified Palestinian political entity as a reason it could not move forward on peace. Most Palestinians have relentlessly called for unity among Palestinian factions. Dahlan’s party, the Democratic Reform Bloc (DRC) is now trying to achieve that elusive aim. However, including Hamas in any political alliance risks rattling Israel, which is suspected of deploying myriad tools at its disposal to influence Palestinian politics.

Born in Khan Younis, the same town as former Hamas leader Yahya Sinwar, who plotted the attack against Israel on Oct. 7, 2023, Dahlan is seen as a son of the soil. He learned the ropes from Yasser Arafat, the architect of the Palestine Liberation Organization (PLO), when Israel deported him to Jordan. In 1994, as the Oslo Accords were set in motion, Abbas appointed Dahlan to manage the enclave’s security as head of the Palestinian Authority’s (PA) Preventive Security organization. His reputation and reach soon earned him a reputation, and Gaza was often referred to as “Dahlistan.”

Dahlan dealt with Israeli intelligence officials and quickly made a name for himself as someone who anyone could work with. He fought against Hamas in 2006, but it was Abbas who later exiled him on corruption charges. Abbas was wary of Dahlan’s ambition and access, fearing that he might be a fierce competitor one day.

Dahlan relocated to the UAE and became a security advisor to the ruler of Abu Dhabi, Mohammed bin Zayed. He was close to the sheikh when the Abraham Accords were signed and during the military escalation in Gaza following Oct. 7, when the UAE maintained its diplomatic commitment and didn’t walk out of the deal. His proximity to Abu Dhabi’s leadership made him appealing to Israel. Could he be a Trojan horse in the Palestinian movement?

“Many Israeli intelligence officials, even those close to [Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin] Netanyahu, have met him,” said Eran Lerman, a former Israeli deputy national security advisor and retired intelligence colonel who serves as vice president of the Jerusalem Institute for Strategy and Security.

Dahlan played an instrumental role in convincing Hamas to decommission its weapons during talks with U.S. President Donald Trump’s Board of Peace—although the fate of that proposal hangs in balance as Israel demands full disarmament. Muhammad Shehada, a visiting fellow with the European Council on Foreign Relations, said that Dahlan was brought into the negotiations to build trust with Israel.

Israel has signed peace treaties with both Egypt and the UAE, and it sees them as firm opponents of the Muslim Brotherhood, a pan-Arab movement that Israel views as an ideological fountainhead of Hamas. Dahlan maintains open channels with both Abu Dhabi and Cairo, and those relationships make him an interesting figure for Israeli strategists.

In a 2024 interview with the New York Times, Dahlan laid out his vision for Palestine, declaring, “No Abbas, no Hamas,” and calling for a new generation to take charge of the PA.

Dahlan has funneled Emirati funds into Gaza, helping him revive his standing and expand his reach. He has maintained a political base through the DRC.

Dimitri Diliani, a DRC spokesperson, said that Dahlan was meeting with former Hamas chief Ismail Haniyeh in Doha when the Times article was published, yet Hamas raised no objections. “That was the first sign that Hamas was willing to give up control of Gaza,” Diliani said. “But neither the U.S. nor Israel took notice.”

Hamas might accept a relegated status in Palestinian governance, but observers have questioned whether there is more than meets the eye. Some have argued that Dahlan is throwing Hamas a lifeline, ostensibly to challenge Abbas but also allowing it to survive another day as a political entity.

The DRC has formed an alliance comprising Hamas, Palestinian Islamic Jihad, and Fatah breakaway factions to support a unified electoral list of independent candidates to compete against Abbas. “Hamas agreed they will not participate directly and will support whatever list we announce,” Diliani said. “It will be a national list that represents us, but none of our political figures will be in it.”

That offers little comfort to Israel, which demands Hamas’s complete exit from Gaza rather than political influence by proxy. A former senior Israeli security official, who previously spoke highly of Dahlan as someone who could be acceptable to Israel to lead Gaza, now warned about his motives. The official described him as “a thief” who “can play both sides.”

Is Dahlan playing Israel, or is there a larger strategy at play behind the scenes? When asked whether he could convince Israelis to discuss a two-state solution, Dahlan struck a harsh tone. “It’s not my job to convince the Israelis,” he told the Times. “Let them go to hell.”

Adopting a firm stance against Israel remains a political necessity for any Palestinian leader—lest they be labeled as a puppet. Yet Dahlan has positioned himself as a rare figure capable of liaising between Israel, the United States, and Hamas.

Dahlan maintains he has no desire to officially run the Palestinian territories but that is standard political speak to hide real intentions. However, Lerman noted that with business ventures spanning Belgrade and beyond, Dahlan is financially well-off and may have little interest in returning to Gaza and enduring the privations that come with it.

Israel is also running out of options. It is unhappy with Abbas and sees him as ineffective in managing armed resistance, and it has called on the European Union to pressure Abbas to reform the PA and link European aid to reforms. A different version of PA reforms has been discussed in Palestinian circles. They wish to downgrade the PA, which was formed as part of the Oslo process to pave the path for a Palestinian state, and revive the PLO, which is the umbrella movement for self-determination. Palestinians argue that they didn’t get a state; instead, the PA has eroded the movement. In this vision, the PLO includes Hamas.

Diliani said that it was pointless to seek Hamas’s decimation. Even if Hamas gives up weapons, another armed rebellion will rise. “As long as Israel occupies Palestinian lands, resistance will persist in one form or another,” he said. “Dahlan has an inclusive vision.”

Israel may have to choose between Dahlan’s broader alliance that includes Hamas as a political actor or backing Abbas and his familiar operatives—such as Hussein al-Sheikh, the PA’s second-in-command, or Majed Faraj, the PA’s intelligence chief who recently called on Hamas to apologize for Oct. 7. Dahlan has a year to navigate these competing pressures and decide if he wants to jettison Hamas or risk being outmaneuvered by Israel.

British Airways $710 Million Tax Fight Could Leave Members With Less Valuable Points

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

British Airways’ parent has €638 million tied up in a fight over taxing Avios. The government wants 20% value added tax when the points are issued. For members, that creates a risk of fewer points for their spending, weaker bonuses or more expensive redemptions (even if the tax authority never sends them a bill). IAG’s November 6 financial results update a dispute with the British tax authority over how its loyalty business should have been taxed going back to March 2018. While IAG is the legal taxpayer, a tax on the business selling your rewards can reduce what those rewards deliver. And VAT is something ultimately passed onto the consumer but collected throughout the value chain. What Britain Wants To Tax IAG historically accounted for value added tax based on what members redeemed their Avios for. Most redemptions were flights attracting a zero rate. The tax authority’s position is that IAG Loyalty is being paid to develop, administer and maintain a loyalty program. On that interpretation, the issuance of Avios attracts 20% tax regardless of what the member eventually books. IAG’s more detailed half-year disclosure says its treatment relied on an earlier ruling from the same tax authority and existing case law. It’s challenging the assessment and whether the government can reverse that treatment retrospectively. And the November filing identifies two amounts: €516 million of historical payments, net of input-tax recoveries, recorded as an asset IAG expects to recover if it wins. €122 million of subsequent payments disclosed as a contingent asset. Some of these amounts reduce the revenue IAG ultimately recognizes from Avios. Together that’s €638 million (US$710 million). IAG expects a tax tribunal hearing during the remainder of 2026 and a decision in 2027. Members Can Pay Without Receiving A Tax Bill Suppose a bank buys Avios to give customers for using its credit card. If supplying those Avios becomes more expensive, several things can happen: IAG receives less after tax. Existing contracts may prevent it from immediately charging partners more, leaving shareholders with lower profits. The bank pays more. It can absorb that cost, buy fewer Avios, reduce earning or initial bonuses, raise card fees, or steer spending toward a different rewards product. The member gets less from the points. Changes to award prices, cash charges or redemption availability can lower the cost of honoring rewards—but also make the currency less attractive to collect. IAG’s disclosure flags payments it doesn’t consider recoverable from commercial partners. That leaves IAG holding those costs initially. But it forces a reevaluation prospectively of those agreements, or revaluing the points themselves. In other words taxing the creation of rewards makes it more expensive to offer the same rewards. If supplying those Avios becomes more expensive, several things can happen: IAG receives less after tax. Existing contracts may prevent it from immediately charging partners more, leaving shareholders with lower profits. The bank pays more. It can absorb that cost, buy fewer Avios, reduce earning or initial bonuses, raise card fees, or steer spending toward a different rewards product. The member gets less from the points. Changes to award prices, cash charges or redemption availability can lower the cost of honoring rewards—but also make the currency less attractive to collect. IAG’s disclosure flags payments it doesn’t consider recoverable from commercial partners. That leaves IAG holding those costs initially. But it forces a reevaluation prospectively of those agreements, or revaluing the points themselves. In other words taxing the creation of rewards makes it more expensive to offer the same rewards. Topics on this pageAviosInternational Airlines GroupLoyalty programValue added taxValue-added taxBritish AirwaysUnited Kingdom+2 more More From View from the Wing

IAG historically accounted for value added tax based on what members redeemed their Avios for. Most redemptions were flights attracting a zero rate.

The tax authority’s position is that IAG Loyalty is being paid to develop, administer and maintain a loyalty program. On that interpretation, the issuance of Avios attracts 20% tax regardless of what the member eventually books.

IAG’s more detailed half-year disclosure says its treatment relied on an earlier ruling from the same tax authority and existing case law. It’s challenging the assessment and whether the government can reverse that treatment retrospectively. And the November filing identifies two amounts:

Together that’s €638 million (US$710 million). IAG expects a tax tribunal hearing during the remainder of 2026 and a decision in 2027.

Existing contracts may prevent it from immediately charging partners more, leaving shareholders with lower profits. The bank pays more. The bank pays more. It can absorb that cost, buy fewer Avios, reduce earning or initial bonuses, raise card fees, or steer spending toward a different rewards product.

The member gets less from the points. Changes to award prices, cash charges or redemption availability can lower the cost of honoring rewards—but also make the currency less attractive to collect.

IAG’s disclosure flags payments it doesn’t consider recoverable from commercial partners. That leaves IAG holding those costs initially. But it forces a reevaluation prospectively of those agreements, or revaluing the points themselves. In other words taxing the creation of rewards makes it more expensive to offer the same rewards.

So disappointed by the constant sane-washing, corporate shilling, and inserting of plays deniability to absolve these major multibillion dollar/pound megacorps from paying their fair share, in taxes and in wages, and actually operating reliably.

Consumers and workers should be saying ‘enough is enough’ and demand better. The UK already has a decent baseline, but even it can be improved; the US really needs to catch-up, fast. Dolly Parton's Estate Drops Lawsuit Against Her Nephew Bryan Seaver

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

More than a month after Dolly Parton's estate She's Alive LLC filed a lawsuit against her nephew Bryan Seaver, the company run by her longtime manager Danny Nozell requested to dismiss the case.

Dolly Parton's estate is clocking out of the family drama.

More than a month after nephew Bryan Seaver was hit with a lawsuit from She's Alive LLC—a company run by Dolly's longtime manager Danny Nozell, established to protect her businesses after her August death—the organization filed to dismiss the suit, according to court documents obtained by The New York Times and People.

The Nov. 5 notice of voluntary dismissal with the Chancery Court of Tennessee was filed without prejudice, so her estate could refile if it chooses.

Following the dismissal filing, Seaver's attorney Dominic J. Leonardo emphasized that his client's love for Parton—who tapped her nephew to announce her death at age 80—was there each step of the way.

"Mr. Seaver cares deeply about his Aunt Dolly, her music, her family, and the incredible legacy she left behind," he said in a statement to TMZ. "He has made a career out of protecting his aunt while she was alive and remains committed to Dolly and his entire family."

"Mr. Seaver is grateful to put this misunderstanding behind him," the message continued, "so that the entire family can continue to grieve peacefully and ensure that the bright light of Dolly's legacy remains entirely untouched."

E! News has reached out to Leonardo for comment and has not yet heard back.

Seaver—who was fired from his longtime role shortly before the lawsuit and an additional temporary restraining order was filed and granted—was accused of embarking on an "obscene, deliberate, and escalating campaign of threats" before and after Parton's death to get money from her estate, according to the September lawsuit obtained by E! News.

In one alleged instance detailed in the suit, her former head of security threatened to ruin a Dollywood business relationship. The message purportedly read, "I'm going to destroy the entire brand if you don't get these [Dollywood business partners] out of our bit."

Another exchange allegedly sent after the "Jolene" singer's death appeared to show Seaver threatening to start a podcast "dedicated to ruining Dolly's brand partnerships and telling the violations" people "have done on us."

"It's going to be great," Seaver purportedly wrote to Nozell. "Or f--king pay me."

Meanwhile, the temporary restraining order was granted, in which he was barred from contacting and coming within 1,000 feet of She's Alive employees and business partners, as well as entering company properties or interfering with business relationships

In response to the request made by She's Alive to extend the restraining order in October, Seaver vehemently denied the accusations against him, though he acknowledged using harsh language in his interactions.

"They were sent in the course of private conversations between two friends," he argued in the October affidavit obtained by CBS News, "who were both frustrated, grieving, and discussing strategy following Ms. Parton's illness and death."

He alleged the messages were not threats but rather a "private warning” about potential associates wanting to harm Parton's brand.

"I have declined to speak with the media about this case and have asked my attorneys to handle all media communications," Seaver concluded in the doc. "I care about Ms. Parton, her music, her legacy, and her family. I do not want this dispute to harm her legacy."

As the Parton family navigates this difficult chapter following the legendary singer's death, keep reading for a look back at her life.

‘Dune’ Star Chang Chen Introduces Six Rising Taiwanese Actors In Golden Horse & TCCF Showcase

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Taiwanese star Chang Chen introduced six rising actors in a showcase co-organized by the Golden Horse Awards and Taiwan Creative Content Fest (TCCF) at the seventh edition of TCCF today.

The event, which marks the first collaboration between TCCF and Taiwan’s Golden Horse Awards, aims to raise the profile of Taiwanese actors at a time when they’re becoming a key factor in structuring international co-productions.

The six talents included actresses Bai Xiao-ying (Girl), Ho Man-xi (Sunshine Women’s Choir), Ma Shih-yuan (Left-Handed Girl), and Lin Yi-ting (A Dance With Rainbows), along with actors Rosen (Marching Boys) and Jimmy Liu (Blind Love). Related Stories News Weta FX CEO Daniel Seah Unpacks TAICCA Partnership: "Taiwan Has Creative Talent, Technology & Government Support" News Taiwan Creative Content Fest Opens With Focus On IP, Co-Production & Pledges Of Government Support

Bai and Ho are both nominated for Best New Performer at this year’s Golden Horse Awards, while the remaining four talents were all nominated last year, with Ma winning in the category. Watch on Deadline

“We’re seeing more co-productions involving Taiwan and it’s encouraging to see this kind of exchange,” said Chang, whose recent credits include Dune and Cannes Directors Fortnight title Lucky Lu. “It’s important for us to learn more about each other, particularly in international co-productions, to avoid misunderstandings that could delay the production process.”

Sue Wang, Chair of TCCF organizer the Taiwan Creative Content Agency (TAICCA), said: “Taiwanese actors are one of the elements that could be used to apply for TAICCA funding, so we need to raise the profile of our acting talent and create more opportunities for them to be seen.”

During the showcase, each of the actors talked about their acting, language and other professional skills and the kind of projects they hope to work on in the future. Bai speaks fluent Japanese, while Rosen and Ma both speak English. Liu was winning Taekwondo competitions before landing a role in Disney+ series American Born Chinese alongside Michelle Yeoh.

Chang, who is currently serving as Chair of the Taipei Golden Horse Film Festival Executive Committee, gave the six young actors a few career tips. “It’s not about whether a project is international or not, it’s about being adaptive to different production teams.”

He also passed on some advice that he was given by a director early in his career: “Read autobiographies of as many people as you can. As an actor, you need to experience different lives throughout your career, but at your young age, your experience of life is limited. So read autobiographies to understand the thoughts and emotions of other people.”

Golden Horse Film Festival opens tonight and runs until November 22, with the Golden Horse Awards being held on the final night of the festival. Sabu’s Arrested Memory and Shu Qi’s Girl are heading the nominations. TCCF also opened today and runs until November 9.
