What’s Next for Tom Petty’s Heartbreakers? Benmont Tench and Ron Blair Get Candid

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When the history of Tom Petty and the Heartbreakers is told, their third LP, 1979’s Damn the Torpedoes, is often characterized as the breakthrough that forever changed their fortunes. And while there’s quite a bit of truth to that, it obscures the fact that it was preceded by two stellar albums, several of their most beloved singles, and nonstop touring that earned them a big fan base in the U.K. and America. Prior to all that, Petty recorded a lot of music with his early band Mudcrutch and during an aborted solo career that followed in the immediate aftermath of their implosion, even though very little of it was actually released.

This fascinating era is finally getting the love it deserves in the revelatory Tom Petty and the Heartbreakers box set Origins 1971-1978, which arrives Nov. 6. It includes several Mudcrutch recordings, songs Petty created with Al Kooper, Leon Russell, Jim Jordan, and Emory Gordon shortly before the Heartbreakers came together in late 1975, remastered editions of 1976’s Tom Petty and the Heartbreakers and 1978’s You’re Gonna Get It!, studio outtakes, a complete concert they played at the Record Plant in 1977, and their performance on BBC’s Old Grey Whistle Test in 1978.

To chat about these formative years for Tom Petty and the Heartbreakers, we hopped on Zoom with bassist Ron Blair and keyboardist Benmont Tench. The conversation covers the early days of Mudcrutch, the formation of the Heartbreakers, the creation of “American Girl,” the endless slog of their early touring, Blair’s return to the band in 2002 after a two-decade hiatus, the band’s recent performance with Chris Stapleton at the 2026 Americana Honors, and the possibility of future reunions.

Ron, you obviously weren’t in Mudcrutch. When do you recall first coming across them?Blair: I went to Gainesville my second year of college, and the town kind of freaked me out. It was 1968, I think, when I got there. It was just this hippie town, like what I saw on TV of San Francisco. And my dad was in the military. I’d been in Japan for three years. I was in culture shock, and Gainesville just freaked me out. The alligators and the swamp and the flatness of it. And there were a million musicians. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

So, eventually I joined a band that needed a bass player called RGF. We started playing around, and we would do some of the same gigs that Mudcrutch would do. I would also just see them around town at different places. There was a university club called the Rathskeller and I saw them play there one time. It was very intriguing. I just thought they were a really mysterious band.

Not only did I not understand Gainesville, but I did not understand this band Mudcrutch. They were bluesy, they were country, they were eclectic, and I just couldn’t peg them. And Ben probably knows what I’m talking about. It just was so unusual. I was really intrigued by them. Then I grew to like them. I met Tom through a girlfriend I had who knew Tom’s wife. So I kind of became friends with Tom as well as an admirer of their band.

Benmont, let’s jump forward a bit. When Mudcrutch got to L.A. in 1974, I imagine you presumed you’d have a similar story to Buffalo Springfield or the Byrds by playing at the famous clubs on the Sunset Strip and signing with a major label. That’s not what happened.Tench: That was kind of the pipe dream, but I don’t think we ever articulated it. Ron is right. When I first saw Mudcrutch… I just saw somebody on Instagram using the shorthand that they were a “Southern rock band.” And no, not at all. It was semi-psychedelic, country, some Grateful Dead in there, a little bit of old rock & roll, some original stuff, all these interweaving wild, beautiful things. They were kind of indescribable. And so I fell in love with them. Then I got to join them.

When we got to L.A., we thought, “Well, it’s the Promised Land.” You either go to New York City and starve in the cold or you go to California and starve in the sunshine, the site of the Byrds and everybody. That was out west. We were like, “Let’s go there.” Related Content The Heartbreakers, With Chris Stapleton Singing, Need to Think About a Reunion Tour Watch Tom Petty's Heartbreakers Reunite to Perform 'American Girl' With Chris Stapleton John Varvatos Recreates Tom Petty's Cool, Carefree Style in a New Capsule Collection Mike Campbell on the First Time He Met Tom Petty

Tom said that he literally picked up a phone book and started calling record companies when he arrived. That didn’t go like he hoped.Tench: No, but he did get heard. I think the best feedback they got was from Playboy Records. And it was somebody who was A&R there, but was also, I think, a jazz critic, who said, “You got something, but you really have to work on your songs.”

And then the second trip out, he opened the phone book, met with a lot of people, and they said, “This is really distinctive.” Only one company wanted to sign us, London, and they wanted us to support ourselves by playing Top 40 songs in a bowling alley. We were like, “If that’s what it takes…” But, man, we were disheartened. We didn’t play Top 40, ever.

There’s a lot of really interesting Mudcrutch studio tracks on this box set. I didn’t realize the band had recorded so many songs. It’s fascinating to imagine what an actual Mudcrutch record could have sounded like if you’d kept going back then.Tench: If you had gone through all the best stuff and focused on Tom’s songs…because I was writing songs that were all over the place and not that good. Danny Roberts was singing my songs because I was too shy. And Danny sang great Southern rock, but I was trying to write, God knows why, in the vein of Todd Rundgren and Steely Dan or something, and the blues-rock voice didn’t work. If they had taken the kind of thing that’s on this record, then you can see what a Mudcrutch record could have been.

So the band dissolves and Tom goes off to work with Leon Russell, Al Kooper, and all these other great people. Do you think this was an important period that really sharpened his writing?Tench: Obviously. It was kind of an apprenticeship. And he got to explore different styles. He could hear different kinds of things and get immersed in different kinds of cultures. And it was also really important for me, too. I’m like, “Uh-oh, my family just ditched me in Los Angeles, basically. I’m on my own. My parents are helping, but I’m on my own. I better get my shit together.”

And so I worked really hard on playing better. I’d go audition for bands, and in general, it just reinforced how good a songwriter Tom was and that there was something really special in Mudcrutch. The only character I met that resonated with me was a rockabilly guitar player with a high pompadour. And he came over to my apartment and we played, and I really dug it. But I didn’t really want to focus on rockabilly, and it was [future X guitarist] Billy Zoom.

So there was nothing there. When we got to actually put the Heartbreakers together, it was like hallelujah. I’m back in the fold with the people with talent.

Ron, the first time you played with the Heartbreakers was that famous session that Benmont put together. Did you immediately feel the chemistry with everybody?Blair: I remember it feeling really comfortable, and it was easy to concentrate on the things you should be concentrating on when you’re in the studio: playing the song well, getting a decent tone out of your instrument, and joining in the camaraderie. It’s a beautiful, communal effort, which happens less and less. Now, you get one or two players together, and then the other two send you files and everything. But it was back in the day when you’d round everybody up, and it was analog tape, and you’d pretty much get the good takes early on without having to slave over it.

I talk a lot about that track “Mystery Man,” how it just unfolded before we knew it. We had cut it, and it was just, “Wow, we can do that without even trying?” It still blows my mind. I’m still trying to do that. It’s like your first shot of a good tequila. You’re always looking for that taste again, which I had to quit. But I’m still looking for that incredible magic when you get a group of people together and you just hit on something for long enough to call it a recording.

Benmont, most people don’t realize that, at least for the first day, this was your band. You put everyone together, including Tom.Tench: Well, it was me and [drummer] Stan [Lynch], and it was Tim Kramer’s band because he was my friend, a recording engineer who said, “Come down and record, get some musicians.” And I had met everybody between me and Stan. We got Mike, Ron, [guitarist] Jeff Jourard, Randall Marsh from Mudcrutch played on a couple things. But I didn’t know Ron. I knew of Ron, but I didn’t know Ron. So that was a really good surprise. It was like, “Okay, Stan, you’re right. This is the cast.”

And it was fun, man. Everybody was from Gainesville. At last, we’re all in a room, and we’re all from the same place.

In early promo shots of the band, Jeff Jourard is presented as a full member. How long did it take you guys to realize that there was one guitarist too many?Blair: Relatively early on.

Tench: He was really good, Jeff Jourard. He’s on three or four songs on the first record. He was really good, just one too many.

Ron, how well do you remember the moment you cut “American Girl” on July 4, 1976? Is that frozen in your mind?Blair: It gets more frozen every time I hear Mike talk about it because he said that that was the moment for him where it really became something. But I guess I thought that it was a really different song. At the time, I can’t even think of another song that was similar, and it just sounded about as original as you can get. I thought, “Wow, we’re an original band.”

I had sort of given up on the thought of being in a fantastic original band at that time. I had been through so much crazy band drama. I just really wanted to play music with like-minded people. And I guess that song must have spurred something. “We’re an American band. We’re an original American band with all the possibilities laid out before us.”

Ben, did you feel the same way?Tench: Well, I loved what we were doing up to then, and I will name-check, like Ron, “Mystery Man,” which I think is a gorgeous little record. But “American Girl,” I don’t know if Stan pointed it out to me at the time or Cordell or who. But it doesn’t rhyme until you get to “Oh yeah, alright/Take it easy baby/Make it last all night.” It just don’t rhyme. Really cool song, unusual beat. And also, it did strike me that “‘American Girl,’ it’s the Bicentennial. That’s kind of interesting, neither here nor there.” But it did make me focus and go, “I wonder if anybody’s ever going to notice this song?” Because I thought it was great, but you never know.

I find it fascinating that despite all those great songs on the first record, including “Breakdown,” “American Girl,” and “Fooled Again (I Don’t Like It),” the first song of yours that charted anywhere was “Anything That’s Rock ‘N’ Roll” in the U.K. How did that happen?Tench: The record companies must have thought that was a single, which is the last thing I would’ve thought was a single. But it got us on Top of the Pops. Right, Ron?

Blair: Right. Well, it just had that catchy kind of pop beat that I guess a record company would latch onto, like, “Hey, we could sell this. This will wake people up.”

Tench: It was very pop, and England will go for the very pop. They’ve always gone for the very, very pop.

You guys landed in England at the red-hot peak of the punk movement, where the Clash and Sex Pistols were playing every night. What was it like to be there at that moment?Tench: Well, I went from feeling like a fish out of water in L.A. to feeling like a fish out of water in London. It was all the same thing.

Tom is wearing a leather jacket with bullets around his neck on the first album cover. It made a lot of people just presume you were a punk band.Tench: I don’t think it was his jacket. I think they gave it to him for a photo shoot along with the bullets.

Tench: It didn’t feel like we looked or behaved at the time, but I like it. But the punk thing, it’s just like we were younger. We were not wearing white satin pants and doing extremely long exhibitions of whatever prowess we had on our instruments. Which probably everybody else might have, but I’m really good at faking it. So we weren’t doing prog rock, and we weren’t doing glam, and we were trying not to be overindulgent. We just wanted really good songs. So what we had in common with actual punk bands was short songs, getting to the point, good and catchy songs. I mean, you’re not going to find a better body of songs than the Clash.

What was it like to open for Al Kooper at those very early shows and see nothing but blank faces who had no clue who you were?Blair: It was just what you said. We even had an A&R man from ABC Records come back and hand us a stack of records, and I reached out and grabbed them. He said, “Here, here you go, man. We can’t give these away.” I said, “Well, I’ll take them.” They were promo copies.

Did you grow as a band during times like that, since you had to learn to win over crowds night after night?Blair: At that time, it must not have mattered because we’d travel all day. God, some days we’d be in five different kinds of vehicles, a helicopter, an airboat or whatever. But by the time you get a chance to play and the gear is pulled out of a truck and set up onstage, you’re just pretty happy to play and make some music, and you’re not really calculating the intake at the door or whatever. It’s just another whistle-stop along a long track.

Ben, what was it like for you?Tench: I was lucky. I was hiding behind an actual grand piano and the big old Hammond C3 organ. I didn’t have to face the people as much. But I figured Tom was gauging all of that stuff as far as the reaction. We all just wanted to be as great as we could. We wanted to be such a good band that we would completely blow them away. But like Ron said, we’re actually traveling around, whether it’s England or the Midwest. What are we here to do? We’re here to play.

So you’d get at the end of an actual eight, 10, 12 hours on a freezing day in a van and pull up outside the Exit/In in Nashville and go in and straight onto the stage, and you just play. And so there’s 12 people there. I remember we played that gig in Boston that got taped, the one at Paul’s Mall. There were far more people between our band and Al Kooper’s band, far more people on the stage than there were in the audience. Didn’t matter, really. Technically, yeah, of course it matters. You want to play to enough people that they want you back, and you want to make an impression, a connection. But it didn’t matter as far as how we played.

What was it like when you opened for Kiss?Tench: We played at Hot Nights in the South, a tiny club in Palm Beach, Florida, with peanut shells instead of sawdust on the floor. And then there was the chance to open for Kiss in a basketball arena. So we went from Hot Nights in the South to opening for Kiss in a basketball arena. And the rumor around was that the opening act was going to be Boston, which had a giant hit since “More Than a Feeling” had just come out. And we weren’t Boston. But we got to see Kiss without their makeup before anybody else did, so it was cool. Then we went back to a really tiny club, probably in Cocoa Beach, Florida, or something, George’s Steak House.

Tom often said that the second record was made too quickly and it suffered as a result. Is that how you see it?Tench: Yeah. I mean, it’s the second record. That’s how they usually are. You have time to stockpile songs before the first one. The second one is, “Come on, get some songs.” But by the time we were playing in England, at least certainly by the time we stuck around and played as a headliner after opening for Nils Lofgren, Tom had written “Listen to Your Heart” and “I Need to Know,” I think. So we were going out and playing those cold to an audience, and you couldn’t get them on the record yet. So he had a couple in his pocket. But a bunch of it, yeah, we kind of made up on the spot.

I love “Magnolia” and the title track. But most songs on that record, you dropped from your live repertoire right after the tour ended.Tench: Yeah. Well, we would play “Too Much Ain’t Enough” sometimes. But things like “Hurt” didn’t get much of an airing. “Magnolia” didn’t get much of an airing. I think we played it once because Peter Bogdanovich loved it. We played it much later, but I don’t think we played it much. “You’re Gonna Get It,” we used to play that song.

You guys played Knebworth with Genesis, Devo, and Jefferson Starship in 1978. The audience was just an ocean of people. What’s your memory of that day?Blair: Oh, it was a pop festival, 300,000 people or something, the biggest gig we ever played, and it was overwhelming. Did we come back and the Kinks were headlining? Was that another trip to Knebworth or was it that first time?

Tench: I don’t remember. I remember we were on fairly early in the day. I remember that we needed to be good, and I think we were good. But we came out and we would open with “Listen to Her Heart,” which starts with the guitars, and then the organ comes in. I’m like, “Hot damn, this is really killer.”

And I hit the first chord on the organ, and because of the discrepancy in voltage and the fact that somebody hadn’t put the right converter on it, it’s way out of tune with the rest of the band. I’m like, “Oh, fuck.” So it was kind of fun. I had to play the whole show on the piano, couldn’t play the organ. What I mainly remember is the pressure and the size of the crowd.

The break with producer and Shelter Records owner Denny Cordell came around this time. Tom always said it was very painful since he played such an enormous role in the early days of the band. But he also said he was just a tiger when it came to business matters.Blair: He had what happened to most of the Black artists and most of the jazz artists early on, where businessmen would get in there and sign people who don’t have the skills of the business. They sign them, they take their publishing, they take all of the money. You end up signing something that you don’t understand, and you may or may not have had a lawyer. And if you did have a lawyer, it’s somebody that they set you up with. “Here, go see this guy.” And so you sign something that’s basically giving away all your rights, and you don’t even know what they are.

Tench: Tom and Denny were best friends. And at one point, I think Tom told me that Denny had said to him, “Hey, don’t take it personally. It’s just business.” And Tom’s like, “I have a new child. It couldn’t be more personal.”

Where I was, I thought it was really sad and awkward because, for the duration of Mudcrutch and then when the Heartbreakers got together, Cordell shepherded us along. And now it was like, “Who am I going to side with?” Well, obviously, for the justice of it, because he’s my friend, Tom. But Cordell’s presence was in that town. He opened that roller disco, Flippers, which was supposed to be a really fun club, though I didn’t roller-skate and I didn’t like disco. But I never went just out of solidarity with Tom because it was Cordell’s.

But they did make up, like, five or six years later. Cordell produced two songs on Southern Accents that didn’t come out. And they were really, really good. One of them, “The Image of Me,” is really good. Cordell came in, produced this, and we sounded like us. Again, I’m like, “Hot damn.” And then it was gone. But they did patch it up. Tom played him Full Moon Fever, and Cordell apparently was thrilled.

Going into Damn the Torpedoes, did you feel extra pressure?Blair: We were out on the road for so long that we ended up coming back to L.A. kind of shell-shocked from all these incredible bad-sounding gigs, like gigs that looked like they took place in a bathtub. And then you don’t have much time to recalibrate your senses. And then you find yourself in a studio with people you don’t really know. Jimmy Iovine and Shelly Yakus, who were really good at helping Tom find good songs. And to me, it was always a shock to come off the road right back into a studio. Like, “Oh, God. Don’t we ever get a break? Can we get a break?”

Tench: The first couple records are us figuring out what we can do. And I guess the third record was with [producer] Jimmy [Iovine] and [engineer] Shelly [Yakus]. Maybe that’s us doing it, but I sure like the sound of bands figuring out what they can do.

Yeah. There’s a youthful spark that can’t really be replicated.Tench: You’re figuring out how to make it sound good in the studio, but you’re also figuring it out for us because we hadn’t played a bunch of gigs in this exact lineup. And so I’m figuring out how to play with Stan and Ron. It was really easy. And I’m realizing if I listen to what Ron’s doing and lean on that, I will never be without something to play. Hearing Tom come in with song after great song after great song, man. I was just trying to keep up with everybody else because they were all badasses. That’s the best place to be, trying to keep up with everybody else.

To flash forward quite a bit here, Ron, you told me last time we spoke that you had a lot of anxiety when you rejoined the band in 2002 after being gone for 20 years. You said it took you a while to get re-acclimated. Ben, how did you help him through that?Tench: I didn’t help him at all. He didn’t need any help. It was just like when Howie left the band, or was forced to leave the band because … Howie was my best friend in the band at that time. We just wanted him to live. The first thing I thought was, “Well, what the hell? Ron!” And it took a minute before that happened.

I still saw Ron at Mike’s house at parties and stuff. Ron never left. Ron and Mike stayed tight, as far as I could tell. So Ron was always on the horizon. I just never dreamed Ron would come back. But the second Ron showed up, it was like, I don’t know how it felt for you, Ron, but it felt to me like this is right.

Even though it wasn’t Stan, it was [Steve] Ferrone. There was a different dynamic. Scott Thurston was also in the band, and it was a way different dynamic than it was when Ron left the band. And the things that counted, I just felt like, “Ron’s here. Great!”

Blair: I had a really good attitude. My dad used to always tell me about my attitude, “Son, you had a terrible attitude.” And I think I must have. By that time, I just got my attitude together, and I just learned that stuff, and I just became the best team player you could be.

I started learning to watch out for the red-flag things, the things that send you down the wrong thought process and all that. So I just learned that stuff. I made it as seamless as I could. I wasn’t going to blow this incredible chance. I still feel that way. Please, let me just be the best version of myself, whether it turns out that way or not. Just put in the effort and the prayers and have a little bit of luck and keep rolling on, baby.

Tench: I think you need to be my guru, Ben.

Blair: Oh, you guys were very kind to me. You’d look at me and you go, “Wow.” You said something to me one night. You said, “Damn, you’ve made this effortless.” And later Tom would say in interviews that he didn’t think he could have done it with an outsider. And it was just such a beautiful thing to have somebody come back into a situation that had learned from the past. I was real lucky.

You guys played with Chris Stapleton at the Ryman in Nashville recently. What was it like emotionally to have everyone back together for the first time since Tom died?Tench: Pretty freaking cool.

Tench: I wanted to do that forever. I wouldn’t have been ready before that to play with everybody without Tom. But I know Chris Stapleton a little. I know he’s a great interpreter for us, and Mike knows him really well. But for me, it’s like to just have us have that emptier sound, Ron, Stan, me, and Mike and Chris singing. Because the more people you add, it changes, it gives you more possibilities. But it also takes up more space.

And the change of the drummer is a massive change of groove. Tom, he didn’t tell Stanley to leave and then try to get an imitator of Stanley. He went for something entirely different, and he got it in a great version. But, man, on “American Girl,” but especially on “Breakdown,” Stan hit that backbeat, and I hit the electric piano. And I looked over and I saw Ron, and I’m like, “I’m home. What the heck? I’m home.” So bloody cool. That’s no shade on Ferrone, and it’s no shade on Scott Thurston.

Ron, what was it like for you to play with Stan for the first time since the Hall of Fame?Blair: It was just awesome. I’d always dreamed of it, and I’d kind of given up that dream, like, “Okay, maybe it won’t happen, just move on.” And when it did happen, it was just amazing. My favorite thing about it is playing with the precious few players that I play with who have an incredible groove. And when I’m playing with Stan and I’m playing with Ben, Ben doesn’t allow me to move from the incredible pocket. If you listen to what Ben’s doing, it just keeps you on the straight and narrow. If you listen to what Stan’s doing, it keeps you on the straight and narrow.

The excitement of a gig can kind of pull you into playing a little too fast or thinking about how I look or some stupid stuff. But when the groove is what you’re serving, you’re serving the right master. You just definitely are. And I was going, “Oh, I felt that light. I’m in that light.”

Are you guys open to playing together again at other special events?Blair: Yeah.

Why?Tench: It would have to be a band discussion. It was really special, but it’s emotionally hard for me and musically hard for me to play without Tom. Because for a one-off, it’s a really cool thing. But the way he played, let alone the way he sang and wrote and led the band, was so central. His guitar playing, and on a couple songs his piano playing, was so central to the sound we make and made that I don’t know. I don’t know.

I don’t want to rain on anyone’s parade because I had said never, never, never, never, never for a long time. I said, “No way.”

A lot of people have fantasized about a big tribute concert with lots of guest singers.Tench: I love that idea. But I don’t want to be the house band. Well, maybe if you had some kind of one-off. It depends on how you curate it. But you also have to get all of us to agree on it. And I don’t think there’s anybody in the band that we would all follow blindly like we followed Tom. It doesn’t mean we’d fight. It means we all go like, “You’re right. Okay. Or I have my doubts, but I believe you. And so I’m going to come along and be happy.” So I don’t know.

Ron, what are your thoughts on all this?Blair: Well, Ben just made me start thinking about something else. When you are in a band and you work for and you’re part of a band with a Tom Petty, you don’t realize what a fabulous force that is that keeps it on its track. And without Tom, it was a little bit of a surprise to me. I’d pictured the band a little more rock-steady as a unit. I never thought about what if Tom was gone. But when he was gone, I went, “Oh my God, when you play with someone that great, you run the risk of if he’s not there, it just kind of dissipates.” I was surprised that that would happen.

And to tell you the truth, I still kind of feel him there. When we played, somebody else was singing, but it just feels like we’re all serving the ghost or whatever. I just feel he’s with us in a good way. And I don’t even miss it because it’s pointless to miss it, to miss him. I mean, I just enjoy … He set up something for us to do, and then it’s a precious few times that we’ll probably get a chance to practice this fine art. And so I just say yes, that it could happen because I’m just a nutty, positive guy. But I don’t know. I was shocked that it happened.

Tench: I’m leery of tributes unless they’re well done. But I’ve been a part of a couple that were. The Band and Robbie [Robertson] a few years ago, and Willie Nelson’s birthday for two nights at the Hollywood Bowl were really beautifully put together. So that’s different. And we wouldn’t do it unless we did it right.

If it’s the right thing, then I might be down for it. But it’s got to be the right thing. I think probably the guys in Queen must have really missed playing together to put somebody else in and go out and tour. Yeah, it’s a money-making proposition, but it’s also really emotional.

I understand missing making that sound because, boy, did I get a lesson in that in Nashville. I understand why the Rolling Stones keep going and going and going. You make that sound, you got to make the closest thing to that sound you can. But I don’t know. I don’t know why I’m leery. But if it’s the right thing, if we come up with a good way to do it, that’s a different matter entirely.

What’s left in the vault that you hope to put on future box sets?Tench: There’s some other Mudcrutch stuff that I think is terrific that didn’t make it on here. I don’t know what there is left over from Damn the Torpedoes or Hard Promises. But I’m sure there’s stuff left over from some of the other records.

I would love an Echo box set.Tench: Yeah, there might be some stuff left over from Echo. Let Me Up, I’ve Had Enough, there’s a ton of stuff. It’s a split-personality record that has about half of it as the stuff that Mike was crafting by himself at home, and then he put us on it afterwards or not. And the other half is us live in the studio, having just played with Bob Dylan for two years, night after night, playing really loose and having a blast. And Tom, a lot of the time, is making the song up on the spot.

So I think a Let Me Up box set… although I don’t know if anybody would care. And also a Southern Accents that would be true to the original idea. Because you’ve got “Trailer,” you’ve got “The Image of Me” that Denny Cordell produced. You’ve got so many songs that would’ve fit into that original concept of an album about the South.

You mentioned the Bob Dylan tour. That would be a great package since there were so many shows, and you even filmed the one in Australia.Tench: Yeah, a great director made a film of it. I think they re-edited it before they put it out. But there’s a lot of stuff I think that was well recorded from that Australian thing. And while we stuck fairly close to the arrangements, it was great, and we had the great gospel choir.

I’d love that as a Bootleg Series.Tench: That’s up to Bob. Maybe when he gets to Bootleg Series number 1,420.

As far as live stuff, the 2013 shows at the Beacon and Henry Fonda theaters would make for a great package.Tench: The Beacon especially. The Beacon’s one of those stages like the Ryman. You’re not going to play a bad show.

Are you working on a new solo record?Tench: I think I’m starting to write some stuff. You got to have something to write about. I’ve been playing gigs, and I love it. Played one with a band, with some people that played on the record the other night, and that was a blast. But I’ve also been playing by myself, which I really enjoy.

Ron, what’s keeping you busy?Blair: I’m just entranced that Ben loves playing by himself. It kind of freaks me out, the thought of playing by myself. It’s always a card you got to hold in your pocket, like what if the band or what if my partner doesn’t show up by singing?

Tench: Ron, if it’s scary, you really got to do it.

Blair: Yeah. I’ve got to do it. It freaks me out. I guess I’m in the process of getting a group of songs together that I could call a record. But it always starts with some idea that I could go back to that. This is how this thing started, here’s what it’s about. And then here’s the original guitar lick, and it’s all there. And so I guess I should probably start thinking, use some of my positive thinking to picture that. But my hat’s off to you, Ben. I just go, “God, you’re brave, man. You get out there and you do it.”

Tench: I read somewhere that the reason to do it is because it freaks you out. And it makes you live. If you aren’t living when you’re getting older, you ain’t living anymore. If you aren’t pushing yourself, you aren’t living.

Do you guys ever think about writing memoirs?Tench: No. Well, I’ve spent so much of my life drunk. I don’t know that I remember anything accurately. Somebody asked me the other day, I said, “My book is going to be called You Had to Be There. You can open it up. It’s 270 pages long, and they’re all blank.” Trending Stories Appeals Court Says Lawsuit Over ‘The Pitt’ Can Proceed, Cites ‘Numerous’ Similarities to ‘ER’ Adam Lambert on His Future With Queen, New Music, and Why the Show Must Go On Pink Floyd Marks ‘Animals’ 50th Anniversary With Reissue Featuring Unreleased Music As Nicki Minaj Rallied for Trump's GOP, Her Lawyers Told Court She Still Owes Them $295,000

Ron?Blair: Yeah. It would be more like oblique stuff. It wouldn’t start, “I was born a poor child in Augusta, Georgia.” It wouldn’t be any of that stuff. It would just start somewhere along the way, and it would just be observations.

Apparently, I just find myself thinking differently from almost everybody that I know. When I see politics, I take a different slant on it. I don’t hear anybody reiterating the way I think on TV. I notice things, and I just seem to be different. So I would just present … If you want to see a different side of things, here it is.

Liverpool, Manchester United, and Chelsea are all among the main suitors for one of the rising talents in Serie A this season.

The player in question is AC Milan defender Koni De Winter, who is growing in stature with almost every appearance at the moment, with Premier League scouts closely monitoring him in recent matches, as first reported via the Daily Briefing.

Despite some struggles for consistency, particularly with some disappointing performances in the Europa League, Milan have shown real improvement under the management of Ruben Amorim, and De Winter is someone he’s really got the best out of.

The 24-year-old only joined Milan last season and is not understood to be desperate for a move away, though he’s expected to be one to watch in upcoming transfer windows.

As well as Liverpool, Man Utd, and Chelsea, sources with ties to the agents industry have also informed me of scouts from Newcastle, Tottenham, Leeds United, and Brentford being present to watch him in recent games.

“Koni De Winter is developing really well at Milan and there’s a lot of Premier League interest,” one source said. “He’s a versatile talent, but he’s now really making a name for himself as one of Milan’s three central defenders.”

United and Chelsea are particularly active on the defender market at the moment, having also watched Como’s Jacobo Ramon as another emerging talent in Serie A.

Even if De Winter isn’t necessarily likely to be an imminent priority, he’s clearly impressing and will surely be up there with the names being internally discussed when these clubs look for options to strengthen in January and next summer.

The Belgian may prioritise regular playing time, however, so could do well to be open to listening to offers from the likes of Newcastle and Leeds before rushing into a big six move.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/industry-voices-new-majority-new-targets-where-healthcare-fraud-enforcement-goes-next" hreflang="en">Industry Voices—New majority, new targets: Where healthcare fraud enforcement goes next</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Industry Voices—New majority, new targets: Where healthcare fraud enforcement goes next" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Control of Congress has changed. The bipartisan appetite for scrutinizing practices across the healthcare industry has not. A new Democratic majority will arrive with no shortage of investigative priorities and only so much time to pursue them, but recent history suggests that healthcare is likely to be among them.

Affordability dominated this election cycle, and healthcare sits at the center of that debate. Combating fraud in Medicare and Medicaid has endured as a rare point of consensus precisely because it allows policymakers of both parties to say they are protecting patients and taxpayers at once. The open question is not whether that work continues, but where it is directed.

Congressional oversight largely reflects the priorities of the majority, which controls committee agendas, hearings and subpoenas. If recent history is a guide, a Democratic majority will gravitate toward a familiar set of concerns: the practices of large insurers including those involved in Medicare Advantage, the stewardship of hospital systems, the role of private equity, vertical integration across the industry, drug pricing, and the hyper-growth sectors attracting new investment.

The administration has elevated healthcare fraud to the top of its enforcement agenda. The Justice Department’s 2026 National Health Care Fraud Takedown charged 455 defendants in schemes involving more than $6.5 billion in alleged losses, with particular emphasis on Medicaid, while CMS suspended or revoked the billing privileges of more than 2,400 providers. In an October 1 directive, DOJ’s newly created National Fraud Enforcement Division went further, designating healthcare fraud its foremost corporate enforcement priority.

That model has been defined largely by scale: the number of defendants charged and the dollar value of alleged fraud identified. It will persist regardless of which party holds Congress, carried forward by entrepreneurial prosecutors and agents. What a Democratic majority could add is a complementary lens, one that weighs not only how much fraud is charged but how the industry’s largest and fastest-growing participants are organized, owned and paid.

The best predictor of the next two years may be the last several. While Democrats controlled the Senate Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, its October 2024 majority staff report examined post-acute care denials by the largest Medicare Advantage insurers. That inquiry did not end with the change in Senate control. In July 2026, Sen. Richard Blumenthal (D-Connecticut) and Sen. Josh Hawley (R-Missouri) sought updated data from one of those insurers on its prior authorization practices and its use of algorithms in coverage decisions. In the House, Democrats have called for closer scrutiny of insurer practices at nursing homes serving Medicare Advantage members.

Hospitals have drawn bipartisan attention as well. A January 2025 Senate Budget Committee report on private equity ownership of hospitals, led by Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-Rhode Island) and Sen. Chuck Grassley (R-Iowa), raised concerns about staffing, hospital closures and distributions to investors. Months earlier, the Senate HELP Committee, then chaired by Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vermont), subpoenaed the chief executive of a bankrupt, private equity-backed hospital system, and the full Senate voted unanimously to hold him in contempt after he declined to testify.

Concern extends to the structure of the industry itself. In May 2026, Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Massachusetts) and Sen. Josh Hawley, joined by House members from both parties, reintroduced legislation that would require companies that own pharmacy benefit managers to divest their pharmacies, a measure that would force significant restructuring at some of the largest vertically integrated healthcare companies. Drug pricing has drawn similar scrutiny. In September 2024, the HELP Committee called the chief executive of a leading manufacturer of GLP-1 drugs to testify about U.S. prices for its diabetes and weight-loss treatments.

To the extent healthcare commands the new majority’s attention, the tools at its disposal are considerable. Committee chairs can compel documents and testimony by subpoena, require executives to testify under oath and publish staff reports that place internal records in the public domain.

Committees’ influence extends well beyond the hearing room. They can call on the Government Accountability Office and the HHS Office of Inspector General to examine particular programs, press CMS for data and refer matters to the Justice Department. Through legislation and appropriations, they can push for reforms in areas such as prior authorization, Medicare Advantage payment and transparency in private equity ownership. Oversight does not generate cases on its own, but it shapes where agencies look, which documents surface, and what insiders conclude is worth reporting. It shapes public opinion as well. Under the glare of hearings and headlines, a misstep that might once have been resolved quietly can escalate into a reputational and regulatory crisis.

Scrutiny of private equity tends to follow the sectors where investment has moved fastest. Applied behavior analysis (ABA) therapy for children with autism illustrates the dynamic. The underlying demand is genuine: the CDC estimates that one in 31 children has autism spectrum disorder. Private equity firms have acquired more than 500 autism therapy centers over the past decade, and CMS reported in August that Medicaid and CHIP spending on ABA rose 421% between 2021 and 2025, far outpacing the 67% growth in the number of children served.

Whatever the causes of that gap, regulators have begun to respond. CMS issued a toolkit to help states strengthen oversight of ABA services, New York halved Medicaid reimbursement for certain ABA services in October, citing program integrity concerns, and Indiana paused new ABA provider enrollment. Sectors that combine rapid growth, substantial public funding and concentrated investor ownership are natural subjects of congressional interest, whichever party is in the majority.

Should the emphasis shift, Medicare Advantage is the likeliest starting point, given the sustained attention already paid to prior authorization, post-acute care denials, algorithm-driven coverage decisions and risk adjustment. Beyond it, the record points to three broader fronts that track how the industry is organized, owned, and paid. The first is integration, as lawmakers ask whether combining insurers, pharmacy benefit managers, pharmacies and providers under one corporate roof serves patients. The second is ownership, as committees examine how private equity affects staffing, billing and quality at hospitals and in hyper-growth sectors in healthcare. The third is pricing, particularly for prescription drugs.

For companies in these areas, the practical consequence is that exposure can arrive before any agency acts, and congressional and Justice Department scrutiny tend to reinforce each other. A single congressional letter can place internal records and decision-making in public view, and the attention that follows tends to encourage potential whistleblowers to come forward. Those insiders have strong incentives to take their concerns to the Justice Department as well. Under the False Claims Act, a whistleblower who files a qui tam suit may receive between 15% and 30% of the government’s recovery, and DOJ’s Corporate Whistleblower Awards Pilot Program offers awards for original information about criminal healthcare fraud, including fraud against private insurers.

The overlap in subject matter is considerable. The joint DOJ-HHS False Claims Act Working Group has named Medicare Advantage and drug pricing among its enforcement priorities, the same areas that have drawn sustained congressional interest. Testimony and documents made public through oversight can become a road map for relators’ counsel and prosecutors alike, and many healthcare fraud cases still originate with an insider.

For companies and investors, the prudent response does not depend on who holds the gavel. It begins with a clear-eyed understanding of where the business’s risks reside, followed by rigorous testing of controls in the areas of fastest growth and highest reimbursement, and a willingness to address problems early, with the benefit of counsel where the facts are uncertain. Enforcement priorities shift with each election. The expectation that companies will identify and remedy their own problems does not.

Kevin Lowell is a member at Miller & Chevalier and previously served as deputy chief of the National Fraud Enforcement Division at the U.S. Department of Justice.

Published Nov 6, 2026 9:52 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more ›

You’ll hear lots of people blathering on about deep fried turkey this year, but smoking the bird is a secret hack to making things way more delicious. Right now, Amazon has Weber smokers (and other outdoor cookers) for their lowest prices of the year, which means you could grab one right now and perfect your technique before guests show up for turkey day. The discounts in Weber’s Amazon storefront cover its pellet smokers and Slate tabletop griddles along with the tongs and spatulas that hang off the side of a grill. As an added bonus, a smoker won’t burn your house down like a turkey fryer might.

A set-it-and-walk-away smoker for holiday turkey and brisket

Pellet smokers make it easy to cook low and slow. An electric auger pushes a carefully measured amount of wood pellets into a burn pot to keep your temp in the perfect zone. The Weber Smoque 22-inch pellet smoker is down to $599, which is the lowest price we’ve tracked since camelcamelcamel started following it in August 2025. Weber rates it for temperatures up to 500°F and says its SmokeBoost setting is tuned for deeper smoke flavor. The Rapid React PID controller, per Weber, heats up fast and recovers temperature after you open the lid, which matters when you’re spritzing ribs every hour. Two food probes come in the box and plug into built-in ports, so you can track two cuts at once. If you want to sear as well as smoke, the Weber Searwood 600 goes up to 600°F and is $899. Our guide to the best pellet grills has more options.

A propane flat-top small enough for the patio table

Rust is the chore that comes with most carbon-steel griddles, and Weber built its Slate line to cut down on it. The Weber Slate 17-inch tabletop griddle is $249 (was $299). Weber says it case-hardens the carbon-steel cooktop and pre-seasons it at the factory, which makes it harder for moisture to collect and rust to form. There’s no nonstick coating, so metal spatulas and scrapers are fair game. It runs a single propane burner that Weber rates to reach over 500°F, and at 42.7 pounds it’s light enough to haul to a tailgate. Weber backs it with a five-year warranty. Our guide to the best portable grills has more options for cooking away from home.

Pellets are the recurring cost of owning a smoker, so it’s worth stocking up when they dip. A 20-pound bag of Weber Grillmaster Blend hardwood pellets costs $14.99, the lowest price we’ve tracked since camelcamelcamel began following it in December 2020. The blend mixes maple, hickory and cherry, and Weber pitches it for everything from pork shoulder to pizza. Weber’s apple and cherry single-wood bags are also $14.99.

Long enough to flip sausages without singeing your knuckles

Tongs are the grill tool you reach for most, and short ones put your hand right over the coals. The Weber Precision Grill Tongs are $14.99, their lowest tracked price since October 2021. They’re 18 inches long with stainless steel arms and a flat front edge that Weber designed for precise grabs, which helps with thin stuff like asparagus spears. They lock closed for storage and go in the dishwasher, and Weber backs them with a two-year warranty. Keep your fingerprints intact this year.

Michael B. Jordan's Fantasy Series On Prime Video Reinvents The Genre, And Some Fans Won't Like It

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

While Michael B. Jordan has a massive Prime Video fantasy show on the way soon, fans of the genre should be aware that the show will divide audiences as it relies heavily on a majorly controversial trope. Ever since HBO spent an unprecedented amount of money on the budget of Game of Thrones season 1 back in 2011, every streaming service and cable network has been working overtime to find the next truly gigantic fantasy series.

From Prime Video’s Lord of the Rings prequel The Rings of Power to Netflix’s The Witcher, creators spread no expense replicating and even outdoing the ambition of HBO’s A Song of Ice & Fire adaptation. However, while Prime Video’s Critical Role shows The Legend of Vox Machina and The Mighty Nein share the mature content and fantasy adventure appeal of early Game of Thrones, no single show has supplanted the HBO hit as the biggest fantasy series ever.

Even HBO’s own Harry Potter reboot may struggle to replicate the popularity of Game of Thrones, since the series is a retread of a highly familiar intellectual property rather than an exciting new fantasy project. In contrast, Prime Video’s upcoming Michael B. Jordan vehicle Fourth Wing could potentially become the show to outdo Game of Thrones, but that hotly anticipated series has another issue of its own. The first major romantasy adaptation, Fourth Wing will decide whether this massive publishing trend can translate to small screen success.

A portmanteau of romance and fantasy, romantasy is a subgenre that mixes romance of varying spice levels with settings, tropes and characters usually found in the world of fantasy novels. Where Terry Pratchett’s books mix fantasy and comedy, romantasy novels mix fantasy and romance, and this blend has become big business in the book world. A Court of Thrones and Roses alone has managed to sell over 13 million copies since the novel series began.

However, as the long wait for George R.R. Martin’s A Song of Ice & Fire to reach the screen proves, fantasy trends in publishing aren't always immediately seized on by movies and TV. Fantasy is expensive and, while Game of Thrones and its spinoffs have proven that there is a mainstream market for mature, R-rated fantasy shows, their tone could not further from the romantic atmosphere of books like Fourth Wing.

Written by Rebecca Yarros, 2023’s Fourth Wing is the first in the Empyrean Pentalogy, followed by Iron Flame and Onyx Storm. Prime Video's Fourth Wing show will adapt the book’s immersive fantasy story, which follows the 20-year-old Violet Sorrengail as she is forced by her strict army general mother to become a dragon rider at the Basgiath War College, despite a challenging physical condition that makes her prone to injury.

While this set up might remind readers of How To Train Your Dragon, the reality of the books is much more adults-only in nature. The Empyrean Pentalogy is unapologetically adult in its content, with a lot of explicit sex scenes surprisingly early on in its enemies to lovers story. Describing the plot any further would run the risk of giving away who these scenes are between, but it is no spoiler to say the story’s romance elements owe as much tonally similar to Bridgerton than Game of Thrones.

Although Game of Thrones was extremely explicit, the fantasy show’s sex scenes were as apt to be disturbing as they were to be romantic. In contrast, the Fourth Wing books are mainstream romance novels in terms of their sexual content, and this means that the show’s big-budget live-action adaptation will have a markedly different tone from earlier major fantasy shows.

From the PG-rated Percy Jackson & The Olympians to Prime Video’s relatively family-friendly The Rings of Power, all the way to hard-R-rated, adult fantasy shows like House of the Dragon, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, and The Witcher, a heavy focus on romance and frequent sex scenes simply aren’t commonplace in the genre on the small screen. As such, some fantasy fans will no doubt be put off by Fourth Wing’s style.

Inflation Just Hit 3.7%. History Says to Own Companies That Can Do This 1 Thing.

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Businesses that possess this valuable characteristic can better navigate the current environment.

Investors won't get a fresh inflation update for another week. However, the trend has been strikingly clear.

The Consumer Price Index rose 3.7% during the month of September. This closely watched metric is still well ahead of the Federal Reserve's long-run 2% target, a level last seen in early 2021. And it marked an acceleration from August.

It's impossible to know when inflation will cool down. Investors are forced to deal with this reality. History says in this type of environment, it's a smart move to own companies that can do this one thing consistently.

If businesses are able to occasionally ask customers to pay more, it requires selling products and services that are truly differentiated. Only high-quality companies fall into this category. They should be on your watch list.

Pricing power is a feature that's developed over long periods of time. It doesn't appear out of thin air. Companies that have it possess some form of economic moat, such as a beloved brand that resonates strongly with customers relative to rival offerings. This usually shows up in the financials through high gross margins and returns on invested capital.

Just as consumers face inflation in their daily lives, companies can feel the same in their cost structures. If they have proven pricing power, it's much easier for them to handle inflationary pressures. They can pass higher costs on to their customers, thereby protecting their profitability.

This provides a unique advantage. Weaker businesses aren't so fortunate. If they raise prices, there's a greater likelihood that they'll lose customers who find their products and services easily substituted. Elite companies don't have this problem, as their earnings power can hold up in inflationary times. And this is where investors need to look.

Apple (AAPL -1.53%) arguably has the world's most valuable brand. It's the perfect example of a high-quality business with pricing power. Its premium hardware devices register robust demand. Within its products segment, the company posted a 39.3% gross margin in fiscal 2026 (ended Sept. 26).

Customer loyalty comes not only from how well-designed and easy to use the products and services are, but from the proprietary ecosystem they combine to form. Once someone owns an iPhone, an Apple Watch, and an iCloud subscription, for example, it's extremely difficult to switch to a rival.

Apple has historically been able to raise prices on new devices without pushing buyers away. When inflation picks up component and labor costs, the company has room to pass them along.

Unlike mass-market carmakers, Ferrari (RACE -2.32%) deliberately limits its supply to maintain the status and exclusivity of its brand name. During the three months that ended Sept. 30, it shipped just 3,368 vehicles. And there are long wait lists for the newest models.

That scarcity is what supports pricing power. Wealthy consumers view these as collectibles. And they aren't deterred by price increases, which might actually drive even more demand. For select special-edition models that cost seven-figure sums, Ferrari collects sizable deposits well before delivery.

The company's profitability speaks for itself. Ferrari just reported a Q3 operating margin of 29.7%, which is typical of a luxury brand. If needed, the company has no problem charging customers more.

In the world of financial services, American Express (AXP -2.29%) stands out. Its pricing power shows up in what it charges its cardholders. In the past 10 years, the average fee per card has risen by 177.6%.

In September 2025, American Express increased the annual fee of its flagship Platinum Card from $695 to $895, a notable bump. "One year after the U.S. Consumer Platinum refresh, spend retention remains at 99% for the portfolio," chief financial officer Christophe Le Caillec said on the Q3 2026 earnings call.

People pay more because they clearly value the perks, rewards, and benefits American Express offers. And there's a certain level of prestige with having these cards in your wallet. Additionally, the 190 million merchant locations are comfortable paying higher processing fees because they get access to affluent customers.

Apple, Ferrari, and American Express, all stocks that have crushed the market in the past decade, are outstanding businesses. Investors shouldn't rush to buy their shares, though, as it's critical to assess their valuations before putting money to work.

American Express is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Neil Patel has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends American Express, Apple, and Ferrari. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Opinion: Wow, they are here, the new Roberto Firmino and Mo Salah have arrived

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A new duo are on the scene, replicating Mo Salah and Roberto Firmino.

Things were different when you tuned into every Liverpool match and these two would be lining up for the Reds.

Firmino smiling pearly whites glaring up at the screen. His twinkle touches and mesmerising movements. Combine that with his silky skills and tireless running.

The Brazilian made things look simple. But he was the glue in the spine of Jurgen Klopp's team. Without Firmino, there is simply no Liverpool success.

Mo Salah is the same. He was different. Explosive and clinical. Unplayable in his prime. Salah was very much the star of the team. The Egyptian King, who we all adored and loved.

He broke records and became one of the most important faces of the club's history in the modern era.

Liverpool's attack miss Firmino and SalahThe most free-flowing attacking football the Reds played was when both of them were still at the club. Firmino departed in 2023, and then Salah has followed this summer. Liverpool have some exciting attackers. Expensive attackers, too. They have all proven themselves at the highest level. But the reality is that what we have seen (so far) is nowhere near at the same level as Salah and Firmino. Sure, Alexander Isak is scoring a lot of goals. Bradley Barcola has moments of flair, and Rio Ngumoha is an extremely tricky winger. Meanwhile, Victor Munoz is hard to contain when he's playing his best football. However, the chemistry between them just isn't there yet. Over time, it might be. Andoni Iraola is undoubtedly working on that in the training ground.

Unai Emery will not be going to Manchester United.

However, The Telegraph report that Emery is in advanced talks over signing a new long-term contract at Villa Park.

The country’s greatest-ever player announced his decision via a video to camera on his social media channels.

Kane is now tied to Bayern until 2029 on a record-breaking contract that is said to make him the highest-paid player in Europe by salary.

The England captain will be 36 by the time it expires, meaning he may well now never return to the Premier League and looks destined to stay second on the list of all-time top scorers behind Alan Shearer.

Manchester United looks set to make a sensational loan swoop for Ivan Toney in January, reports claim.

According to Football Insider, United want England star Toney, who now plays his football in Saudi Arabia with Al-Ahli.

They are more likely to make a loan move for the former Brentford frontman than a permanent offer, however.

Singapore Airlines Unveils Posh New First Class Suites With Double Beds For 777-9 And A350ULR

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Singapore Airlines has unveiled its next-generation first class seat, with sliding doors, companion dining, and center seats that combine into a double bed measuring five feet across. The new product will appear on the Airbus A350-900ULR and Boeing 777-9, bringing first class to the airline’s ultra-long-range A350 fleet for the first time.

Singapore Airlines’ new first class specifications reveal a small cabin with substantial personal space. The A350-900ULR will have four seats, while the Boeing 777-9 will have six. Both use a 1-2-1 arrangement with direct aisle access and a curved cabin layout.

PriestmanGoode designed the product, with Collins Aerospace manufacturing the seats. The interiors feature Poltrona Frau leather and padded walls incorporating Singapore Airlines’ batik motif.

The centerpiece is the pair of center seats. A fully retractable divider allows passengers to share the space, with the seats moving together to create either a two-person sofa or a double bed. That bed measures 80 inches long and 60 inches wide. Singapore Airlines describes it as the largest double bed in commercial aviation.

Solo passengers receive an 80-inch-long, 30-inch-wide bed. The seat itself measures 23 inches across with the armrests raised, expanding to 30 inches when they are lowered.

Every seat also supports face-to-face dining with a companion, a feature I appreciate in first class: traveling together should include the option to sit across from one another for dinner, even when you prefer separate window seats.

The seat includes electronically adjustable side wings, upper and lower lumbar support, adjustable armrests, a leg rest, and an ottoman. Integrated myKrisWorld controls can save preferred seat positions and lighting settings for future flights.

For sleeping, the crew will prepare the bed with cotton linens, a duvet, a choice of pillows, and a new mattress topper from German bedding company Paradies. Singapore Airlines says the topper uses breathable spacer fabric to improve cushioning and temperature regulation.

Dedicated storage includes a personal wardrobe, a leather-lined vanity drawer, and space for cabin baggage beneath the ottoman.

Both aircraft receive a 32-inch 4K entertainment screen, but the A350ULR version is OLED. The 777-9 specification lists a conventional 4K display.

Passengers will be offered Bang & Olufsen wireless headphones or can pair Bluetooth headphones with the system. A separate touch panel displays flight information, connectivity status, and lavatory availability without interrupting the main screen.

The new cabins are scheduled to begin entering service in the second half of 2027. Unlike with ANA, the unveiling does not signal an imminent onboard debut. First class is planned for the A350-900ULR and 777-9. The standard long-haul A350-900 will receive the new business class, but no first class cabin.

Singapore Airlines’ new first class looks lovely, particularly for couples who can use the center seats as a shared living and sleeping space. Companion dining at every seat and substantial bedding are welcome details too.
